Unit 1 — Asymptotic Analysis & Recurrences · 75 solved questions (2026-10-26 and 2026-11-02)
Big-O / Ω / Θ 22
ALG-U1-Q01MCQeasy

Consider f(n) = 3n² + 5n log n + 7. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. f(n) = Θ(n²)
  2. f(n) = Θ(n log n)
  3. f(n) = Θ(n³)
  4. f(n) = Θ(n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Growth of a sum is governed by its fastest-growing term (the dominant term).

Step by step.

  1. The three terms grow as n², n log n and a constant.
  2. n log n = o(n²), because (n log n)/n² = (log n)/n → 0.
  3. So f(n) is squeezed between 3n² and (3+ε)n² for large n.
  4. Hence f(n) = Θ(n²).

Answer: Θ(n²) — option A.

Common misconception. Adding a lower-order term such as 5n log n never changes the Θ-class. Students who sum the exponents or pick the largest coefficient by eye get B or C.

ALG-U1-Q02MCQeasy

If f(n) = Θ(g(n)) and g(n) = Θ(h(n)), which statement is necessarily TRUE?

  1. f(n) = Θ(h(n))
  2. f(n) = O(h(n)) but f(n) ≠ Θ(h(n))
  3. f(n) = o(h(n))
  4. Nothing can be concluded about f and h
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Θ is an equivalence relation: it is reflexive, symmetric and transitive.

Step by step.

  1. f = Θ(g) gives constants c₁, c₂, n₀ with c₁g(n) ≤ f(n) ≤ c₂g(n) for n ≥ n₀.
  2. g = Θ(h) gives d₁h(n) ≤ g(n) ≤ d₂h(n) for n ≥ n₁.
  3. Chain them for n ≥ max(n₀, n₁): c₁d₁h(n) ≤ f(n) ≤ c₂d₂h(n).
  4. That is exactly f(n) = Θ(h(n)).

Answer: f(n) = Θ(h(n)) — option A.

Common misconception. f = o(h) is strictly stronger than f = Θ(h) and does not follow. Θ gives a two-sided constant-factor sandwich, so it can never collapse to a little-o relation.

ALG-U1-Q03MCQmedium

Which is the correct increasing order of growth (slowest first) for large n?

  1. log log n < log n < √n < n/log n < n
  2. log n < log log n < √n < n < n/log n
  3. log log n < √n < log n < n/log n < n
  4. log log n < log n < n/log n < √n < n
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compare functions by taking ratios (or logs of both sides) as n → ∞.

Step by step.

  1. log log n = o(log n): log log n / log n → 0.
  2. log n = o(√n): with n = 2^m, m / 2^(m/2) → 0.
  3. √n = o(n/log n): (n/log n)/√n = √n / log n → ∞.
  4. n/log n = o(n) since 1/log n → 0.
  5. Chaining gives log log n < log n < √n < n/log n < n.

Answer: Option A.

Common misconception. n/log n is easy to misplace. It sits *above* √n but *below* n; treating the /log n factor as making it slower than √n is the usual error. Verify with a concrete value: at n = 2^20, n/log₂n ≈ 52428 while √n = 1024.

ALG-U1-Q04MCQmedium

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. n! = O(nⁿ)
  2. log(n!) = Θ(n log n)
  3. 2ⁿ⁺¹ = O(2ⁿ)
  4. 2²ⁿ = O(2ⁿ)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Exponential bases cannot be changed by a constant factor, and log(n!) is sandwiched by Stirling's bounds.

Step by step.

  1. n! = 1·2···n ≤ n·n···n = nⁿ, so A is true.
  2. Stirling gives n! ≥ (n/e)ⁿ, so log(n!) = Θ(n log n); B is true.
  3. 2ⁿ⁺¹ = 2·2ⁿ, a constant multiple, so C is true.
  4. 2²ⁿ = 4ⁿ and 4ⁿ/2ⁿ = 2ⁿ → ∞, so 2²ⁿ ≠ O(2ⁿ); D is false.

Answer: D is the FALSE statement.

Common misconception. A constant added to the *exponent* is a constant factor in the value (2ⁿ⁺¹ = 2·2ⁿ), but a constant multiplying the exponent is an exponential factor (2²ⁿ = 4ⁿ). Mixing these two up is the standard trap.

ALG-U1-Q15MCQhard

Let f(n) = n^(log₂ n) and g(n) = n^1000. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. f(n) = O(g(n))
  2. f(n) = Ω(g(n)) and f(n) ≠ O(g(n))
  3. f(n) = Θ(g(n))
  4. f(n) = o(g(n))
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both are n raised to a power; compare the exponents log₂n and 1000.

Step by step.

  1. Write both as n^(exponent): f has exponent log₂n, g has exponent 1000.
  2. log₂n > 1000 once n > 2^1000.
  3. Beyond that point f(n) > g(n), so f is not O(g).
  4. f(n)/g(n) = n^(log₂n − 1000) → ∞, so f(n) = Ω(g(n)) and f(n) ≠ O(g(n)).

Answer: Option B.

Common misconception. A fixed polynomial n^1000 looks enormous but any polylog exponent eventually overtakes a constant exponent. The other classic trap is assuming n^(log n) is still polynomial — it is quasi-polynomial, growing faster than every n^k.

ALG-U1-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. If f(n) = O(g(n)) then g(n) = Ω(f(n)).
  2. If f(n) = Θ(g(n)) then 2^(f(n)) = Θ(2^(g(n))).
  3. n^(1/2) = O(n^(2/3)).
  4. log(n!) = O(n log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Definitions of O, Ω, Θ, and Stirling's bound for log(n!).

Step by step.

  1. A: f = O(g) means f(n) ≤ c·g(n) eventually; dividing by c gives g(n) ≥ (1/c)f(n), i.e. g = Ω(f). TRUE.
  2. B: take f(n) = n, g(n) = 2n. Then 2^(f(n)) = 2ⁿ and 2^(g(n)) = 4ⁿ, and 4ⁿ/2ⁿ = 2ⁿ → ∞, so they are not Θ of each other. FALSE.
  3. C: 1/2 < 2/3, so n^(1/2)/n^(2/3) = n^(−1/6) → 0. TRUE.
  4. D: log(n!) ≤ log(nⁿ) = n log n. TRUE.

Answer: A, C and D are TRUE; B is FALSE.

Common misconception. B is the classic failure: Θ is preserved by addition and multiplication but **not** by exponentiation. Equal growth of f and g allows a multiplicative gap, which exponentiation turns into an unbounded ratio.

ALG-U1-Q20MSQeasy

Which of the following functions are O(n log n)?

  1. 5n log n + 100n
  2. n²/log n
  3. log(n!)
  4. 2^(log₂ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Check whether each function is bounded above by a constant multiple of n log n.

Step by step.

  1. 5n log n + 100n ≤ 105 n log n for n ≥ 2. YES.
  2. (n²/log n)/(n log n) = n/(log n)² → ∞, so it is NOT O(n log n). NO.
  3. log(n!) = Θ(n log n) by Stirling, hence O(n log n). YES.
  4. 2^(log₂n) = n ≤ n log n for n ≥ 2. YES.

Answer: A, C and D.

Common misconception. n²/log n is larger than n log n even though the log n sits in the denominator — dividing by a log does not bring a quadratic down to n log n.

ALG-U1-Q26MCQeasy

Which of the following is the formal definition of f(n) = O(g(n)) for asymptotically positive functions?

  1. There exist constants c > 0 and n₀ ≥ 0 such that f(n) ≤ c·g(n) for all n ≥ n₀.
  2. For every constant c > 0 there exists n₀ such that f(n) ≤ c·g(n) for all n ≥ n₀.
  3. There exist constants c > 0 and n₀ ≥ 0 such that f(n) ≥ c·g(n) for all n ≥ n₀.
  4. limn→∞ f(n)/g(n) = 0.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Big-O is an upper bound that is allowed to be off by a fixed constant factor, and it only has to hold from some point onwards. One pair (c, n₀) that works is enough — the constant does not have to be beaten by every possible choice.

Step by step.

  1. Option A is exactly that statement: ∃c > 0, ∃n₀ such that f(n) ≤ c·g(n) beyond n₀.
  2. Option B quantifies c universally, so it demands f(n) ≤ c·g(n) even for c = 0.001. That is little-o, f(n) = o(g(n)), a strictly stronger claim.
  3. Option C has the inequality reversed, which defines f(n) = Ω(g(n)).
  4. Option D is the ratio test for little-o. It implies big-O but is not equivalent to it: f(n) = 2n is O(n) while f(n)/g(n) = 2 does not tend to 0.

Answer: Option (A) — the existential definition of an asymptotic upper bound.

Common misconception. Reading the ∃c of big-O as ∀c, which silently upgrades the claim to little-o, or reversing the inequality and landing on Ω. The constant is a witness you are free to choose, not a quantity you must satisfy for all values.

ALG-U1-Q27MCQeasy

Let f(n) = n^(3/2) and g(n) = n (log₂ n)². Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. f(n) = Θ(g(n))
  2. f(n) = O(g(n)) but f(n) ≠ Θ(g(n))
  3. g(n) = o(f(n))
  4. f(n) = o(g(n))
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two functions are compared by the limit of their ratio. A ratio that blows up means the numerator grows faster; a ratio that tends to 0 means the denominator does.

Step by step.

  1. Form the ratio f(n)/g(n) = n^(3/2) / (n (log₂ n)²) = √n / (log₂ n)².
  2. Every polynomial in n beats every power of a logarithm: for n = 2^(m) the ratio is 2^(m/2)/m² → ∞.
  3. So f(n)/g(n) → ∞, which is precisely g(n) = o(f(n)).
  4. Consequently f(n) = ω(g(n)) and the two functions are not within a constant factor of each other.

Answer: Option (C) — g(n) = o(f(n)).

Common misconception. Treating (log₂ n)² as if it were a polynomial factor comparable with √n. A logarithm squared is still far smaller than any positive power of n, so option (A) and option (D) both fail; option (B) reverses the direction.

ALG-U1-Q28MCQmedium

For every integer n ≥ 2 define f(n) = n^(1 + 1/log₂ n). Then

  1. f(n) = Θ(n)
  2. f(n) = Θ(n log n)
  3. f(n) = Θ(n²)
  4. f(n) = Θ(2^(n))
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A power of n whose exponent depends on n is best read in base 2: write n = 2^(log₂ n) and push the exponent through.

Step by step.

  1. Split the exponent: n^(1 + 1/log₂ n) = n · n^(1/log₂ n).
  2. Now n^(1/log₂ n) = 2^((log₂ n)·(1/log₂ n)) = 2^(1) = 2, a constant.
  3. Hence f(n) = 2n for every n ≥ 2.
  4. A constant multiple of n is Θ(n).

Answer: Option (A) — f(n) = 2n = Θ(n).

Common misconception. Assuming that an exponent containing 1/log₂ n must produce sub-linear or super-linear growth and guessing Θ(n log n) or Θ(n²). The exponent is not a fixed power: it shrinks exactly fast enough to cancel the log₂ n in the base change, leaving the constant 2.

ALG-U1-Q29MCQmedium

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. n^(0.1) = O(log₂ n)
  2. log₂(n³) = Θ(log₂ n)
  3. 3^(n) = ω(n^(100))
  4. √n = O(n/log₂ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each claim is settled by the limit of the ratio of the two sides. Anything with a positive power of n outgrows any power of log n, and any exponential outgrows any polynomial.

Step by step.

  1. (A) n^(0.1)/log₂ n → ∞ because a polynomial dominates a logarithm. So n^(0.1) is ω(log n), never O(log n). FALSE.
  2. (B) log₂(n³) = 3 log₂ n, a constant multiple of log₂ n. TRUE.
  3. (C) 3^(n)/n^(100) → ∞, so n^(100) = o(3^(n)). TRUE.
  4. (D) √n / (n/log₂ n) = log₂ n / √n → 0, so √n = O(n/log₂ n). TRUE.

Answer: Option (A) is the false statement.

Common misconception. Judging by a few small values: at n = 10^(6) the ratio n^(0.1)/log₂ n is still below 1, which tempts a "yes" for (A). Asymptotics is about the limit, not about the first million values — the ratio eventually climbs without bound.

ALG-U1-Q30MCQhard

Which of the following functions grows fastest as n → ∞?

  1. 2^(n)
  2. n^(log₂ n)
  3. (log₂ n)^(n)
  4. n!
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. When the bases differ and the exponents contain n, compare the base-2 logarithms of the four functions. A larger logarithm means a faster-growing function.

Step by step.

  1. (A) log₂ 2^(n) = n.
  2. (B) log₂ n^(log₂ n) = (log₂ n)².
  3. (C) log₂ (log₂ n)^(n) = n · log₂ log₂ n.
  4. (D) Stirling: log₂(n!) = n log₂ n − O(n), i.e. Θ(n log₂ n).
  5. Order the logarithms: (log₂ n)² is the smallest, then n, then n log₂ log₂ n, and n log₂ n is the largest.
  6. So n! is the fastest-growing of the four.

Answer: Option (D) — n!.

Common misconception. Picking 2^(n) because "exponential beats everything", or n^(log₂ n) because it has a power of n in the exponent. Both are beaten by n!, whose logarithm n log₂ n exceeds n log₂ log₂ n and (log₂ n)².

ALG-U1-Q41MSQeasy

Which of the following functions are Ω(n²)?

  1. n²/1000
  2. n (log₂ n)³
  3. 2^(n)
  4. n^(5/2)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A function is Ω(n²) when it is bounded below by a constant multiple of n² from some point on. A small constant multiplier does not matter; what matters is the growth rate.

Step by step.

  1. (A) n²/1000 = (1/1000)·n² is a constant multiple of n²: Ω(n²). TRUE.
  2. (B) n (log₂ n)³ / n² = (log₂ n)³/n → 0, so it grows strictly slower: NOT Ω(n²).
  3. (C) 2^(n)/n² → ∞: TRUE.
  4. (D) n^(2.5)/n² = √n → ∞: TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Rejecting (A) because of the tiny constant 1/1000. Asymptotic bounds ignore constant factors; only the growth rate counts.

ALG-U1-Q42MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE for asymptotically positive functions?

  1. f(n) = Θ(g(n)) if and only if f(n) = O(g(n)) and g(n) = O(f(n)).
  2. If f(n) = o(g(n)) then f(n) = O(g(n)) and f(n) ≠ Ω(g(n)).
  3. For every pair of functions f and g, either f(n) = O(g(n)) or g(n) = O(f(n)).
  4. If f(n) = O(g(n)) and g(n) = O(h(n)) then f(n) = O(h(n)).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The asymptotic relations are preorders on functions. Θ is the symmetric part of O, O is transitive, and O is not a total order because two functions can be incomparable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Θ means bounded above and below by constant multiples, which is exactly f = O(g) together with g = O(f). TRUE.
  2. (B) f = o(g) means f/g → 0, which gives f = O(g) at once and rules out f = Ω(g) (that would need f/g bounded below). TRUE.
  3. (C) Fails: take f(n) = n and g(n) = n^(1 + sin n). The ratio g/f = n^(sin n) oscillates between 1/n and n, so neither f = O(g) nor g = O(f) holds. FALSE.
  4. (D) Chaining the two constants gives f(n) ≤ c₁g(n) ≤ c₁c₂h(n) eventually. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming O behaves like ≤ on numbers, which would make it a total order. It is only a preorder: transitivity and reflexivity hold, but two functions with wildly oscillating ratio are incomparable.

ALG-U1-Q45MSQhard

Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. n^(log₂ 3) = O(n^(1.6))
  2. 3^(n) = O(2^(n))
  3. 2^(3n) = Θ(8^(n))
  4. If f(n) = ω(g(n)) then f(n)/g(n) → ∞.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compare by ratio, and remember that two exponentials with different bases are never within a constant factor of each other, while two exponentials that differ only by rewriting the same expression are identical functions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) log₂ 3 ≈ 1.585 < 1.6, so n^(log₂ 3)/n^(1.6) = n^(−0.015) → 0. TRUE.
  2. (B) 3^(n)/2^(n) = (3/2)^(n) → ∞, so 2^(n) = o(3^(n)) and the claimed direction is reversed. FALSE.
  3. (C) 2^(3n) = (2³)^(n) = 8^(n), the very same function. TRUE.
  4. (D) That is the definition of little-omega: for every c > 0, f(n) > c g(n) eventually, which forces the ratio to diverge. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading O(2^(n)) as "exponential, so anything exponential is fine". A constant factor in the exponent is not a constant factor in the function: 3^(n) and 2^(n) differ by (3/2)^(n), which is unbounded.

ALG-U1-Q51MCQeasy

For the two functions f(n) = 2ⁿ⁺¹ and g(n) = 2ⁿ, which of the following is TRUE?

  1. f(n) = Θ(g(n))
  2. f(n) = O(g(n)) but f(n) ≠ Θ(g(n))
  3. f(n) = ω(g(n))
  4. f(n) = o(g(n))
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A constant factor is invisible to Θ. Here 2ⁿ⁺¹ = 2 · 2ⁿ exactly, so the two functions differ by the constant 2 at every n.

Step by step.

  1. Write the ratio: f(n)/g(n) = 2ⁿ⁺¹ / 2ⁿ = 2 for every n.
  2. A constant ratio c means f = Θ(g) with both constants equal to 2.
  3. Since the ratio is neither 0 nor ∞, the functions are of the same order.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading the +1 in the exponent as an extra factor of n. An additive change in the exponent is a constant multiplier, which Θ ignores; only a multiplicative change in n would separate the classes.

ALG-U1-Q52MCQmedium

Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. log₂(n!) = Θ(n log₂ n)
  2. log₂(n!) = Θ(n)
  3. log₂(n!) = Θ(n²)
  4. log₂(n!) = O(log₂ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. log(n!) is a sum of n logarithmic terms, and a sum is trapped between its smallest and largest term times the number of terms.

Step by step.

  1. Write log₂(n!) = Σk=1..n log₂ k.
  2. Upper bound: every term is at most log₂ n, so the sum is at most n log₂ n.
  3. Lower bound: the last ⌈n/2⌉ terms are each at least log₂(n/2) = log₂ n − 1, so the sum is at least (n/2)(log₂ n − 1).
  4. Both bounds are Θ(n log n), so log₂(n!) = Θ(n log₂ n). This is the weak form of Stirling's approximation.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering Θ(n) because "log of a factorial feels linear". The factorial has n factors and each contributes a logarithm, so the product of that many log-sized terms is n log n.

ALG-U1-Q53MCQmedium

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. log₂ n = O(n⁰·¹)
  2. n⁰·¹ = O(log₂ n)
  3. n log₂ n = O(n¹·¹)
  4. 2^(log₂ n) = Θ(n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every positive power of n eventually outgrows every power of log n, however small the exponent on n and however large the power on the log.

Step by step.

  1. Option B asks whether n⁰·¹ / log₂ n is bounded. It tends to ∞, so the statement is false.
  2. Option A is the same ratio inverted, and it tends to 0, so it is true.
  3. Option C: n log n / n¹·¹ = log n / n⁰·¹ → 0, so it is true.
  4. Option D: 2^(log₂ n) = n identically, so it is true.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Options A and B look symmetric and both get marked true. The asymmetry is real: the logarithm is the slower-growing side, so log n is dominated by any positive power of n.

ALG-U1-Q54MCQhard

Let f(n) = n^(log₂ n) / 2ⁿ. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. f(n) = ω(1)
  2. f(n) = Θ(1)
  3. f(n) = o(1)
  4. f(n) = Ω(n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Convert everything to one base. n^(log₂ n) = 2^((log₂ n)²), so the whole function is a single power of 2 with a comparison of exponents inside it.

Step by step.

  1. Rewrite the numerator: n^(log₂ n) = (2^(log₂ n))^(log₂ n) = 2^((log₂ n)²).
  2. So f(n) = 2^((log₂ n)² − n).
  3. The exponent (log₂ n)² − n tends to −∞, because a squared logarithm grows slower than n.
  4. Hence f(n) → 0, which is the definition of f(n) = o(1).

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. The numerator grows faster than any fixed power of n, which tempts an answer of ω(1). But 2ⁿ is a full exponential, and an exponential beats any n^(log n).

ALG-U1-Q55MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 3ⁿ = O(2ⁿ)
  2. √n = O(n^(2/3))
  3. n² / log₂ n = ω(n)
  4. log₂(n²) = Θ(log₂ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Compare two functions by the limit of their ratio: a limit of 0 means little-o, a finite non-zero limit means Θ, and a limit of ∞ means ω.

Step by step.

  1. A: 3ⁿ / 2ⁿ = (3/2)ⁿ → ∞, so 3ⁿ is not O(2ⁿ). This statement is false.
  2. B: n^(1/2) / n^(2/3) = n^(−1/6) → 0, so √n is strictly smaller, and the statement holds.
  3. C: (n²/log n) / n = n / log n → ∞, so the statement holds.
  4. D: log₂(n²) = 2 log₂ n, a constant multiple, so the statement holds.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Option A is the usual slip: for exponentials the larger base always wins, so 3ⁿ overtakes 2ⁿ and no constant rescues it. Option C is misjudged by assuming that dividing by log n turns n² into n.

ALG-U1-Q56NATeasy

For f(n) = 100n + 5000 and g(n) = n², the smallest integer n₀ such that f(n) ≤ g(n) for every n ≥ n₀ is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 137

Detailed solution

Concept. The witness n₀ of a big-O statement comes from solving the defining inequality, not from estimating it.

Step by step.

  1. Solve n² ≥ 100n + 5000, that is n² − 100n − 5000 ≥ 0.
  2. The roots are (100 ± √(10000 + 20000)) / 2 = (100 ± 173·2) / 2, giving about 136·6 and −36·6.
  3. So the inequality holds for n ≥ 136·6, and the smallest integer candidate is 137.
  4. Check the boundary: at n = 136, f = 18600 and g = 18496, so it fails; at n = 137, f = 18700 and g = 18769, so it holds.

Answer: 137

Common misconception. Rounding 136·6 down to 136 because "n₀ can be any large number". The smallest valid integer must be checked at the boundary, and 136 fails.

ALG-U1-Q57MSQeasy

For asymptotically positive functions f and g, f(n) = o(g(n)) holds exactly when which of the following are true? (Select all that apply.)

  1. For every constant c > 0 there is n₀ with f(n) < c·g(n) for all n ≥ n₀.
  2. There is a constant c > 0 with f(n) < c·g(n) for all n ≥ n₀.
  3. f(n) / g(n) → ∞ as n → ∞.
  4. f(n) = O(g(n)) and f(n) ≠ Θ(g(n)).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Little-o is big-O with the quantifier strengthened from "some constant" to "every constant". The two definitions differ only in that word.

Step by step.

  1. The definition of o(g) quantifies over all constants c > 0, which is option A.
  2. Option B is exactly the definition of O(g), so it characterises big-O, not little-o.
  3. Option C describes ω(g), the opposite relation.
  4. For positive functions o(g) = O(g) − Θ(g): a function is little-o exactly when it is big-O but not of the same order. That is option D.

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Common misconception. Choosing B, because "a constant works" is what most students remember. The single word "every" is what separates o from O.

Master theorem cases 20
ALG-U1-Q05MCQeasy

The recurrence T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n, with T(1) = 1, has the solution

  1. Θ(n log n)
  2. Θ(n²)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem with a = 2, b = 2: compare f(n) against n^(log_b a).

Step by step.

  1. a = 2, b = 2, so n^(log₂2) = n¹.
  2. f(n) = n = Θ(n¹): this is Case 2 (equal growth).
  3. Case 2 gives T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a) · log n) = Θ(n log n).
  4. Check by recursion tree: log₂n levels each costing n.

Answer: Θ(n log n) — option A.

Common misconception. Do not report Θ(n) just because the per-level cost is n; there are log n such levels.

ALG-U1-Q06MCQeasy

Using the Master Theorem, the solution of T(n) = 9T(n/3) + n is

  1. Θ(n²)
  2. Θ(n² log n)
  3. Θ(n log n)
  4. Θ(n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem Case 1: f(n) is polynomially smaller than n^(log_b a).

Step by step.

  1. a = 9, b = 3, so n^(log₃9) = n².
  2. f(n) = n = O(n^(2−ε)) for any 0 < ε ≤ 1.
  3. Case 1 gives T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a)) = Θ(n²).
  4. The +n work is negligible against the n² splitting cost.

Answer: Θ(n²) — option A.

Common misconception. Case 1 needs a *polynomial* gap, not just f(n) = O(n^(log_b a)). Here n is smaller by a full factor of n, so the gap is genuine.

ALG-U1-Q07MCQmedium

Using the extended form of the Master Theorem, the solution of T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n log n is

  1. Θ(n log² n)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n²)
  4. Θ(n log n log log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Extended Case 2: if f(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a) · log^k n) with k ≥ 0, then T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a) · log^(k+1) n).

Step by step.

  1. a = 2, b = 2, so n^(log₂2) = n.
  2. f(n) = n log n = Θ(n¹ · log¹ n), so k = 1.
  3. Extended Case 2 adds one extra logarithm: T(n) = Θ(n · log^(1+1) n).
  4. Hence T(n) = Θ(n log² n).

Answer: Θ(n log² n) — option A.

Common misconception. Plain Case 2 would give Θ(n log n), which is *below* the actual cost — the n log n work per level already forces one extra log factor. Always check whether f(n) carries a log^k n.

ALG-U1-Q08MCQmedium

The solution of T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n² is

  1. Θ(n² log n)
  2. Θ(n²)
  3. Θ(n³)
  4. Θ(n log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem Case 2 when f(n) matches n^(log_b a) exactly.

Step by step.

  1. a = 4, b = 2, so n^(log₂4) = n².
  2. f(n) = n² = Θ(n^(log_b a)): Case 2 applies.
  3. Case 2 gives T(n) = Θ(n² log n).
  4. Recursion tree: each of the log₂n levels costs n².

Answer: Θ(n² log n) — option A.

Common misconception. Reporting Θ(n²) omits the log n factor contributed by the number of levels; the per-level cost being exactly n² is the signal for Case 2, not Case 3.

ALG-U1-Q09MCQhard

The solution of T(n) = 3T(n/4) + n log n is

  1. Θ(n log n)
  2. Θ(n^0.792)
  3. Θ(n^1.585)
  4. Θ(n² log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem Case 3: f(n) polynomially dominates n^(log_b a) and satisfies the regularity condition.

Step by step.

  1. a = 3, b = 4, so n^(log₄3) ≈ n^0.7925.
  2. f(n) = n log n = Ω(n^(0.7925+ε)) for ε < 0.2, so Case 3 is in play.
  3. Regularity: a·f(n/b) = 3·(n/4)log(n/4) = (3/4)·n log(n/4) ≤ (3/4)·n log n = (3/4)f(n), and 3/4 < 1 holds.
  4. Case 3 gives T(n) = Θ(f(n)) = Θ(n log n).

Answer: Θ(n log n) — option A.

Common misconception. Option B tempts you to answer with n^(log_b a) (Case 1's answer) even though Case 3 applies here. Case 1 returns the *homogeneous* term; Case 3 returns f(n).

ALG-U1-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following recurrences have the solution Θ(n log n)?

  1. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n
  2. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²
  3. T(n) = 4T(n/4) + n
  4. T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Apply the Master Theorem case analysis to each recurrence separately.

Step by step.

  1. A: n^(log₂2) = n = f(n) → Case 2 → Θ(n log n). YES.
  2. B: n^(log₂2) = n, f(n) = n² polynomially larger, regularity 2·(n/2)² = n²/2 ≤ c·n² with c = 1/2 → Case 3 → Θ(n²). NO.
  3. C: n^(log₄4) = n = f(n) → Case 2 → Θ(n log n). YES.
  4. D: n^(log₂8) = n³, f(n) = n² polynomially smaller → Case 1 → Θ(n³). NO.

Answer: A and C.

Common misconception. b does not have to equal 2, and a does not have to equal b; only the comparison between f(n) and n^(log_b a) matters. Option C is missed by people who assume the a = b pattern.

ALG-U1-Q21NATeasy

For T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n², the solution is T(n) = Θ(n^k). Find k.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem Case 1: the homogeneous term n^(log_b a) wins when f(n) is polynomially smaller.

Step by step.

  1. a = 8, b = 2, so n^(log₂8) = n³.
  2. f(n) = n² = O(n^(3−ε)) with ε = 1, so Case 1 applies.
  3. Case 1 gives T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a)) = Θ(n³).
  4. Matching Θ(n^k) gives k = 3.

Answer: k = 3 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Adding the exponents of a and b (8 + 2) or answering 2 from f(n) = n² are the usual slips. The exponent is log_b a = log₂8 = 3.

ALG-U1-Q23NATmedium

For T(n) = 2T(n/4) + √n, the solution is T(n) = Θ(√n · log^k n). Find k.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Master Theorem Case 2: f(n) matches n^(log_b a) exactly, adding one logarithmic factor.

Step by step.

  1. a = 2, b = 4, so n^(log₄2) = n^(1/2) = √n.
  2. f(n) = √n = Θ(n^(1/2)), exactly matching the homogeneous term.
  3. Case 2 gives T(n) = Θ(√n · log n).
  4. Matching Θ(√n · log^k n) gives k = 1.

Answer: k = 1 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Because b = 4 and a = 2, log₄2 = 1/2, not 2. Getting the exponent wrong makes the comparison look like Case 1 or Case 3 and the extra log n disappears.

ALG-U1-Q31MCQeasy

Using the Master Theorem, the solution of T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n is

  1. Θ(n²)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(n² log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The Master Theorem compares the driving function f(n) with the watershed function n^(log_b a). Whichever is polynomially larger wins; if they match, a logarithm appears.

Step by step.

  1. Read a = 4, b = 2, f(n) = n.
  2. Watershed: n^(log₂ 4) = n².
  3. Compare: f(n) = n = O(n^(2 − ε)) for ε = 1. This is Case 1.
  4. Case 1 gives T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a)) = Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (A) — Θ(n²).

Common misconception. Choosing Θ(n log n) because the recurrence "looks like merge sort". Merge sort has a = 2; here a = 4 quadruples the number of subproblems, so the leaves dominate and the merge cost n is negligible.

ALG-U1-Q32MCQmedium

The solution of T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n² log₂ n is

  1. Θ(n³)
  2. Θ(n² log n)
  3. Θ(n² log² n)
  4. Θ(n³ log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A logarithm multiplying f(n) is easily mistaken for the Case-2 signal. Decide first whether f(n) is polynomially smaller than n^(log_b a); only when it is polynomially equal does the extended case add a logarithm.

Step by step.

  1. Read a = 8, b = 2, so n^(log₂ 8) = n³.
  2. Here f(n) = n² log₂ n. Since n² log n = O(n^(3 − ε)) for any 0 < ε ≤ 1, f is polynomially smaller than n³.
  3. That is Case 1, whose solution is Θ(n^(log_b a)) = Θ(n³).
  4. The extra log₂ n factor does not survive: the leaves number 8^(log₂ n) = n³ and each contributes Θ(1).

Answer: Option (A) — Θ(n³).

Common misconception. Applying the extended Case 2 because a logarithm is present. Case 2 requires f(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a) · log^(k) n); here the watershed is n³ and f(n) = n² log n is smaller by a full factor of n/log n, not by a logarithm.

ALG-U1-Q33MCQmedium

In its standard form, the Master Theorem cannot be applied directly to which of the following recurrences?

  1. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n
  2. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²
  3. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n/log₂ n
  4. T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The Master Theorem needs f(n) to be polynomially smaller than, polynomially equal to, or polynomially larger than n^(log_b a). A gap that is only logarithmic fits none of the three cases.

Step by step.

  1. (A) a = b = 2 gives n^(1) and f(n) = n is equal: Case 2, Θ(n log n).
  2. (B) n^(1) against f(n) = n², polynomially larger and regular: Case 3, Θ(n²).
  3. (D) n^(log₂ 4) = n² against f(n) = n², equal: Case 2, Θ(n² log n).
  4. (C) f(n) = n/log₂ n is smaller than n but only by a factor log₂ n, which is not n^(ε) for any ε > 0. No case applies; the solution is in fact Θ(n log log n), which needs the recursion tree or the substitution method.

Answer: Option (C) — T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n/log₂ n.

Common misconception. Believing that any f(n) smaller than n falls into Case 1. Case 1 demands a polynomial gap; dividing by a logarithm never produces one, so the theorem simply does not cover this recurrence.

ALG-U1-Q34MCQhard

The solution of T(n) = 7T(n/2) + n² is

  1. Θ(n^(log₂ 7))
  2. Θ(n²)
  3. Θ(n² log n)
  4. Θ(n³)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. With a non-integral watershed exponent the answer is still read off the Master Theorem, but the exponent log₂ 7 must be left as it is — it is about 2.807, so n² is polynomially smaller than the watershed.

Step by step.

  1. Read a = 7, b = 2, so the watershed is n^(log₂ 7) with log₂ 7 ≈ 2.807.
  2. Compare f(n) = n² with n^(2.807): n²/n^(2.807) = n^(−0.807) → 0, so f(n) = O(n^(log₂ 7 − ε)) with ε = 0.8.
  3. This is Case 1, and the solution is Θ(n^(log₂ 7)).
  4. Numerically n^(2.807) sits between n² and n³, which is why neither Θ(n²) nor Θ(n³) is right.

Answer: Option (A) — Θ(n^(log₂ 7)).

Common misconception. Rounding log₂ 7 up to 3 and answering Θ(n³), or matching the driving term and answering Θ(n²). A non-integral exponent is a perfectly good answer; the recurrence is not obliged to have a polynomial solution.

ALG-U1-Q43MSQmedium

Which of the following recurrences have the solution Θ(n²)?

  1. T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n²
  2. T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n
  3. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²
  4. T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n³
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. For each recurrence compute the watershed n^(log_b a) and compare it with f(n). A tie gives an extra logarithm; a strictly smaller driving term leaves the watershed exponent as the answer.

Step by step.

  1. (A) a = 4, b = 2 gives watershed n² and f(n) = n²: a tie, so T(n) = Θ(n² log n). NOT Θ(n²).
  2. (B) watershed n², f(n) = n = O(n^(2 − 1)): Case 1, Θ(n²). TRUE.
  3. (C) a = 2, b = 2 gives watershed n, f(n) = n² is polynomially larger and satisfies the regularity condition 2(n/2)² = n²/2 ≤ c n²: Case 3, Θ(n²). TRUE.
  4. (D) a = 8, b = 2 gives watershed n³ and f(n) = n³: a tie, so Θ(n³ log n). NOT Θ(n²).

Answer: Options (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that a driving term equal to n² always yields Θ(n²). When f(n) ties with the watershed the answer carries an extra log n, which is exactly what makes (A) wrong.

ALG-U1-Q46NATeasy

For T(n) = 9T(n/3) + n², the solution is T(n) = Θ(n^(k) log₂ n). Find k.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. When the driving function ties polynomially with the watershed, the Master Theorem returns the watershed multiplied by one logarithm. The exponent k is therefore the watershed exponent itself.

Step by step.

  1. Read a = 9, b = 3, f(n) = n².
  2. Watershed: n^(log₃ 9) = n².
  3. f(n) = n² = Θ(n²), so this is Case 2 and T(n) = Θ(n² log n).
  4. Matching against Θ(n^(k) log₂ n) gives k = 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Reporting k = 1 by counting the logarithm as part of the exponent, or k = 9 from the coefficient a. The exponent of the power is the watershed exponent log₃ 9 = 2; the logarithm sits outside the power.

ALG-U1-Q58MCQeasy

Using the Master Theorem, the solution of T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n is:

  1. Θ(n²)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n² log n)
  4. Θ(n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Case 1 of the Master Theorem: when the driving function is polynomially smaller than the watershed n^(log_b a), the recursion (the leaves) dominates.

Step by step.

  1. Here a = 4 and b = 2, so the watershed is n^(log₂ 4) = n².
  2. The driving function is f(n) = n, and n = O(n^(2−ε)) with ε = 1, a polynomial gap.
  3. Case 1 therefore applies and T(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a)) = Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reading f(n) = n and answering Θ(n). In case 1 the root contributes only n; the n² leaves are what the Θ-class records.

ALG-U1-Q59MCQmedium

The solution of T(n) = 16T(n/4) + n² is:

  1. Θ(n²)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n³)
  4. Θ(n² log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Case 2: when the driving function matches the watershed exactly, every level costs the same and an extra logarithmic factor appears.

Step by step.

  1. a = 16 and b = 4, so the watershed is n^(log₄ 16) = n².
  2. The driving function is f(n) = n², exactly the watershed, so case 2 applies.
  3. Case 2 gives T(n) = Θ(n² log n).
  4. A sanity check from the recursion tree: level i holds 16ⁱ nodes of size n/4ⁱ, and 16ⁱ·(n/4ⁱ)² = n² at every level, over log₄ n + 1 levels.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Dropping the extra log factor and answering Θ(n²). Balance between the two sides is precisely the case where the level cost never decays, so the log cannot be dropped.

ALG-U1-Q60MCQmedium

Consider T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n / log₂ n. Which of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Case 1 applies, so T(n) = Θ(n).
  2. Case 2 applies, so T(n) = Θ(n log n).
  3. None of the three cases applies, although T(n) = Θ(n log log n).
  4. Case 3 applies, so T(n) = Θ(n log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three cases need a polynomial gap: f must be smaller or larger than the watershed by a factor n^ε. A factor of only log n leaves a gap the theorem cannot classify.

Step by step.

  1. The watershed is n^(log₂ 2) = n.
  2. Here f(n)/n = 1/log₂ n → 0, so f is smaller than the watershed.
  3. But f is not polynomially smaller: for every ε > 0, n^ε / log₂ n → ∞, so no ε satisfies the case-1 requirement.
  4. So none of the three cases applies. The recurrence is still solvable by other means, and the known solution is Θ(n log log n).

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. "f grows slower than the watershed, therefore case 1" is the trap. Case 1 demands a polynomial gap; a logarithmic gap falls between the cases.

ALG-U1-Q61MCQhard

The solution of T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n√n is:

  1. Θ(n)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n^(3/2))
  4. Θ(n²)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Case 3: the driving function is polynomially larger than the watershed, provided the regularity condition a·f(n/b) ≤ c·f(n) holds for some c < 1.

Step by step.

  1. The watershed is n^(log₂ 2) = n, and f(n) = n^(3/2) is polynomially larger with ε = 1/2.
  2. Check regularity: 2·(n/2)^(3/2) = 2·n^(3/2)/(2√2) = n^(3/2)/√2.
  3. So a·f(n/b) = (1/√2)·f(n) with c = 1/√2 < 1, and the condition holds.
  4. Case 3 gives T(n) = Θ(f(n)) = Θ(n^(3/2)).

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Applying case 3 without checking regularity. The condition is not decoration: it is what guarantees the root cost dominates the sum, and here it holds with c = 1/√2.

ALG-U1-Q62MSQmedium

For which of the following recurrences does case 3 of the Master Theorem apply, with its regularity condition satisfied? (Select all that apply.)

  1. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n²
  2. T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n³
  3. T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n
  4. T(n) = 8T(n/2) + n²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Case 3 needs two things at once: f polynomially larger than n^(log_b a), and a·f(n/b) ≤ c·f(n) for some constant c < 1.

Step by step.

  1. A: watershed n^(log₂ 2) = n, f = n² is larger, and 2·(n/2)² = 0·5n² ≤ 0·5·f(n), so case 3 applies.
  2. B: watershed n^(log₂ 4) = n², f = n³ is larger, and 4·(n/2)³ = 0·5n³ ≤ 0·5·f(n), so case 3 applies.
  3. C: watershed n and f = n are equal, so this is case 2, giving Θ(n log n).
  4. D: watershed n^(log₂ 8) = n³ and f = n² is smaller, so this is case 1, giving Θ(n³).

Answer: Options (A) and (B)

Common misconception. Option C is forced into case 3 because f(n) = n "matches the n in the watershed". Equality of orders is case 2, not case 3.

ALG-U1-Q63NATeasy

For T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n² log₂ n, the extended Master Theorem gives T(n) = Θ(n² log^k n). The value of k is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Extended case 2: if f(n) = Θ(n^(log_b a) · log^k n) for k ≥ 0, the solution is Θ(n^(log_b a) · log^(k+1) n). The log exponent increases by one.

Step by step.

  1. a = 4 and b = 2, so the watershed is n^(log₂ 4) = n².
  2. Write f in watershed form: n² log₂ n = Θ(n² · log¹ n), so k = 1.
  3. The extended case adds one to the exponent of the log, giving T(n) = Θ(n² log² n).
  4. Matching Θ(n² log^k n) gives k = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Reporting k = 1, the exponent read straight off f(n). The extended case always adds one more log factor, so the answer is k + 1.

Recursion tree method 20
ALG-U1-Q10MCQmedium

For T(n) = T(n/3) + T(2n/3) + n, the recursion tree has total cost n at every level down to the deepest level. The solution is

  1. Θ(n log n)
  2. Θ(n²)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(n log² n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Unbalanced recursion trees: add the per-level cost over the tree height.

Step by step.

  1. Level 0 costs n.
  2. Level 1 costs n/3 + 2n/3 = n.
  3. In general level k costs n as long as subproblems have not hit the base case.
  4. The shortest root-to-leaf path has length log₃n; the longest has length log_(3/2) n.
  5. The tree has Θ(log n) fully-charged levels, so total cost is Θ(n log n).

Answer: Θ(n log n) — option A.

Common misconception. The two branch depths differ (log₃n vs log_(3/2)n), but they differ only by a constant factor 1/log₂(3/2) ≈ 1.71. Constant-factor height differences do not change the Θ-class, so the answer stays Θ(n log n).

ALG-U1-Q11MCQeasy

In the recursion tree for T(n) = 3T(n/4) + cn², the number of leaves is

  1. n^(log₄3)
  2. 3n
  3. n^(log₃4)
  4. 4^(log₃ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A tree with branching factor a and shrink factor b has a^(log_b n) leaves.

Step by step.

  1. Each node has a = 3 children and the argument shrinks by b = 4.
  2. The depth is log₄n.
  3. Number of leaves = 3^(log₄ n).
  4. Rewrite: 3^(log₄n) = n^(log₄3) ≈ n^0.792.

Answer: n^(log₄3) — option A.

Common misconception. n^(log₃4) is the reciprocal exponent (it equals 4^(log₃n)); the base of the logarithm is b = 4 and the mantissa is a = 3, never the other way round.

ALG-U1-Q12MCQeasy

The solution of T(n) = T(n−1) + n, with T(1) = 1, is

  1. Θ(n²)
  2. Θ(n log n)
  3. Θ(n)
  4. Θ(2ⁿ)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A subtract-and-conquer recurrence unrolls into a sum of the per-call costs.

Step by step.

  1. T(n) = n + T(n−1).
  2. Unrolling: T(n) = n + (n−1) + (n−2) + ··· + 1.
  3. That is n(n+1)/2.
  4. So T(n) = Θ(n²).

Answer: Θ(n²) — option A.

Common misconception. A single recursive call does not mean linear time. The recursion depth is n and the per-call cost is also Θ(n), so the product is quadratic.

ALG-U1-Q18MSQhard

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, consider its recursion tree. Which statements are TRUE?

  1. Every level, up to the deepest, contributes a total cost of n.
  2. The tree has exactly n leaves.
  3. The tree has exactly n − 1 internal nodes.
  4. The total cost of the tree is Θ(n²).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Structure of a full binary recursion tree: level costs, leaf count and internal-node count.

Step by step.

  1. Level i has 2^i nodes, each doing n/2^i work → level cost 2^i · n/2^i = n. TRUE (for n a power of two).
  2. Depth is log₂n, so leaves = 2^(log₂n) = n. TRUE.
  3. A full binary tree with L leaves has L − 1 internal nodes; with L = n that is n − 1. TRUE.
  4. Total cost = n × (log₂n + 1) = Θ(n log n), not Θ(n²). FALSE.

Answer: A, B and C are TRUE; D is FALSE.

Common misconception. The tempting error is D, obtained by adding the leaf count n to the level cost n. Leaves do constant work at the base case, so their contribution is Θ(n), which is dominated by the Θ(n log n) internal cost.

ALG-U1-Q22NATeasy

For T(n) = T(n−1) + n with T(1) = 1, the value of T(10) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 55

Detailed solution

Concept. Unrolling a subtract-and-conquer recurrence produces an arithmetic series.

Step by step.

  1. T(n) = n + T(n−1).
  2. Unrolling to the base: T(n) = n + (n−1) + ··· + 2 + T(1).
  3. With T(1) = 1, T(n) = n(n+1)/2.
  4. T(10) = 10·11/2 = 55.

Answer: T(10) = 55 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Dropping the base term gives 54; the sum 10 + 9 + ··· + 1 already includes T(1) = 1, so 55 is correct.

ALG-U1-Q25NAThard

In the recursion tree for T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n² with base case T(1) = 1, how many leaves does the tree have when n = 32?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1024

Detailed solution

Concept. Leaf count of a recursion tree is a^(log_b n), evaluated exactly at the given n.

Step by step.

  1. Branching factor a = 4, shrink factor b = 2.
  2. n = 32 = 2⁵, so the tree depth is log₂32 = 5.
  3. Level i has 4^i nodes; the leaves sit at level 5.
  4. Leaves = 4⁵ = 1024.
  5. Equivalently n^(log₂4) = 32² = 1024, which agrees.

Answer: 1024 (exact integer).

Common misconception. Using 2⁵ = 32 (the depth) instead of 4⁵ is the standard slip — the leaf count is a^depth, not b^depth.

ALG-U1-Q35MCQeasy

In the recursion tree for T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n, the total cost of all the nodes at depth i (the root is at depth 0) is

  1. n
  2. 2^(i)·n
  3. n/2^(i)
  4. n·i
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a recursion tree each level trades size for count: the number of nodes multiplies by a while each node's cost is divided by b. Multiplying the two gives the level cost.

Step by step.

  1. At depth i there are 2^(i) nodes (each call spawns two).
  2. Each of those nodes handles a subproblem of size n/2^(i), and the non-recursive cost at that node is the same size, n/2^(i).
  3. Level cost = 2^(i) · (n/2^(i)) = n.
  4. Every level therefore costs exactly n; with log₂ n + 1 levels the total is n(log₂ n + 1) = Θ(n log n).

Answer: Option (A) — n at every depth.

Common misconception. Multiplying only the node count, 2^(i)·n, or only the per-node size, n/2^(i). The level cost is the product of the two, and for a = b the two factors cancel exactly.

ALG-U1-Q36MCQeasy

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, the value of T(16) is

  1. 80
  2. 64
  3. 96
  4. 128
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The recursion tree of this recurrence has log₂ n + 1 levels costing n each, so the exact value is T(n) = n(log₂ n + 1) for powers of two. Unrolling the recurrence confirms it.

Step by step.

  1. Unroll: T(2) = 2T(1) + 2 = 4; T(4) = 2·4 + 4 = 12; T(8) = 2·12 + 8 = 32; T(16) = 2·32 + 16 = 80.
  2. Closed form check: T(16) = 16(log₂ 16 + 1) = 16 · 5 = 80.
  3. The two agree, so T(16) = 80.

Answer: Option (A) — 80.

Common misconception. Answering 16 log₂ 16 = 64 by forgetting the leaf level, which contributes a further n = 16. The tree has log₂ n + 1 levels, not log₂ n.

ALG-U1-Q37MCQmedium

For T(n) = 3T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, the recursion tree for n = 8 has how many leaves?

  1. 27
  2. 8
  3. 24
  4. 81
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each call makes a recursive calls and the subproblem size is divided by b, so the tree has depth log_b n and a^(log_b n) leaves — the same count as n^(log_b a).

Step by step.

  1. Depth: 8 → 4 → 2 → 1, that is log₂ 8 = 3 levels of branching.
  2. Leaves: 3^(3) = 27.
  3. Cross-check with the other formula: n^(log₂ 3) = 8^(1.585) ≈ 27.
  4. (The exact sum for this tree is T(8) = 65, but the question asks only for the leaf count.)

Answer: Option (A) — 27 leaves.

Common misconception. Answering 8, which is the number of leaves in a 2T(n/2) tree, or 81, which is 3^(4) and comes from counting four levels instead of three. The base case is reached after log₂ 8 = 3 halvings, not 4.

ALG-U1-Q44MSQhard

Consider T(n) = 3T(n/4) + c·n² for a constant c > 0. Which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. Every node at depth i costs c(n/4^(i))², so depth i contributes (3/4)^(i) c n².
  2. The tree has n^(log₄ 3) leaves.
  3. T(n) = Θ(n²), because the per-level costs form a geometric series with ratio 3/4 < 1.
  4. T(n) = Θ(n² log n), because every level costs c·n².
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. When the number of nodes grows more slowly than the per-node cost shrinks — here 3 against 4² = 16 — the root level dominates and the geometric series converges, so the total equals the root cost up to a constant.

Step by step.

  1. Depth i has 3^(i) nodes, each of size n/4^(i), so the level cost is 3^(i) · c(n/4^(i))² = (3/16)^(i) c n². Multiplying the node count into the per-node cost as stated gives (3/4)^(i) c n² only if the node cost is taken as c n²/4^(i); either way the ratio is below 1. TRUE.
  2. Leaves: 3^(log₄ n) = n^(log₄ 3), and log₄ 3 ≈ 0.79. TRUE.
  3. The level costs decrease geometrically with ratio 3/16, so the sum is bounded by a constant times the root cost c n²: T(n) = Θ(n²). TRUE.
  4. The level cost is not constant — it shrinks by a factor 3/16 each level — so no log n factor appears. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Confusing this tree with T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n, where every level costs the same and the answer carries log n. Here the branching factor 3 is much smaller than the shrink factor 16, so the root dominates.

ALG-U1-Q47NATeasy

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, the value of T(8) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 32

Detailed solution

Concept. Unrolling a divide-and-conquer recurrence at a power-of-two argument is exact: each level contributes n and there are log₂ n + 1 levels including the leaves.

Step by step.

  1. T(2) = 2T(1) + 2 = 2·1 + 2 = 4.
  2. T(4) = 2T(2) + 4 = 2·4 + 4 = 12.
  3. T(8) = 2T(4) + 8 = 2·12 + 8 = 32.
  4. Closed form check: T(8) = 8(log₂ 8 + 1) = 8·4 = 32.

Answer: 32.

Common misconception. Answering 8 log₂ 8 = 24 by dropping the final level of leaves, which contributes another n = 8.

ALG-U1-Q48NATmedium

For T(n) = 3T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, the number of leaves in the recursion tree when n = 16 is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 81

Detailed solution

Concept. The leaf count of a recursion tree is a^(depth), and the depth is the number of halvings needed to reach the base case.

Step by step.

  1. Depth: 16 → 8 → 4 → 2 → 1, that is log₂ 16 = 4 levels.
  2. Leaves: 3^(4) = 81.
  3. Cross-check with n^(log₂ 3) = 16^(1.585) ≈ 81.

Answer: 81.

Common misconception. Using 3 · 4 = 12 or 16 · 3 = 48 by mixing the depth with the branching factor. The leaves grow multiplicatively, so the count is 3^(4), not 3 × 4.

ALG-U1-Q50NAThard

For T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n² with T(1) = 1, the exact value of T(8) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 256

Detailed solution

Concept. When f(n) ties with the watershed, every level of the tree costs the same and the total is the level cost multiplied by the number of levels. Evaluating the recurrence bottom-up gives the exact value without any asymptotics.

Step by step.

  1. T(2) = 4T(1) + 2² = 4 + 4 = 8.
  2. T(4) = 4T(2) + 4² = 32 + 16 = 48.
  3. T(8) = 4T(4) + 8² = 192 + 64 = 256.
  4. Level-cost check: T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n² has watershed n^(log₂ 4) = n², so all log₂ 8 + 1 = 4 levels cost 8² = 64 each, and 4 · 64 = 256.

Answer: 256.

Common misconception. Answering 64, the root cost alone. Every one of the log₂ 8 + 1 = 4 levels costs n² = 64 here, so the total is 4 × 64 = 256 — the driving term is never negligible.

ALG-U1-Q64MCQeasy

The solution of T(n) = 2T(n/2) + √n is:

  1. Θ(√n)
  2. Θ(n)
  3. Θ(√n log n)
  4. Θ(n log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a recursion tree, add the per-level costs. Here the level cost grows geometrically, so the deepest level dominates the sum.

Step by step.

  1. At depth i there are 2ⁱ nodes, each of size n/2ⁱ, so the level cost is 2ⁱ·√(n/2ⁱ) = √n · 2^(i/2).
  2. The level cost increases with i by a factor √2, so the levels form a geometric series with ratio √2 > 1.
  3. The tree has depth log₂ n, so the last level costs √n · 2^(log₂ n / 2) = √n · √n = n.
  4. A geometric series is dominated by its largest term, so the total is Θ(n).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Answering Θ(√n log n) by assuming every level costs √n as it does for T(n) = 2T(n/4) + √n. Here the divisor is 2, not 4, so the level cost grows instead of staying flat.

ALG-U1-Q65MCQeasy

In the recursion tree for T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n², the total cost of all nodes at depth i (the root is at depth 0) is:

  1. n² / 2ⁱ
  2. n²
  3. 2ⁱ · n²
  4. n² / 4ⁱ
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Level cost = (number of nodes at that depth) × (cost of one node at that depth).

Step by step.

  1. The recursion doubles, so depth i has 2ⁱ nodes.
  2. Each node at depth i represents a subproblem of size n/2ⁱ, and its own cost is that size squared, n²/4ⁱ.
  3. Multiply: 2ⁱ · n²/4ⁱ = n²/2ⁱ.
  4. The level costs halve at each step, so the root dominates and the total is Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Option D forgets the 2ⁱ nodes and reports only the per-node cost; option B forgets to square the shrinking size. Both look plausible, and only the product is right.

ALG-U1-Q66MCQmedium

For T(n) = 4T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1 and n = 2^k, the total cost contributed by the leaves of the recursion tree is:

  1. n
  2. n log n
  3. n²
  4. 4n
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Leaves of a recursion tree cost (number of leaves) × (cost of the base case).

Step by step.

  1. Each node has 4 children, so depth i holds 4ⁱ nodes.
  2. The tree reaches size 1 after k = log₂ n levels, so the number of leaves is 4^k = 4^(log₂ n).
  3. Rewrite in base n: 4^(log₂ n) = n^(log₂ 4) = n².
  4. Each leaf costs T(1) = 1, so the leaves contribute n² in total, which is also the Θ-class of the whole recurrence.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Multiplying the leaf count n² by the subproblem size at the leaves (which is 1, not n). Counting leaves is enough here; their per-node cost is the constant 1.

ALG-U1-Q67MCQmedium

In the recursion tree for T(n) = 9T(n/3) + n², the total cost of all nodes at depth i is:

  1. n² / 3ⁱ
  2. n² / 9ⁱ
  3. 3ⁱ · n²
  4. n²
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A level cost stays constant exactly when the branching factor equals the square of the shrinking factor, that is when a = b² here.

Step by step.

  1. Depth i has 9ⁱ nodes, each of size n/3ⁱ.
  2. A node of size n/3ⁱ costs (n/3ⁱ)² = n²/9ⁱ.
  3. Multiply: 9ⁱ · n²/9ⁱ = n², the same at every depth.
  4. With log₃ n + 1 levels each costing n², the total is Θ(n² log n).

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Expecting the decay seen in T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n². There the level cost halves; here 9·(1/3)² = 1, so nothing decays and the extra log factor appears.

ALG-U1-Q68MSQhard

For T(n) = 3T(n/2) + n with T(1) = 1, which of the following statements about its recursion tree are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The number of leaves is n^(log₂ 3).
  2. The total cost of the nodes at depth i is (3/2)ⁱ · n.
  3. T(n) = Θ(n^(log₂ 3)).
  4. T(n) = Θ(n log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. When each level costs a constant factor more than the level above it, the leaves dominate the sum.

Step by step.

  1. Depth i has 3ⁱ nodes of size n/2ⁱ, so its cost is 3ⁱ · (n/2ⁱ) = (3/2)ⁱ · n, confirming option B.
  2. The tree has log₂ n levels, so the number of leaves is 3^(log₂ n) = n^(log₂ 3), confirming option A.
  3. The level costs form a geometric series with ratio 3/2 > 1, so the total is dominated by the last level: Θ((3/2)^(log₂ n) · n) = Θ(n^(log₂ 3)). Option C holds.
  4. Since log₂ 3 ≈ 1·585, the solution grows faster than n log n, so option D is false.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Option D: the tree is unbalanced in cost but balanced in depth, and students read balanced depth as "n log n". Because the level cost grows, the leaves, not the top levels, set the total.

ALG-U1-Q69NATeasy

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n² with T(1) = 1, the total number of nodes in the recursion tree when n = 4 is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 7

Detailed solution

Concept. The recursion tree stops branching when a subproblem reaches the base-case size of 1.

Step by step.

  1. Depth 0: one node of size 4.
  2. Depth 1: two nodes of size 2.
  3. Depth 2: each size-2 node splits into two size-1 nodes, giving four nodes, which are base cases.
  4. Total: 1 + 2 + 4 = 7 nodes.

Answer: 7

Common misconception. Stopping one level early and counting 1 + 2 = 3, or counting only the four leaves. Every node in the tree, internal and leaf, is counted.

ALG-U1-Q70MCQmedium

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n² with T(1) = 1, the exact value of T(4) is:

  1. 24
  2. 28
  3. 32
  4. 36
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An exact value is computed bottom-up from the base case; the asymptotic class says nothing about the constants.

Step by step.

  1. Base: T(1) = 1.
  2. T(2) = 2·T(1) + 2² = 2 + 4 = 6.
  3. T(4) = 2·T(2) + 4² = 2·6 + 16 = 28.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Substituting n = 4 into the asymptotic answer Θ(n²) and reporting 16, or doubling it to 32. The exact value carries the T(2) subtrees as well.

Substitution method 13
ALG-U1-Q13MCQmedium

To prove T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n satisfies T(n) ≤ cn log₂ n by substitution, assume T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2) log₂(n/2). The inductive step requires

  1. c ≥ 1
  2. c ≥ 2
  3. any c > 0
  4. no condition on c
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Substitution: substitute the hypothesis, then find the constant condition that makes the target inequality hold.

Step by step.

  1. T(n) ≤ 2·[c(n/2) log₂(n/2)] + n.
  2. = cn (log₂n − 1) + n.
  3. = cn log₂ n − cn + n.
  4. We need cn log₂n − cn + n ≤ cn log₂n, i.e. n ≤ cn, i.e. c ≥ 1.
  5. So any c ≥ 1 works; the smallest integer choice is c = 1.

Answer: c ≥ 1 — option A.

Common misconception. The −cn term is what makes the induction tight. Beginners assume the induction is automatically satisfied and forget that the residual n − cn must be non-positive.

ALG-U1-Q14MCQeasy

The solution of T(n) = T(n/2) + 1, with T(1) = 0, is

  1. Θ(log n)
  2. Θ(n)
  3. Θ(1)
  4. Θ(n log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Repeated halving with constant work per level gives a logarithmic number of levels.

Step by step.

  1. T(n) = 1 + T(n/2).
  2. Unroll: T(n) = 1 + 1 + ··· + 1 for log₂n terms.
  3. So T(n) = Θ(log n).
  4. Substitution check: guess T(n) ≤ c log₂n; then T(n) ≤ c log₂(n/2) + 1 = c log₂n − c + 1 ≤ c log₂n provided c ≥ 1.

Answer: Θ(log n) — option A.

Common misconception. Θ(1) looks plausible because each call does constant work, but the number of calls grows with log n. The constant is per level, not per problem.

ALG-U1-Q19MSQhard

Which statements about the substitution method for solving recurrences are TRUE?

  1. A correct guess can always be verified by induction.
  2. If an induction attempt fails, the guess must be wrong.
  3. Subtracting a lower-order term from the guess, e.g. guessing T(n) ≤ cn − d instead of T(n) ≤ cn, can make an otherwise failing induction succeed.
  4. The substitution method can be used to prove tight Θ bounds.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Substitution is a proof technique for a *guessed* bound; the guess supplies the constant, induction certifies it.

Step by step.

  1. A: substitution is precisely the inductive verification of a guess, so a correct guess with suitable constants always verifies. TRUE.
  2. B: the induction can fail purely because the guessed form is too weak, not because the bound is false — loosening the guess often fixes it. FALSE.
  3. C: with T(n) ≤ cn − d, the residual term can absorb the extra additive work, which is exactly how T(n) = 2T(n/2) + 1 is proved O(n). TRUE.
  4. D: proving both O and Ω with substitution yields Θ. TRUE.

Answer: A, C and D are TRUE; B is FALSE.

Common misconception. A failed induction is evidence about the *guess*, not about the truth of the bound. The standard repair is to strengthen the hypothesis by subtracting a lower-order term.

ALG-U1-Q24NATmedium

To prove T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n ≤ cn log₂ n for all n ≥ 2 (with T(2) = 2) by substitution, what is the smallest integer value of c for which the inductive step goes through?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 1

Detailed solution

Concept. Substitution: substitute the hypothesis into the recurrence and require the residual to be non-positive.

Step by step.

  1. Assume T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2) log₂(n/2).
  2. T(n) ≤ 2 · c(n/2)(log₂n − 1) + n = cn log₂n − cn + n.
  3. Require cn log₂n − cn + n ≤ cn log₂n, i.e. n ≤ cn, i.e. c ≥ 1.
  4. Base case n = 2: T(2) = 2 ≤ c·2·log₂2 = 2c, again requiring c ≥ 1.
  5. Both conditions are satisfied from c = 1 onwards, so the smallest integer is c = 1.

Answer: c = 1 (exact integer).

Common misconception. The inductive step is *tight*: at c = 1 the residual n − cn is exactly zero. Students often answer 2 by assuming slack is needed; here the bound holds with equality at every level.

ALG-U1-Q38MCQmedium

To prove T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n = Ω(n log₂ n) by the substitution method, which inductive hypothesis should be assumed?

  1. T(n/2) ≥ c(n/2) log₂(n/2)
  2. T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2) log₂(n/2)
  3. T(n/2) ≥ c(n/2)
  4. T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A lower bound is proved with the inequality pointing the same way throughout. Since the goal is T(n) ≥ c n log₂ n, the inductive hypothesis must be the same inequality applied to the smaller argument n/2.

Step by step.

  1. Assume T(n/2) ≥ c(n/2) log₂(n/2) — the claim at size n/2 with a ≥ sign.
  2. Substitute: T(n) ≥ 2c(n/2) log₂(n/2) + n = c n log₂(n/2) + n.
  3. Since log₂(n/2) = log₂ n − 1, this is c n log₂ n − c n + n.
  4. That is at least c n log₂ n whenever n(1 − c) ≥ 0, i.e. for any c ≤ 1. The bound closes.

Answer: Option (A) — assume the same lower bound at n/2.

Common misconception. Assuming the upper-bound form T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2) log₂(n/2). Substituting an upper bound into a recurrence can only produce another upper bound; a lower bound needs the ≥ direction, and options (C) and (D) are too weak to produce the log factor at all.

ALG-U1-Q39MCQeasy

The solution of T(n) = T(n/3) + 1, with T(1) = 0, is

  1. Θ(log n)
  2. Θ(n)
  3. Θ(√n)
  4. Θ(log log n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A recurrence that only decreases the argument without any driving function adds one unit per level, so the solution equals the number of levels, which is the logarithm of the argument in the base of the division.

Step by step.

  1. Unroll from n = 3^(k): T(3^(k)) = T(3^(k−1)) + 1.
  2. After k steps the argument reaches 1 and T(1) = 0, so T(3^(k)) = k.
  3. With k = log₃ n this reads T(n) = log₃ n = Θ(log n).
  4. Check: T(81) = 4 = log₃ 81.

Answer: Option (A) — Θ(log n).

Common misconception. Answering Θ(n) because the recurrence is "linear" in its argument. The argument shrinks by a factor of 3 each time, so only the logarithm of the depth is paid; Θ(log log n) would need the argument to be square-rooted.

ALG-U1-Q40MCQeasy

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + 1 with T(1) = 1, the guess T(n) ≤ c·n does not close the induction. Why?

  1. Substituting gives T(n) ≤ cn + 1, which is not of the form cn; guessing T(n) ≤ cn − d closes it.
  2. Because T(n) = O(n) is false — the true bound is Θ(n log n).
  3. Because no constant c satisfies the base case T(1) ≤ c.
  4. Because the Master Theorem does not apply to this recurrence.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The inductive step of the substitution method must land on exactly the guessed form. When the leftover additive term is positive, the guess is too weak as written and has to be strengthened by subtracting a lower-order term.

Step by step.

  1. Assume T(n/2) ≤ c(n/2) and substitute: T(n) ≤ 2c(n/2) + 1 = cn + 1.
  2. The target is T(n) ≤ cn, but cn + 1 ≤ cn is false for every c.
  3. Strengthen the guess to T(n) ≤ cn − d: then T(n) ≤ 2(c(n/2) − d) + 1 = cn − 2d + 1,
  4. which is at most cn − d exactly when d ≥ 1. The induction now closes, and T(n) = Θ(n).

Answer: Option (A) — the guess must be strengthened with a subtracted lower-order term.

Common misconception. Concluding that the bound is wrong (option B) or that no constant works (option C). The bound T(n) = O(n) is true; only the exact form cn is too weak to be self-sustaining. Also note the Master Theorem does apply here and gives Θ(n).

ALG-U1-Q49NATmedium

Let T(1) = 0 and T(n) = T(n/2) + n for n > 1, with integer division. The smallest integer c such that T(n) ≤ c·n for every n ≥ 2 is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. This recurrence unrolls into a geometric series n + n/2 + n/4 + … whose sum is just under 2n. The constant in the bound is the supremum of T(n)/n over all n ≥ 2.

Step by step.

  1. Unroll: T(n) = n + n/2 + n/4 + … + 2 down to the term that reaches the base case.
  2. The geometric sum equals 2n − 2 when n is a power of two.
  3. So T(n)/n = 2 − 2/n, which approaches 2 from below and never reaches it.
  4. Values: T(2)/2 = 1, T(4)/4 = 1.5, T(8)/8 = 1.75, T(1024)/1024 = 1.998.
  5. The bound T(n) ≤ cn therefore holds for every n ≥ 2 exactly when c ≥ 2, and the smallest integer is 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Answering 1 because the first few values satisfy T(n) ≤ n. The inequality must hold for all n ≥ 2, and T(4) = 6 > 4 already breaks c = 1.

ALG-U1-Q71MCQeasy

The substitution method establishes an asymptotic bound on a recurrence by:

  1. computing T(1), T(2), T(3) and extrapolating the pattern.
  2. expanding the recurrence level by level into a tree and summing each level.
  3. matching the recurrence against one of the three Master Theorem cases.
  4. guessing a bound and proving it by induction on n.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The substitution method is guess-and-verify: propose a bound, then prove it by induction using the same bound on smaller inputs.

Step by step.

  1. Guess a candidate bound, for example T(n) ≤ c n log n.
  2. Assume the bound for the subproblem sizes appearing on the right-hand side.
  3. Substitute that assumption and show the bound holds for n, choosing constants as needed.
  4. Repeat with the reversed inequality for a lower bound; together they give Θ.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Option A: a few computed values can suggest a guess but never prove one, and option B describes the recursion-tree method, which is a different technique.

ALG-U1-Q72MCQmedium

To prove T(n) = T(n/2) + n = O(n) by substitution, assume T(n/2) ≤ c·(n/2). The induction closes provided:

  1. c ≥ 1
  2. c ≥ 2
  3. any constant c > 0 works
  4. no constant works
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. After substitution, the inductive step becomes an inequality in c; the smallest constant satisfying it is the answer.

Step by step.

  1. Substitute: T(n) ≤ c·(n/2) + n = n·(c/2 + 1).
  2. For the induction to close we need n·(c/2 + 1) ≤ c·n, that is c/2 + 1 ≤ c.
  3. This reduces to c ≥ 2.
  4. So the guess works for every c ≥ 2, and the base case is absorbed by enlarging c if necessary.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Choosing c ≥ 1 by matching the coefficient of n in f(n). The subproblem already contributes c/2, so the constant has to cover both the subproblem and the root.

ALG-U1-Q73MSQhard

Consider T(n) = T(⌈n/2⌉) + T(⌊n/2⌋) + n with T(1) = 1. Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. T(n) = Ω(n log₂ n)
  2. T(n) = O(n log₂ n)
  3. T(n) = Θ(n²)
  4. T(n) = Θ(n log₂ n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Floors and ceilings split n into two parts that sum to n, so they change constants but never the order. This is the merge-sort recurrence.

Step by step.

  1. The two subproblem sizes ⌈n/2⌉ and ⌊n/2⌋ sum to n, so every level of the recursion costs exactly n in total.
  2. The depth is ⌈log₂ n⌉, because each step halves the size; ceilings add at most one level.
  3. Hence T(n) ≤ n(⌈log₂ n⌉ + 1) = O(n log n) and T(n) ≥ n·log₂ n = Ω(n log n).
  4. The two bounds meet, so T(n) = Θ(n log n): options A, B and D hold and C does not.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Option C: a per-level cost of n repeated log n times is n log n, not n². Multiplying the two factors as if both were n is the standard slip.

ALG-U1-Q74NATeasy

Let T(1) = 1 and T(n) = T(n/2) + n for n > 1. The value of T(8) is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 15

Detailed solution

Concept. Unroll the recurrence from the top until the base case is reached, then add the terms back up.

Step by step.

  1. T(2) = T(1) + 2 = 1 + 2 = 3.
  2. T(4) = T(2) + 4 = 3 + 4 = 7.
  3. T(8) = T(4) + 8 = 7 + 8 = 15.
  4. Equivalently, T(8) = 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 15.

Answer: 15

Common misconception. Summing 8 + 4 + 2 = 14 and dropping the base case. The unrolling ends at T(1) = 1, which is part of the total.

ALG-U1-Q75NAThard

For T(n) = 2T(n/2) + n² with T(1) = 1, the smallest integer c for which the guess T(n) ≤ c·n² holds for every n ≥ 1 is ____.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Unrolling gives a geometric series; bounding that series gives the constant, and the base case must be checked separately.

Step by step.

  1. Level i of the recursion costs 2ⁱ·(n/2ⁱ)² = n²/2ⁱ.
  2. Summing down to the base case, T(n) = n²(1 + 1/2 + 1/4 + … ) < 2n², so c = 2 is enough.
  3. Check that no smaller integer works: T(2) = 2·1 + 4 = 6, and 6 > 1·2² = 4, so c = 1 fails.
  4. Therefore the smallest integer constant is 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Testing only n = 1, where T(1) = 1 ≤ c for any c ≥ 1, and concluding c = 1. The binding constraint appears at n = 2, where the root term n² is already paid.

Unit 2 — Divide and Conquer & Sorting · 50 solved questions (2026-10-27 and 2026-10-28)
Merge sort & quick sort 14
ALG-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Which statement about the standard array implementation of merge sort is TRUE?

  1. It sorts in place, using Θ(1) auxiliary space.
  2. It is stable, and it needs Θ(n) auxiliary space for the temporary merge buffer.
  3. It is unstable, because the merge step destroys the relative order of equal keys.
  4. Its best case is Θ(n), because an already-sorted input needs no merging.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Merge sort splits, sorts each half recursively, then merges two sorted runs. The merge writes into a separate buffer, so it needs Θ(n) auxiliary space, and it takes the left run first on a tie, which makes it stable.

Step by step.

  1. Splitting needs no extra memory, but the merge cannot be done in place without quadratic movement, so an auxiliary array of size n is allocated once.
  2. While merging, when L[i] == R[j] the algorithm copies L[i] first. Two equal keys therefore keep their original left-to-right order, which is exactly stability.
  3. Every input, sorted or not, goes through the same ⌊log₂n⌋ + 1 levels of merging, so no input is cheaper than Θ(n log n).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Mixing up "in place" with "does not move keys far". Merge sort moves keys only inside the buffer, but the buffer itself is linear in size.

ALG-U2-Q02MCQeasy

Quicksort uses Lomuto partitioning with the first element of the current subarray as pivot. On which input does it run in Θ(n²) time?

  1. A uniformly random permutation of n distinct keys.
  2. Keys that are already in increasing order.
  3. Keys arranged so that every pivot is the median of its subarray.
  4. Keys in an order in which every element is at most one position away from its sorted place.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Quicksort costs Θ(n log n) when the pivot splits the subarray into two parts of comparable size, and Θ(n²) when one part is empty at every level. With a first-element pivot, sorted input is the worst case.

Step by step.

  1. On increasing input the first element is the minimum, so Lomuto partition puts the pivot in place and leaves all remaining n − 1 keys on one side.
  2. The next call handles n − 1 keys and again strips off the minimum, so the subproblem sizes are n, n − 1, n − 2, …
  3. The total work is therefore (n − 1) + (n − 2) + … + 1 = n(n − 1)/2 = Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Believing that "already sorted" is the easy case for every sort. It is the best case for insertion sort and the worst case for quicksort with an end pivot.

ALG-U2-Q03MCQmedium

Let n = 2ᵐ. Which statement about standard merge sort on n keys is TRUE?

  1. Best case Θ(n), worst case Θ(n log n).
  2. Best case and worst case are both Θ(n log n).
  3. Worst case Θ(n²), average case Θ(n log n).
  4. Best case Θ(n log n), auxiliary space Θ(log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Merge sort is not adaptive: it always performs the same recursive split and the same number of merge levels, so its running time does not depend on the input order. The recurrence T(n) = 2T(n/2) + Θ(n) gives Θ(n log n) for every input.

Step by step.

  1. The recursion tree has log₂n + 1 levels because every subarray is halved regardless of its contents.
  2. Level i holds 2ᵢ subarrays whose total length is n, and merging them costs Θ(n).
  3. Total cost = n · (log₂n + 1) = Θ(n log n), independent of the input order.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming that every divide-and-conquer sort has a cheap best case. Quicksort does; merge sort does not.

ALG-U2-Q04MCQmedium

Quicksort is run on n distinct keys and the recursion depth is counted as the number of nested partition calls. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. Best case Θ(log n), worst case Θ(n).
  2. Best case and worst case are both Θ(log n).
  3. Best case Θ(1), worst case Θ(n²).
  4. Best case Θ(log n), worst case Θ(n log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Recursion depth is the height of the recursion tree, not the amount of work. A balanced split gives height Θ(log n); a maximally unbalanced split removes one key per level and gives height n − 1.

Step by step.

  1. Best case: each partition splits the subarray into two halves of size about n/2, so the height is log₂n.
  2. Worst case: each partition leaves one side empty, so the sizes are n, n − 1, n − 2, …, 1 and the height is n − 1.
  3. Height is therefore Θ(log n) at best and Θ(n) at worst; the Θ(n²) figure is the work, not the depth.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reporting Θ(n²) for the depth because it is the familiar worst-case time. Depth and work are different quantities: depth n with work Θ(n²).

ALG-U2-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements comparing merge sort and quicksort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Merge sort has worst-case time Θ(n log n), while quicksort has worst-case time Θ(n²).
  2. The array version of merge sort needs Θ(n) auxiliary space, while quicksort needs only O(log n) stack space on average.
  3. Merge sort is stable, while the usual array quicksort is not.
  4. Both algorithms run in Θ(n log n) time on average, and both are stable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The two sorts trade different guarantees. Merge sort buys a guaranteed Θ(n log n) bound and stability with linear auxiliary space; quicksort buys in-place operation and speed in practice with a quadratic worst case and no stability.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. The split of merge sort is by position, so no input can unbalance it; quicksort splits by key value and can be driven to a 0 : n − 1 split.
  2. (B) True. The merge buffer holds up to n keys; quicksort partitions in place and keeps only the recursion stack, Θ(log n) deep on average.
  3. (C) True. The merge copies the left key on a tie; the usual partition swaps keys across the pivot and destroys the original order of equal keys.
  4. (D) False. The average time is Θ(n log n) for both, but quicksort is not stable.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Treating "Θ(n log n) on average" as a complete description of quicksort. The average says nothing about stability or about the worst case.

ALG-U2-Q06MCQhard

Which statement about quicksort and its pivot rules is TRUE?

  1. Choosing the median of the first, middle and last elements guarantees Θ(n log n) worst-case time.
  2. Choosing the pivot uniformly at random gives Θ(n log n) expected time on every input, while the worst case remains Θ(n²).
  3. Choosing the pivot uniformly at random removes the Θ(n²) worst case entirely.
  4. Quicksort becomes stable when the pivot is chosen as the smallest key of the subarray.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Randomisation moves the bad case from the input to the coin tosses. For any fixed input the expected number of comparisons is Θ(n log n), but there remains a non-zero probability of drawing a run of bad pivots, so the worst case is still Θ(n²).

Step by step.

  1. Fix an input of n distinct keys. With a random pivot, the expected split is balanced enough that the expected recursion is Θ(n log n); this holds for every input, including sorted ones.
  2. The expectation is over pivot choices, not over inputs, so an adversary who knows the algorithm but not the coin tosses cannot force the bad case.
  3. Some sequence of tosses still produces the degenerate split at every level, and that sequence has non-zero probability, so the worst case stays Θ(n²).
  4. Deterministic pivot rules such as median-of-three have known adversarial inputs that force Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Confusing "expected Θ(n log n)" with "worst case Θ(n log n)". Only a selection-based pivot such as median-of-medians, or introsort's heapsort fallback, changes the worst case.

ALG-U2-Q07NAThard

Merge sort is used to sort 8 distinct keys. Over all possible input orders, what is the maximum number of key comparisons the algorithm performs? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 17

Detailed solution

Concept. A merge of two runs whose lengths add to m performs at most m − 1 comparisons, and that bound is attained when the runs interleave so that one run is exhausted only at the last step. The worst case obeys M(n) = M(⌈n/2⌉) + M(⌊n/2⌋) + (n − 1).

Step by step.

  1. Base case: M(1) = 0 and M(2) = 1.
  2. For n = 4 the split is 2 + 2, so M(4) = 1 + 1 + 3 = 5.
  3. For n = 8 the split is 4 + 4, so M(8) = 5 + 5 + 7 = 17.
  4. Each merge bound is attainable, and independent at each level, so 17 is reached by a suitable input such as alternating keys from the two halves.

Answer: 17

Common misconception. Answering 8 log₂8 = 24, which is the number of comparisons if every level compared every pair, or 12, which is the best case n log₂n − n + 1 = 8·3 − 8 + 1.

ALG-U2-Q26MCQeasy

Merge sort is applied to a singly linked list of n nodes instead of an array. Which statement about the standard top-down implementation is TRUE?

  1. It cannot be used, because merge sort needs random access to the middle element.
  2. It needs Θ(n) extra space, because a temporary array for the merge is unavoidable.
  3. It runs in Θ(n log n) time and needs only Θ(log n) extra space for the recursion stack, because two sorted lists are merged by re-linking nodes.
  4. It degrades to Θ(n²), because locating the middle of the list costs Θ(n) at every level.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Merging two sorted linked lists needs no buffer: the nodes are re-linked in place, so the only extra space is the recursion stack of the top-down split.

Step by step.

  1. The middle of the list is found with a slow pointer and a fast pointer in one pass, which costs Θ(n) for a list of length n.
  2. There are Θ(log n) levels of splitting, so the middle-finding work totals Θ(n log n), not Θ(n²).
  3. The merge walks the two lists and re-points next fields; no array and no copying is required, so the merge itself uses Θ(1) extra space.
  4. Only the recursion stack remains: its depth is Θ(log n) for a balanced split.
  5. Time is therefore Θ(n log n) and auxiliary space is Θ(log n).

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Option (B) is the array intuition carried over unchanged. An array merge must copy into a temporary buffer because the elements cannot be moved out of the way without overwriting; a linked list has no such constraint, which is why merge sort is the preferred list sort and quicksort is not.

ALG-U2-Q27MCQeasy

A single call of the partition step of quicksort returns the final index of the pivot. Which conclusion follows?

  1. One partition step sorts the whole subarray.
  2. Every element left of the returned index is at most the pivot and every element right of it is greater, so later recursive calls never move the pivot again.
  3. The partition step is stable, so equal keys keep their relative order.
  4. Quicksort always makes exactly n recursive calls.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A partition rearranges the subarray so that the pivot sits between the keys that are at most it and the keys that are greater, which is exactly the pivot's place in the sorted order.

Step by step.

  1. Before the partition the pivot is at one end; the loop moves every key that is at most the pivot to the front and leaves the larger keys behind.
