  2. The final exchange puts the pivot between those two groups, so its index is final.
  3. Both recursive calls then work on strict subarrays that exclude the pivot.
  4. Hence no comparison with the pivot is ever repeated and the pivot is never moved again.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Option (D) confuses the number of recursive calls with the number of keys. A balanced run makes about n−1 calls, a degenerate run makes n−1 as well, but a three-way partition on equal keys can make only one. Option (C) is the classic error: the swap-based partition is not stable.

ALG-U2-Q28MCQmedium

Quicksort is run on an array of n keys that are all equal. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. Both the two-way (Lomuto) partition and the three-way partition finish in Θ(n log n).
  2. Both finish in Θ(n²).
  3. Both finish in Θ(n).
  4. The two-way partition takes Θ(n²), because each call removes only the pivot, while a three-way partition finishes in Θ(n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-way partition sends every key equal to the pivot to one side, so the recursion shrinks by one element per level; a three-way partition groups the equals in the middle and stops.

Step by step.

  1. With all keys equal, every key satisfies key ≤ pivot, so the two-way partition returns an index at one end of the subarray.
  2. The recursive call is then on n−1 keys, giving the recurrence T(n) = T(n−1) + Θ(n) = Θ(n²).
  3. A three-way partition (Dutch national flag) splits the subarray into keys < pivot, keys = pivot and keys > pivot.
  4. When every key is equal, the equals group is the whole subarray, both outer parts are empty, and the recursion stops after one pass of Θ(n).

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Assuming that a 'balanced on average' sort cannot hit its quadratic case on a friendly-looking input. Duplicate keys are a standard adversarial input for a two-way quicksort.

ALG-U2-Q29MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about quicksort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Choosing the pivot uniformly at random makes the expected running time Θ(n log n) for every fixed input.
  2. Recursing into the smaller part and looping on the larger part bounds the recursion depth by Θ(log n) in the worst case.
  3. A three-way partition sorts an array with many duplicate keys in Θ(n) time.
  4. Quicksort is a stable sort.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Randomisation changes the guarantee from 'for every input' to 'in expectation over the pivot choices', which is a different and often stronger statement.

Step by step.

  1. (A) For a fixed input the algorithm still makes random choices; linearity of expectation over the Θ(n log n) expected number of comparisons holds for each input separately. TRUE.
  2. (B) After each partition the smaller part has at most half the keys of its parent, so the depth of the recursion is at most log₂ n whatever the pivot rule. TRUE.
  3. (C) A three-way partition puts all keys equal to the pivot in the middle and never recurses on them, so an array of equal keys is finished in one pass. TRUE.
  4. (D) The partition exchanges keys at a distance, which can reorder equal keys. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Reading 'randomised' as 'no worst case'. A randomised quicksort still has a Θ(n²) worst case for a particular unlucky sequence of pivots; what disappears is the fixed adversarial input.

ALG-U2-Q30NATeasy

Two sorted runs of 5 and 8 keys are merged by the standard two-way merge. What is the largest number of key comparisons the merge can perform? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 12

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-way merge compares the two current heads and outputs the smaller one, so every comparison removes exactly one key except the last, when one run is already exhausted.

Step by step.

  1. Each comparison consumes one key from one of the two runs.
  2. The merge ends as soon as one run is empty; the remaining keys of the other run are copied without any comparison.
  3. The worst case leaves exactly one key in a run when the other is exhausted, so the number of comparisons is at most m + n − 1.
  4. Here m + n − 1 = 5 + 8 − 1 = 12.

Answer: 12.

Common misconception. Answering 13 by adding the run lengths, or 8 by assuming the longer run is scanned completely. The minimum is 5, reached when every key of the shorter run is smaller than every key of the longer one.

ALG-U2-Q31MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the comparison count of quicksort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. On a uniformly random permutation the expected number of comparisons is 2n ln n + O(n), about 1.39 n log₂ n.
  2. If every partition splits off exactly one element, then T(n) = T(n−1) + Θ(n) gives T(n) = Θ(n²).
  3. That same one-sided recurrence gives a recursion depth of Θ(log n).
  4. If every partition splits the array in the ratio 1 : 9, then T(n) = T(n/10) + T(9n/10) + Θ(n) is Θ(n log n).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A quicksort recurrence is governed by how much of the array the two recursive calls still cover: a constant fraction below 1 keeps the level cost Θ(n) and the depth Θ(log n).

Step by step.

  1. (A) The expected comparison count is Σₖ₋ₖ 2/(j−i+1) over all pairs, which evaluates to 2n ln n + O(n); converting to base 2 gives the factor 2/ln 2 ≈ 1.39. TRUE.
  2. (B) Unrolling gives n + (n−1) + ⋯ + 1 = n(n+1)/2 = Θ(n²). TRUE.
  3. (C) The same recurrence removes one element per level, so its depth is n, not log n. FALSE.
  4. (D) The level cost is at most n and the depth is log₁₀/₉ n = Θ(log n), so the total is Θ(n log n); the constant factor is about 1/log₂(10/9) ≈ 6.6. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming any unbalanced split is quadratic. Only a split that removes a constant number of elements per level is quadratic; a fixed ratio such as 1 : 9 still leaves a constant fraction to recurse on.

ALG-U2-Q32NAThard

Quicksort uses the Lomuto partition with the last element as pivot. Its loop performs the exchange a[i] ↔ a[j] every time a[j] ≤ pivot, even when i = j, and one final exchange places the pivot. Run on the whole array [7, 3, 9, 4, 2, 8, 5], how many exchanges does the complete quicksort perform in total? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 9

Detailed solution

Concept. The exchange count of a Lomuto quicksort is a trace, not a formula: every partition must be followed by hand, and self-exchanges count because the code executes them.

Step by step.

  1. First partition, pivot 5: 3 and 4 and 2 are moved (3 exchanges) and the pivot is placed (1), giving [3, 4, 2, 5, 9, 8, 7] with the pivot at index 4 (1-based). Total so far 4.
  2. Left part [3, 4, 2], pivot 2: no key is ≤ 2, so the only exchange is the final one, giving [2, 4, 3]. Total 5.
  3. Left part [4, 3], pivot 3: no key is ≤ 3, so again only the final exchange, giving [2, 3, 4]. Total 6.
  4. Right part [9, 8, 7], pivot 7: no key is ≤ 7, so only the final exchange, giving [7, 8, 9]. Total 7.
  5. Right part [8, 9], pivot 9: 8 ≤ 9 causes a self-exchange a[i] ↔ a[i] (1) and the final exchange is also a self-exchange (1). Total 9.
  6. The array is now sorted and no further call has more than one element.

Answer: 9.

Common misconception. Reporting 7 by skipping the two self-exchanges in the last partition. In Lomuto's loop the exchange is executed whenever a[j] ≤ pivot, whether or not i = j, and the final exchange is executed even when the pivot is already in place - the question fixes that convention precisely so the count is unambiguous.

Counting & radix sort 12
ALG-U2-Q08MCQeasy

Counting sort is applied to n = 1000 keys, each an integer in the range 0 to 99. What is its running time?

  1. Θ(n log n)
  2. Θ(n + k), which here is Θ(n) because k < n
  3. Θ(n·k)
  4. Θ(n²)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting sort never compares keys. It counts occurrences in an array of size k + 1 and then writes the output by prefix sums, so the cost is one pass to count and one pass to place: Θ(n + k).

Step by step.

  1. Build the frequency array C[0..99]: one pass over the n keys, Θ(n).
  2. Convert C to prefix sums: one pass over the k + 1 counters, Θ(k).
  3. Write the output: one more pass over the n keys, Θ(n).
  4. With k = 100 and n = 1000, k is dominated by n, so the total is Θ(n + k) = Θ(n).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Thinking that "linear time" means the key range is irrelevant. The term k is part of the cost; it is small here only because the keys are bounded by 99.

ALG-U2-Q09MCQeasy

LSD radix sort sorts digit by digit, starting from the least significant digit. Why must each digit sort be stable?

  1. Because an unstable digit sort would make the algorithm slower.
  2. Because the order already established by the less significant digits must survive when two keys tie on the current digit.
  3. Because radix sort compares digits with one another and needs them sorted.
  4. Because stability is what makes radix sort in place.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. LSD radix sort treats the digits already processed as the low-order part of the key. Two keys that tie on the digit now being sorted must keep the relative order their lower digits gave them, otherwise that earlier work is destroyed.

Step by step.

  1. After pass j the keys are sorted by their j least significant digits.
  2. Pass j + 1 sorts by digit j + 1. Keys that tie on that digit must remain in the order the previous pass produced, which is exactly what a stable sort guarantees.
  3. With a stable per-digit sort the invariant "sorted by the last j digits" holds after every pass, so after the last pass the keys are fully sorted.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming that any correct digit sort will do. MSD radix sort can recurse on buckets, but the LSD version is correct only with a stable subroutine.

ALG-U2-Q10MCQeasy

Counting sort first fills a frequency array C[0..k] and then converts it into prefix sums (running totals). Immediately after that conversion, what does C[v] hold?

  1. The number of keys equal to v.
  2. The number of keys that are ≤ v, which is the last 1-based output position of value v.
  3. The number of distinct key values that are ≤ v.
  4. The 0-based index of the first occurrence of value v in the output.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The prefix-sum step turns counts into positions. After it, C[v] is the cumulative count of keys up to and including v, so a key with value v belongs at output position C[v] (1-based) once that counter is decremented.

Step by step.

  1. Before the conversion C[v] is the frequency of v.
  2. The conversion sets C[v] ← C[v] + C[v − 1] for v = 1..k, so C[v] becomes the number of keys that are ≤ v.
  3. Walking the input from right to left, each key of value v is written at position C[v] and then C[v] is decremented, which keeps equal keys in their original order.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Using the frequency array directly as an index, which places every duplicate at the same output slot.

ALG-U2-Q11MCQmedium

Which statement about the Ω(n log n) lower bound for sorting is TRUE?

  1. It applies to every sorting algorithm, including counting sort and radix sort.
  2. It applies to any algorithm that learns about the input order only through pairwise comparisons; counting sort avoids it by using a key as an array index.
  3. It states that no algorithm can sort n keys with fewer than n log₂n comparisons, and that value is exact for every n.
  4. It follows from the fact that n! > 2ⁿ for every n ≥ 1.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The bound is a decision-tree argument: a comparison sort must distinguish n! possible orders, and a binary tree of height h has at most 2ʰ leaves, so h ≥ log₂(n!) = Θ(n log n). It constrains comparison sorts only.

Step by step.

  1. Model the algorithm as a binary decision tree whose internal nodes are comparisons.
  2. Each of the n! permutations must reach its own leaf, so the tree has at least n! leaves.
  3. A binary tree with L leaves has height at least log₂L, hence h ≥ log₂(n!) = Θ(n log n).
  4. Counting sort and radix sort never branch on comparisons; they read the key as an address, so the decision-tree model does not apply to them.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Believing the bound covers all sorting. It covers comparison sorting; the escape route is to use the key value as an index, which requires a bounded key range.

ALG-U2-Q12MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about counting sort and radix sort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Counting sort is stable when the output pass walks the input from right to left.
  2. Radix sort with base b over d-digit keys runs in Θ(d(n + b)).
  3. Counting sort uses Θ(k) auxiliary space, where k is the size of the key range.
  4. Radix sort is a comparison sort, so the Ω(n log n) bound applies to it.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both algorithms trade memory for time: they use the key value as an index instead of comparing keys. Counting sort costs Θ(n + k) time and Θ(k) space; radix sort repeats a stable linear pass once per digit.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Walking right to left and decrementing the position counter places later duplicates after earlier ones, which preserves their input order.
  2. (B) True. Each of the d passes counts into b buckets and moves n keys, so one pass is Θ(n + b) and the whole sort is Θ(d(n + b)).
  3. (C) True. The counter array has k + 1 entries, and the output buffer adds Θ(n).
  4. (D) False. Radix sort distributes keys into buckets by digit; it never compares two keys.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming a linear-time sort must be comparison based. Both algorithms here are distribution sorts, and that is why they can beat Ω(n log n).

ALG-U2-Q13NATmedium

A radix sort with base b = 1000 is applied to n = 10⁶ keys, each a 9-digit decimal integer (every key is below 10⁹). Counting each pass as n + b elementary operations, what is the total number of elementary operations performed? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3003000

Detailed solution

Concept. Radix sort needs one pass per digit in the chosen base, and one pass costs n + b operations: n to distribute the keys into buckets and b to scan the counters.

Step by step.

  1. The number of passes is d = ⌈log₁₀₀₀(10⁹)⌉ = 3, because 1000³ = 10⁹ covers every 9-digit decimal key.
  2. One pass costs n + b = 10⁶ + 1000 = 1,001,000 operations.
  3. Total = d(n + b) = 3 × 1,001,000 = 3,003,000.

Answer: 3003000

Common misconception. Using d = 9, the number of decimal digits, instead of the number of digits in the chosen base. The base fixes d: base 1000 packs three decimal digits into one pass.

ALG-U2-Q33MCQeasy

Which of the following does counting sort NOT use?

  1. A count array of size k for keys in the range 0..k−1.
  2. A comparison between two keys.
  3. A prefix-sum pass over the count array.
  4. A stable placement pass over the input.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting sort indexes an array by key value instead of deciding order by comparing keys, which is why it escapes the Ω(n log n) comparison lower bound.

Step by step.

  1. Counting sort builds C[v] = number of keys equal to v for v in 0..k−1; this uses a count array of size k.
  2. A prefix sum turns C[v] into the number of keys ≤ v, which gives every key its final position.
  3. Placing the keys from right to left makes the pass stable.
  4. No step ever compares two keys with each other.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing every sort must compare. Only comparison sorts are bound by Ω(n log n); counting sort trades a Θ(k) array for the right to avoid comparisons.

ALG-U2-Q34MCQeasy

n = 500 keys, each an integer in the range 0..999 999, must be sorted. Counting sort runs in Θ(n + k). Why is it a poor choice here?

  1. The 10⁶-entry count array dominates the cost, so the running time is Θ(k) = Θ(10⁶) rather than Θ(n).
  2. Counting sort requires all keys to be distinct.
  3. Counting sort is unstable when the key range is large.
  4. Counting sort cannot handle keys above 65 535.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting sort pays for the size of the key universe, not only for the number of keys.

Step by step.

  1. Here k = 10⁶ and n = 500, so Θ(n + k) is Θ(10⁶).
  2. The count array of 10⁶ entries must be zeroed and then prefix-summed, which costs more than a comparison sort of 500 keys by many orders of magnitude.
  3. Distinctness is not required - the prefix sum handles duplicates.
  4. Stability does not depend on the key range, and there is no 16-bit limit in the algorithm.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Quoting Θ(n + k) as if it were Θ(n). The k term is the whole point: a sparse, wide key range makes counting sort arbitrarily bad.

ALG-U2-Q35MCQmedium

n keys, each an integer in the range 1..n², must be sorted. Which method runs in Θ(n) time?

  1. Any comparison sort, because the keys are integers.
  2. Counting sort with a count array of size n².
  3. Radix sort with base n, which needs only two digit passes.
  4. Quicksort with a median-of-three pivot.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Radix sort in base b costs Θ(d(n + b)) with d = ⌈log_b M⌉; choosing b ≈ n makes the per-pass cost Θ(n) and the pass count a constant.

Step by step.

  1. The keys are below n², so in base n each key has d = ⌈log_n n²⌉ = 2 digits.
  2. Each pass is a stable counting sort over n + 1 buckets, costing Θ(n + n) = Θ(n).
  3. Two passes give Θ(2n) = Θ(n).
  4. Counting sort alone would need a count array of size n², costing Θ(n²), and any comparison sort costs Ω(n log n).

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Choosing counting sort because the keys are small integers - they are small only relative to n², which is far too wide. The comparison lower bound also applies to integer keys unless a non-comparison method is used.

ALG-U2-Q36MCQmedium

LSD radix sort with base b is applied to n keys whose largest value is M. It performs d = ⌈log_b M⌉ passes, each costing Θ(n + b). Which base minimises the total cost Θ(d(n + b))?

  1. b = 2, because each pass is then as cheap as possible.
  2. b ≈ n, which balances the per-pass cost n + b against the number of passes.
  3. b = M, because a single pass then suffices.
  4. b = n², because the count array then fits in cache.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The total cost d(n + b) = (ln M / ln b)(n + b) trades the number of passes against the width of each pass's count array.

Step by step.

  1. Write the cost as C(b) = (ln M / ln b)(n + b).
  2. For b much smaller than n the term n + b ≈ n, but d grows like ln M / ln b - too many passes.
  3. For b much larger than n the pass count shrinks slowly while each pass pays Θ(b) - too wide a count array.
  4. The minimum of C(b) sits near b = n; at that point d = log_n M and the cost is Θ(n log_n M).
  5. b = M gives one pass but a count array of M entries, which is exactly the counting sort cost the radix trick was meant to avoid.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Maximising the base to minimise the passes. One pass with a count array of size M is counting sort, whose cost is Θ(M) and can dwarf Θ(n).

ALG-U2-Q37MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about radix sort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. LSD radix sort is a stable sort of the whole keys.
  2. It compares keys in pairs, so it cannot beat the Ω(n log n) bound.
  3. With base b and d-digit keys its running time is Θ(d(n + b)).
  4. When every key is equal, LSD radix sort still performs all d passes.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. LSD radix sort is a sequence of stable passes, one per digit, from the least significant digit to the most significant.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Each pass is stable and the passes run from the least significant digit upwards, so keys that agree on all digits keep their original relative order. TRUE.
  2. (B) Radix sort never compares two keys; it distributes them into buckets by digit. FALSE - it is a non-comparison sort and is not bound by Ω(n log n).
  3. (C) d passes, each a stable counting sort over b + 1 buckets on n keys, cost Θ(d(n + b)). TRUE.
  4. (D) The pass count depends only on the number of digits, not on the values, so equal keys still go through d passes. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming that because radix sort is 'linear' it must be a comparison sort with a loophole, or that it can stop early when the data look sorted. Neither is true: the pass count is fixed by the key width.

ALG-U2-Q38NATmedium

LSD radix sort with base 16 is applied to 32-bit unsigned integers. How many passes does it need? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. Each base-16 pass consumes four bits, so the number of passes is the bit width divided by the digits' width in bits.

Step by step.

  1. A base-16 digit encodes log₂ 16 = 4 bits.
  2. A 32-bit key therefore has 32 / 4 = 8 digits in base 16.
  3. LSD radix sort performs one pass per digit.
  4. So the pass count is 8, and each pass uses a count array of 17 slots (0..15 plus the prefix-sum slot).

Answer: 8.

Common misconception. Answering 16 by using the number of symbols in the base rather than the number of bits per symbol, or 32 by forgetting that each pass handles four bits at once.

Selection & k-th element 12
ALG-U2-Q14MCQeasy

Which method finds the k-th smallest of n distinct keys in Θ(n) time in the worst case, for any k?

  1. Sorting the keys and reading position k.
  2. The median-of-medians selection algorithm.
  3. Building a max-heap and performing k extractions.
  4. Binary searching over the key range and counting keys below each candidate value.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Selection is easier than sorting. The median-of-medians algorithm picks a pivot that is guaranteed to discard a constant fraction of the keys, which gives the recurrence T(n) ≤ T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + Θ(n) = Θ(n).

Step by step.

  1. Group the n keys into ⌊n/5⌋ blocks of five and take the median of each block by sorting five keys: Θ(n) work in total.
  2. Recursively take the median of those block medians; that pivot has at least 3n/10 keys below it and at least 3n/10 above it.
  3. Recurse on one side only, of size at most 7n/10.
  4. Since 1/5 + 7/10 = 9/10 < 1, the recurrence solves to Θ(n).

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Concluding that selection must cost Θ(n log n) because sorting does. Selection is strictly easier: it answers one question about the order, not all of them.

ALG-U2-Q15MCQmedium

Randomized quickselect is used to find the median of n distinct keys. Which statement about its running time is TRUE?

  1. Θ(n) expected, Θ(n²) worst case.
  2. Θ(n log n) expected, Θ(n log n) worst case.
  3. Θ(n) expected, Θ(n) worst case.
  4. Θ(log n) expected, Θ(n) worst case.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Quickselect partitions once and recurses into the one side that contains the k-th element. The expected sum of the subproblem sizes is n + n/2 + n/4 + … = 2n, which is Θ(n); the worst case is still the degenerate chain of partitions, Θ(n²).

Step by step.

  1. One partition of a subarray of size m costs Θ(m).
  2. With a random pivot the expected size of the surviving side is at most 3m/4, so the expected total work satisfies E(n) ≤ n + E(3n/4) = Θ(n).
  3. If every pivot is extreme, the sizes fall by one each time and the total is Θ(n²).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Assuming that "select instead of sort" automatically means worst-case linear. Only the median-of-medians pivot gives that guarantee.

ALG-U2-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about selection algorithms are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Quickselect's expected Θ(n) time follows from the expected subproblem sizes forming a decreasing geometric series.
  2. The median-of-medians algorithm attains Θ(n) in the worst case by using a pivot that is guaranteed to lie between the 30th and 70th percentile.
  3. Sorting first and indexing costs Θ(n log n), which is asymptotically worse than the Θ(n) worst case of median-of-medians.
  4. Any comparison-based algorithm that finds the k-th smallest of n distinct keys needs Ω(n log n) comparisons in the worst case.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Selection is a weaker problem than sorting, so it has a weaker lower bound: Ω(n), not Ω(n log n). The two ways to reach Θ(n) are randomisation (expected) and a guaranteed-fraction pivot (worst case).

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. With a random pivot the surviving side has expected size at most 3m/4, so the expected work is n + (3/4)n + (3/4)²n + … = 4n.
  2. (B) True. Each block of five contributes three keys on each side of its median, so the median of medians has at least 3n/10 keys below and at least 3n/10 above it.
  3. (C) True. Θ(n log n) dominates Θ(n).
  4. (D) False. Median-of-medians finds the k-th smallest in Θ(n) comparisons, so the lower bound is Ω(n).

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Quoting Ω(n log n) for selection. That bound is for producing the whole sorted order; a single order statistic costs only Ω(n).

ALG-U2-Q17NATeasy

An array holds 10 distinct keys. Both the maximum and the minimum must be determined. What is the minimum number of comparisons that suffices in the worst case? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 13

Detailed solution

Concept. Finding the maximum alone needs n − 1 comparisons, and doing the two searches independently costs 2n − 2. Comparing pairs first makes each key lose at most once as a maximum candidate and win at most once as a minimum candidate, which brings the total down to ⌈3n/2⌉ − 2.

Step by step.

  1. Pair the 10 keys into 5 pairs and compare within each pair: 5 comparisons. Call the larger element the pair maximum and the smaller the pair minimum.
  2. The overall maximum is one of the 5 pair maxima, so 4 more comparisons find it.
  3. The overall minimum is one of the 5 pair minima, so 4 more comparisons find it.
  4. Total = 5 + 4 + 4 = 13, which equals ⌈3n/2⌉ − 2 = 15 − 2.

Answer: 13

Common misconception. Answering 18 = 2n − 2 by running two independent scans, which wastes the information each comparison already gives about both extremes.

ALG-U2-Q18MSQhard

Which of the following statements about linear-time selection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. With blocks of five, the recurrence T(n) ≤ T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + O(n) solves to Θ(n) because 1/5 + 7/10 < 1.
  2. Grouping into blocks of three instead of five still gives a linear-time algorithm, because the fraction of keys discarded at each step stays positive.
  3. Quickselect with a random pivot is Θ(n) in expectation even though its worst case is Θ(n²).
  4. Every comparison-based selection algorithm needs Ω(n) comparisons in the worst case, since a key that is never examined could be the answer.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The block size controls how much the pivot guarantees. Blocks of five discard 3n/10 on each side, giving the 9/10 recurrence that solves to linear time. Blocks of three discard only n/3, and the recurrence then sums to Θ(n log n).

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. The fractions 1/5 (the recursive median-of-medians call) and 7/10 (the surviving side) add to 9/10 < 1, so the level costs form a decreasing geometric series.
  2. (B) False. With blocks of three the pivot only guarantees n/3 on each side, so the recurrence is T(n) ≤ T(n/3) + T(2n/3) + O(n), and 1/3 + 2/3 = 1 makes the sum Θ(n log n).
  3. (C) True. Randomisation gives a geometric expected decrease in subproblem size while leaving the adversarial worst case intact.
  4. (D) True. An unexamined key could be the k-th smallest, so every key must be touched at least once.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Thinking that any constant block size works. The guarantee needs a block size of at least five, because only then is the recursive fraction sum below 1.

ALG-U2-Q19NAThard

Sixteen distinct keys are stored in an array. Both the largest and the second largest key must be determined. What is the minimum number of comparisons needed in the worst case? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 18

Detailed solution

Concept. Finding the maximum costs n − 1 comparisons, and only the keys that lost directly to the winner can be second largest. A tournament makes each key lose at most once, so the winner has beaten exactly ⌈log₂n⌉ opponents, and the second largest is the maximum of those.

Step by step.

  1. Run a knockout tournament on 16 keys: 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 15 comparisons identify the largest key.
  2. The largest key played exactly 4 matches, one per round, so exactly 4 keys lost directly to it.
  3. The second largest must be among those 4 losers, because every other key lost to some key that is itself smaller than the maximum.
  4. Finding the maximum of 4 keys costs 3 comparisons. Total = 15 + 3 = 18, that is n + ⌈log₂n⌉ − 2.

Answer: 18

Common misconception. Answering 16 + 15 = 31 by finding the maximum and then rescanning the array, which redoes comparisons that the tournament already made.

ALG-U2-Q39MCQeasy

Quickselect looks for the 3rd smallest key of 100 distinct keys. Its first partition returns a pivot that is the 40th smallest. Which part does the algorithm recurse into?

  1. The part holding the 39 keys smaller than the pivot.
  2. The part holding the 60 keys larger than the pivot.
  3. Both parts, because both are still needed.
  4. Neither; the answer is the pivot itself.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A partition tells you the exact rank of the pivot, so it also tells you on which side of it the wanted rank must lie.

Step by step.

  1. The pivot is the 40th smallest, so the 39 keys to its left are the 1st to 39th smallest.
  2. The target rank 3 is among them, so the answer lies in the left part.
  3. The right part holds ranks 41 to 100 and cannot contain rank 3, so it is discarded.
  4. Only one side is recursed into, which is what makes quickselect Θ(n) in expectation rather than Θ(n log n).

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Recursing into both parts, which turns quickselect back into quicksort. The single-sided recursion is the whole reason selection is cheaper than sorting.

ALG-U2-Q40MCQeasy

Finding the maximum of n distinct keys needs at least n − 1 comparisons. Which argument proves this?

  1. The maximum must be compared with every other key, so n comparisons are needed.
  2. Sorting the array first is necessary, which costs Ω(n log n).
  3. The maximum can be found only by a tournament of depth log₂ n.
  4. Every key except the maximum must lose at least one comparison, and one comparison produces at most one loser.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. An adversary argument: a key that has never lost a comparison could still be the maximum, so the algorithm cannot stop before n − 1 keys have lost.

Step by step.

  1. A comparison between two keys makes exactly one of them a loser.
  2. If some key other than the true maximum has never lost, then swapping it with the maximum gives an input consistent with every comparison seen so far but with a different answer.
  3. So an algorithm that has performed fewer than n − 1 comparisons cannot be correct.
  4. n − 1 comparisons suffice, by scanning and keeping the current maximum.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Option (A) is the intuitive but wrong count: the maximum is not compared with itself, so n − 1 comparisons are enough, not n.

ALG-U2-Q41MCQeasy

Which is the correct lower bound for finding the median of n distinct keys?

  1. Ω(n log n), because the median is a special case of sorting.
  2. Ω(log n), because a binary search over the ranks suffices.
  3. Ω(n), because every key must be inspected at least once, and Ω(n) is achievable.
  4. Ω(1), because the median of a sorted array sits at a fixed index.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A lower bound applies to a problem, not to the method used to solve it: selection is strictly weaker than sorting, so sorting's bound does not transfer.

Step by step.

  1. Any correct algorithm must read every key, since an unread key could be the median; that gives the Ω(n) bound.
  2. Median-of-medians finds the median in Θ(n) worst-case time, so the bound is tight.
  3. Sorting is one way to find the median, but it is not the only way, and a harder problem's bound does not constrain an easier one.
  4. The Ω(n log n) bound applies to producing the whole sorted order, which selection does not need.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that because the median can be found by sorting, the sorting bound must apply. Reductions give upper bounds on difficulty in one direction only.

ALG-U2-Q42MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the median-of-medians (BFPRT) selection algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Grouping into blocks of 3 keeps the same guarantee, because a constant block size is all that matters.
  2. With groups of 5 it guarantees that at least 3n/10 − 6 keys are no larger than the chosen pivot and at least 3n/10 − 6 are no smaller.
  3. Its worst-case running time obeys T(n) ≤ T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + Θ(n), which solves to Θ(n).
  4. It chooses its pivot deterministically, without randomisation.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A deterministic pivot is good enough when it is guaranteed to discard a constant fraction, so that the two recursive calls together cover less than the whole array.

Step by step.

  1. (B) Half of the ⌈n/5⌉ block medians are at most the median of medians, and each such median has three block elements at most itself; discounting the pivot's own block and the last, incomplete block leaves at least 3n/10 − 6 keys on each side. TRUE.
  2. (C) One call of size n/5 chooses the pivot, and at most 7n/10 keys remain to be searched; since 1/5 + 7/10 = 9/10 < 1, the total is Θ(n). TRUE.
  3. (A) Blocks of 3 give the recurrence T(n/3) + T(2n/3) + Θ(n), and 1/3 + 2/3 = 1, which solves to Θ(n log n) rather than Θ(n). FALSE - the constant 5 matters, not merely constancy.
  4. (D) The pivot is the median of the block medians, computed recursively; there is no random choice. TRUE.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking any constant block size works. The sum of the two recursion fractions must be strictly below 1; with blocks of 3 it equals 1 and the linear bound collapses.

ALG-U2-Q43NATmedium

The median-of-medians algorithm with groups of 5 processes n = 60 keys. Using the standard guarantee 3n/10 − 6, at least how many keys are guaranteed to be no smaller than the chosen pivot? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 12

Detailed solution

Concept. The pivot of median-of-medians is the median of the block medians, so half the blocks contribute three keys at least as large as it.

Step by step.

  1. The guarantee is 3n/10 − 6 keys on each side of the pivot.
  2. Substitute n = 60: 3 × 60 / 10 = 18.
  3. Subtract 6 to discount the pivot's own block and the incomplete last block: 18 − 6 = 12.
  4. So at least 12 keys are ≥ the pivot and at least 12 are ≤ it, which is what bounds the recursive call at 7n/10.

Answer: 12.

Common misconception. Forgetting the −6 correction and answering 18. The correction accounts for the block containing the pivot and the block that may be short.

ALG-U2-Q44MCQhard

Why does the recurrence T(n) ≤ T(n/5) + T(7n/10) + Θ(n) of median-of-medians solve to Θ(n)?

  1. Because 1/5 + 7/10 = 9/10 < 1, so the work per level forms a geometric series with ratio below 1.
  2. Because T(n/5) is asymptotically negligible beside T(7n/10) and can be dropped.
  3. Because 7n/10 < n for every n, so the recursion must terminate.
  4. Because the pivot is chosen at random, so the expected split is balanced.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. For a recurrence whose recursive subproblem sizes sum to a fraction c < 1 of n, the total work is bounded by a geometric series and stays Θ(n).

Step by step.

  1. Level 1 costs at most cn for a constant c.
  2. Level 2 costs at most c(n/5 + 7n/10) = c(9n/10).
  3. Level k costs at most c(9/10)^(k−1) n.
  4. Summing the geometric series gives cn / (1 − 9/10) = 10cn = Θ(n).
  5. The recursion depth is log₁₀/₉ n = Θ(log n), but each level is smaller by a constant factor, so the depth does not add a log factor to the total.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (C) is the usual confusion: termination only gives an upper bound of n levels times Θ(n) per level, that is Θ(n²), or Θ(n log n) if the levels shrink slowly. The constant ratio below 1 is what turns it linear.

Inversion counting 12
ALG-U2-Q20MCQeasy

An array of 6 distinct keys is stored in strictly decreasing order. How many inversions does it have?

  1. 6
  2. 15
  3. 21
  4. 30
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An inversion is a pair of indices i < j with a[i] > a[j]. In a decreasing array every pair is inverted, so the count is the number of pairs, C(n,2) = n(n − 1)/2.

Step by step.

  1. Every one of the C(6,2) pairs of positions has the larger key first.
  2. C(6,2) = 6 × 5 / 2 = 15.
  3. So the array has 15 inversions, the maximum possible for 6 distinct keys.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Counting ordered pairs instead of index pairs, which doubles the answer.

ALG-U2-Q21MCQeasy

Insertion sort is run on an array of n keys that contains exactly I inversions. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. It performs exactly I adjacent exchanges and runs in Θ(n + I) time.
  2. It runs in Θ(n log n) time regardless of I.
  3. It performs exactly I key comparisons in total.
  4. It cannot be used to count inversions, because it destroys them as it runs.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Insertion sort moves a key left by swapping it with its immediate predecessor, and each swap removes exactly one inversion. The number of swaps is therefore exactly the inversion count, and the work is one pass over n keys plus one swap per inversion.

Step by step.

  1. Each inner-loop exchange swaps two adjacent keys that are out of order, which removes exactly one inversion and creates none.
  2. The process stops when no inversion remains, so exactly I exchanges are performed.
  3. The outer loop still visits all n keys, so the time is Θ(n + I).

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Concluding that because insertion sort is Θ(n²) in the worst case, it is useless for counting. It is exactly linear in the number of inversions, which is the worst case n(n − 1)/2.

ALG-U2-Q22MCQmedium

In the merge-sort based inversion count, the left and right runs are being merged and the current key of the right run is smaller than the current key of the left run. How many new inversions are counted at that moment?

  1. Exactly one.
  2. As many as the number of keys still remaining in the left run, including the current one.
  3. As many as the number of keys still remaining in the right run.
  4. As many as the number of keys already written to the merged output.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Within each run the keys are already sorted, so an inversion can only pair a left-run key with a right-run key. When the current right key r is smaller than the current left key, r is smaller than every key still in the left run, and all of them precede r in the original array.

Step by step.

  1. Both runs are sorted, so no inversion lies inside either run; only cross pairs matter.
  2. Let L[i..] be the remaining left run and R[j] the current right key, with R[j] < L[i].
  3. Since the left run is sorted, R[j] < L[i] ≤ L[i+1] ≤ …, so R[j] forms an inversion with each of them.
  4. The count added is therefore the number of keys left in the left run, that is len(L) − i.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Adding one inversion per comparison. The merge step must add a whole block of inversions, which is what makes the counting Θ(n log n) rather than Θ(n²).

ALG-U2-Q23MCQmedium

A permutation of n distinct keys is drawn uniformly at random. What is the expected number of inversions?

  1. n(n − 1)/2
  2. n(n − 1)/4
  3. n²/2
  4. (n − 1)/2
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Use linearity of expectation over pairs. For any fixed pair of positions, the two keys are equally likely to appear in either order, so the pair is inverted with probability 1/2, independently of all other pairs for the purpose of the sum.

Step by step.

  1. There are C(n,2) = n(n − 1)/2 pairs of positions.
  2. For a uniformly random permutation each pair is ordered one way or the other with probability 1/2, so the indicator of that pair being an inversion has expectation 1/2.
  3. By linearity of expectation the expected total is C(n,2) × 1/2 = n(n − 1)/4.
  4. For n = 4 the formula gives 4·3/4 = 3, and the 24 permutations of four keys do average exactly three inversions.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Assuming the expectation must be a whole number. Expectations need not be integers; n(n − 1)/4 is 1.5 for n = 4.

ALG-U2-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about inversions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The maximum possible number of inversions in an array of n distinct keys is n(n − 1)/2.
  2. Two different permutations of the same n distinct keys can have the same number of inversions.
  3. If an array has exactly one inversion, a single adjacent exchange sorts it.
  4. The number of inversions of a permutation equals the minimum number of arbitrary element exchanges needed to sort it.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Inversions measure disorder in the adjacent-swap metric. Each adjacent exchange changes the count by exactly one, which is why inversions equal the adjacent-transposition distance. Exchanging arbitrary pairs is a different metric, governed by cycle structure.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. There are C(n,2) pairs, and the decreasing order inverts every one of them.
  2. (B) True. For three keys, [2,3,1] and [3,1,2] both have two inversions.
  3. (C) True. Exactly one inversion means exactly one adjacent pair is out of order, and swapping it sorts the array.
  4. (D) False. The minimum number of arbitrary exchanges is n − (number of cycles), for example 1 for [2,3,1], which has two inversions.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Treating every measure of disorder as the inversion count. Inversions count adjacent swaps only.

ALG-U2-Q25NAThard

How many inversions does the array [3, 1, 2, 3, 1, 2] contain, where an inversion is a pair of indices i < j with a[i] > a[j]? Equal keys do not form an inversion. (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 7

Detailed solution

Concept. With duplicates, an inversion is still a strict inequality on values but a strict inequality on positions. Equal keys never count, so the array is not simply "disordered everywhere".

Step by step.

  1. Count per position, looking only to the right. Position 1 (value 3): smaller keys to its right are 1, 2, 1, 2, giving 4.
  2. Position 2 (value 1): nothing to its right is smaller, giving 0.
  3. Position 3 (value 2): the 1 at position 5 is smaller, giving 1.
  4. Position 4 (value 3): 1 and 2 are smaller, giving 2. Position 5 (value 1): 0. Position 6: 0.
  5. Total = 4 + 0 + 1 + 2 + 0 + 0 = 7.

Answer: 7

Common misconception. Counting the equal 3s and the equal 1s as inversions, which adds 1 + 1 = 2 and gives 9. An inversion needs a strictly greater value on the left.

ALG-U2-Q45MCQeasy

A permutation of n distinct keys has inversion count I. Its reverse - the same keys read backwards - has inversion count I′. What is I + I′?

  1. n(n − 1)/4, for every permutation
  2. n(n − 1)/2, for every permutation
  3. n², for every permutation
  4. It depends on the permutation
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Reversing an array reverses the left-to-right order of every pair, so exactly one of the two orders of each pair is an inversion.

Step by step.

  1. Take any two positions p < q in the original array, holding values x and y.
  2. In the reversed array the same two values appear in the opposite order: y before x.
  3. So the pair {x, y} contributes an inversion in exactly one of the two arrays.
  4. There are n(n − 1)/2 pairs, hence I + I′ = n(n − 1)/2.
  5. Check with n = 3 and a = [1, 2, 3]: I = 0, the reverse is [3, 2, 1] with I′ = 3, and 3 = 3 × 2 / 2.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming the reverse has the same inversion count, or that the sum depends on the permutation. The identity is exact for every permutation.

ALG-U2-Q46MCQeasy

Two adjacent elements of an array are exchanged. What happens to the inversion count?

  1. It may change by more than 1.
  2. It never changes, because the multiset of keys is unchanged.
  3. It changes by exactly 1, up or down, and by nothing else.
  4. It changes by exactly n.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. An adjacent exchange only reorders one pair; every other pair keeps its relative order.

Step by step.

  1. Let the exchanged neighbours hold x at position k and y at position k+1, with x < y before the exchange.
  2. Any other element is either before both or after both, so its relative order with x and with y is unchanged.
  3. The only pair whose order flips is (x, y), which was not an inversion and becomes one.
  4. Hence the count rises by exactly 1; exchanging them back lowers it by exactly 1.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Thinking a swap can fix several inversions at once. Only a non-adjacent exchange can, which is why bubble sort and insertion sort need exactly I adjacent exchanges while a general swap can fix more.

ALG-U2-Q47MCQmedium

An array a[1..n] is a permutation. Let I be its inversion count and let C be the number of cycles of the permutation, a fixed point counting as a cycle. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. I = n − C for every permutation.
  2. Both I and n − C equal the minimum number of adjacent exchanges.
  3. I ≤ n − C for every permutation.
  4. I is the minimum number of adjacent exchanges needed to sort a, and n − C is the minimum number of arbitrary exchanges needed to sort it.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two different distance measures: adjacent exchanges (inversions) and exchanges of any two positions (cycle decomposition).

Step by step.

  1. An adjacent exchange changes the inversion count by exactly 1, and insertion sort achieves the sort with exactly I of them, so I is the adjacent-exchange distance.
  2. An exchange of any two positions can join two cycles or split one, so each exchange reduces the cycle count by at most 1.
  3. A sorted array has n cycles (n fixed points), so at least n − C exchanges are needed, and n − C suffice by fixing one cycle at a time.
  4. The two quantities differ in general: for a = [2, 3, 1] we get I = 2 but C = 1, so n − C = 2; for a = [3, 2, 1] we get I = 3 while C = 2 and n − C = 1.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Equating the two distances. The last example separates them: [3, 2, 1] needs three adjacent exchanges but only one general exchange.

ALG-U2-Q48MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about counting inversions with a modified merge sort are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every inversion is counted exactly once, at the level of the recursion where the two keys first fall into different halves.
  2. Whenever the current key of the right run is taken, the number of keys still left in the left run is added to the count.
  3. The algorithm needs Θ(n²) time, because an array of n keys has Θ(n²) pairs.
  4. Equal keys never contribute, so the count stays well defined when the array has duplicates.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The merge step sees two sorted runs and can count, in one step, all pairs that cross the split.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A pair is either inside one half, where the recursive calls count it, or split across the two halves, where this merge counts it. The two cases partition all pairs. TRUE.
  2. (B) If the right key is smaller than the left key at position i, then every key from i to the end of the left run is larger, so the whole tail is a set of inversions with this right key. TRUE.
  3. (C) The counting adds Θ(1) work to each merge comparison, so the total remains Θ(n log n). FALSE.
  4. (D) An inversion needs a strictly larger key on the left; equal keys are never counted, so duplicates are handled consistently. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Counting inversions twice, once in the merge and once in the recursion, or adding 1 instead of the size of the remaining left run.

ALG-U2-Q49NAThard

How many inversions does the array [5, 2, 4, 1, 3, 2, 6, 1] contain, where an inversion is a pair of indices i < j with a[i] > a[j]? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 16

Detailed solution

Concept. Count per position, looking only to the right, and remember that equal keys never form an inversion.

Step by step.

  1. Position 1 (value 5): smaller to its right are 2, 4, 1, 3, 2, 1 - six inversions.
  2. Position 2 (value 2): the two 1s - two inversions.
  3. Position 3 (value 4): 1, 3, 2, 1 - four inversions.
  4. Position 4 (value 1): nothing smaller - zero.
  5. Position 5 (value 3): 2 and 1 - two inversions.
  6. Position 6 (value 2): the 1 - one inversion. Position 7 (value 6): the 1 - one inversion. Position 8 (value 1): zero.
  7. Total 6 + 2 + 4 + 0 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 16.

Answer: 16.

Common misconception. Counting the two 2s against each other, or counting the two 1s against each other. An inversion needs a strictly greater key on the left, so equal values never contribute - the duplicate 1s and 2s are where most miscounts happen.

ALG-U2-Q50MCQhard

For a permutation a[1..n] define the inversion vector d_i = #{ j > i : a[j] < a[i] }. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. 0 ≤ d_i ≤ n − i for each i, the sum of the d_i is the inversion count, and the map from permutations to inversion vectors is a bijection.
  2. 0 ≤ d_i ≤ n − 1 for each i, and the map from permutations to inversion vectors is a bijection.
  3. The sum of the d_i equals n(n − 1)/2 for every permutation.
  4. The inversion vector determines only the parity of the permutation.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The inversion vector (Lehmer code) records, for each position, how many smaller keys lie to its right; its ranges are independent, which makes it a bijection.

Step by step.

  1. d_i counts keys to the right of position i, and there are only n − i of them, so 0 ≤ d_i ≤ n − i.
  2. Summing d_i over all i counts every inversion exactly once, at its left endpoint.
  3. The number of possible vectors is the product over i of (n − i + 1) = n!, which equals the number of permutations.
  4. The reconstruction is constructive - insert keys from the largest down, placing the key of rank k at the position fixed by its d value - so distinct permutations give distinct vectors.
  5. The sum is n(n−1)/2 only for the decreasing permutation, not for every one, and the vector determines the whole permutation, not just its parity.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) replaces the position-dependent bound n − i by the uniform n − 1, which would allow n^n vectors instead of n! and destroy the bijection.

Unit 3 — Greedy Algorithms · 50 solved questions (2026-10-29)
Fractional knapsack 13
ALG-U3-Q01MCQeasy

In the fractional knapsack problem the items are divisible, so any fraction of an item may be taken. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Taking items in decreasing order of value, and then the remainder of the next item, is optimal.
  2. Taking items in decreasing order of value per unit weight, and then the remainder of the next item, is optimal; the same rule can be suboptimal for 0/1 knapsack.
  3. Taking items in increasing order of weight is optimal, and the same rule is also optimal for 0/1 knapsack.
  4. Fractional knapsack is NP-hard, so no polynomial-time algorithm is known for it.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The exchange rate between weight and value is the ratio v_i/w_i. When weight is divisible, the only thing that matters is which item gives the most value per unit of the scarce resource, so the greedy rule sorts by ratio.

Step by step.

  1. Fractional knapsack is a linear program whose feasible region is a simplex-like polytope; its extreme points take all items in ratio order and at most one item fractionally.
  2. Hence sorting by decreasing v_i/w_i and filling the knapsack in that order is optimal (option B).
  3. Sorting by value alone ignores weight: a light item of moderate value can beat a heavy item of slightly larger value, so option A fails.
  4. Sorting by weight ignores value altogether, and for 0/1 knapsack even the ratio rule fails — for example capacity 10 with items (6, 30), (5, 20), (5, 20): ratio-greedy returns 30 while taking both 5-weight items gives 40.
  5. Fractional knapsack is solved in O(n log n) by the ratio rule, so it is not NP-hard; 0/1 knapsack is the NP-hard relative.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that the ratio rule is optimal for both variants. Divisibility is exactly what makes the exchange argument work; with indivisible items the leftover capacity can make the greedy answer worse than optimal.

ALG-U3-Q02MCQeasy

A knapsack has capacity 15. Three items are available, given as (weight, value): (10, 60), (5, 25) and (10, 40). Items may be split. What value does the ratio-greedy algorithm obtain?

  1. 65
  2. 80
  3. 85
  4. 100
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Ratio-greedy orders items by value per unit weight, takes whole items while they fit and then the largest fraction of the next item that still fits.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 60/10 = 6, 25/5 = 5, 40/10 = 4.
  2. Order: item 1 (ratio 6), item 2 (ratio 5), item 3 (ratio 4).
  3. Take item 1 whole: weight 10, value 60, capacity left 5.
  4. Take item 2 whole: weight 5, value 25, capacity left 0.
  5. Total value = 60 + 25 = 85; item 3 contributes nothing.

Answer: 85 — option (C).

Common misconception. Adding the two largest values, 60 + 40 = 100, and stopping because the capacity is exhausted. Those two items weigh 10 + 10 = 20, more than the capacity 15, so the answer is infeasible. Check the weight, not the value, while filling.

ALG-U3-Q03MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about fractional knapsack and its greedy algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The greedy algorithm that considers items in decreasing order of value per unit weight is optimal.
  2. In an optimal solution produced by the ratio rule, at most one item is taken fractionally.
  3. The greedy algorithm is asymptotically slower than the pseudo-polynomial dynamic program for 0/1 knapsack, so the dynamic program is preferred for large capacities.
  4. If every item has the same weight, sorting by decreasing value gives an optimal solution.
  5. The linear-programming relaxation of fractional knapsack always has an integral optimal solution.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The ratio rule fills the knapsack in one pass over the ratio-sorted order, so all items before the break point are whole and all after it are zero — only the break item can be partial.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the optimality theorem for fractional knapsack.
  2. (B) holds because a single item straddles the capacity boundary; every earlier item fits entirely and every later item does not fit at all.
  3. (C) is false: the ratio rule is O(n log n), while the 0/1 dynamic program is O(nC), which is pseudo-polynomial and slower for large C.
  4. (D) is true: with equal weights, v_i/w_i = v_i/w is a positive multiple of v_i, so ratio order is value order and the same argument applies.
  5. (E) is false: the LP optimum is usually fractional — that is precisely what allows splitting — and is generally not integral.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Confusing the LP relaxation with the integer problem. The relaxation is exactly the fractional problem, and its optimum normally uses a fraction of one item.

ALG-U3-Q04NATmedium

A knapsack has capacity 20. The available items, given as (weight, value), are (8, 40), (6, 36), (5, 25), (4, 16) and (10, 50). Items are divisible. What is the maximum total value that can be carried? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 106

Detailed solution

Concept. Sort by value per unit weight, take whole items in that order and split the first item that does not fit completely.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 40/8 = 5, 36/6 = 6, 25/5 = 5, 16/4 = 4, 50/10 = 5.
  2. Order: (6, 36) ratio 6, then the ratio-5 items (8, 40), (5, 25), (10, 50) in any order, then (4, 16) ratio 4.
  3. Take (6, 36): weight 6, value 36, capacity left 14.
  4. Take (8, 40): weight 14, value 76, capacity left 6.
  5. Take (5, 25): weight 19, value 101, capacity left 1.
  6. Take 1 of the 10 units of (10, 50): value 50 × (1/10) = 5, giving 106 with the knapsack exactly full.

Answer: 106.

Common misconception. Ignoring the fractional tail and answering 101, the value of the whole items only. In this instance starting from the highest-value item (10, 50) also happens to reach 106, which can hide a wrong method: value order is not optimal in general, ratio order is.

ALG-U3-Q05MCQmedium

A 0/1 knapsack has capacity 10 and three items given as (weight, value): (6, 30), (5, 20), (5, 20). The ratio-greedy algorithm takes items in decreasing order of value per unit weight and never splits an item. What value does it return, and what is the optimum?

  1. Greedy 30, optimum 40
  2. Greedy 30, optimum 45
  3. Greedy 40, optimum 40
  4. Greedy 46, optimum 40
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. On 0/1 knapsack the ratio rule can be forced to waste capacity, because the item that fills the remainder may be indivisible.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 30/6 = 5, and 20/5 = 4 for each of the other two items.
  2. Greedy takes (6, 30): weight 6, value 30, capacity left 4.
  3. Neither remaining item of weight 5 fits, so greedy stops at value 30.
  4. The optimal solution takes both (5, 20) items: weight 10, value 40.
  5. So greedy gives 30 and the optimum is 40 — a gap of 10 on a three-item instance.

Answer: Greedy 30, optimum 40 — option (A).

Common misconception. Answering 46 by allowing a split of the second item. That is the fractional answer (30 + 20 × 4/5 = 46) and is not available in 0/1 knapsack.

ALG-U3-Q06MCQhard

The optimality of ratio-greedy for fractional knapsack is proved by an exchange argument. Which one of the following is the essential step of that proof?

  1. Sort the items by weight and show that any solution which uses a heavier item before a lighter one can be improved.
  2. Take an optimal solution that agrees with greedy on the longest possible prefix; if it differs at the next item x, move weight from the lowest-ratio item that the solution uses to x, which keeps the total weight and cannot decrease the value, so an optimal solution agreeing on one more item exists.
  3. Show by induction on the capacity that the greedy first choice is the unique optimal first choice.
  4. Show that the linear-programming relaxation of fractional knapsack has an integral optimum, so greedy and the optimum coincide.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An exchange argument replaces part of an optimal solution by the greedy choice without losing optimality, so that an optimal solution containing the greedy choice exists; induction then finishes the proof.

Step by step.

  1. Let G be the greedy solution and O an optimal solution agreeing with G on the longest prefix of items.
  2. At the first disagreement the greedy item x has ratio at least that of every item still used by O after the prefix, because greedy took items in non-increasing ratio order.
  3. Move weight δ from the lowest-ratio item y that O still uses to x, where δ is the amount of x that greedy takes and that O does not.
  4. Total weight is unchanged, so feasibility is preserved; the change in value is δ (v_x/w_x - v_y/w_y) ≥ 0, so O does not become worse.
  5. Repeating the exchange gives an optimal solution equal to G, so G is optimal.
  6. Option (A) sorts by weight, which is irrelevant; option (C) is false because the greedy first choice need not be unique; option (D) is false because the LP optimum is generally fractional.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Thinking the greedy choice must be unique. Ties in ratio give several optimal first choices, and the proof only needs one optimal solution containing the greedy choice, not uniqueness.

ALG-U3-Q07NATeasy

An item weighs 12 kg and is worth ₹180 in total. In the fractional-knapsack greedy rule, what is this item's value per unit weight? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 15

Detailed solution

Concept. The greedy key of an item is its value density v_i/w_i — value per single unit of weight.

Step by step.

  1. Value = 180, weight = 12.
  2. Density = 180 / 12 = 15 value units per kg.
  3. This density, not the total value 180, is what the greedy sort compares between items.

Answer: 15.

Common misconception. Sorting by total value instead of density. A heavy item can have a large total value and still be a poor use of capacity.

ALG-U3-Q26MCQeasy

In the fractional knapsack problem the items are divisible. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The problem is solved optimally in O(n log n) time by sorting the items on value per unit weight.
  2. The problem needs dynamic programming, because a greedy choice may have to be undone later.
  3. The problem is NP-hard, exactly like 0/1 knapsack.
  4. The greedy algorithm may end up taking three or more items fractionally.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Fractional knapsack is a continuous problem: the exchange rate between weight and value is the ratio v_i/w_i, and only that ratio decides which item deserves the scarce capacity.

Step by step.

  1. Sorting on the ratio costs O(n log n) and filling the knapsack afterwards costs O(n), so the whole algorithm is O(n log n).
  2. At most one item is split, because the first item that does not fit whole exhausts whatever capacity is left.
  3. Divisibility is exactly what makes the exchange argument valid, so no dynamic program and no backtracking is needed.
  4. 0/1 knapsack is the NP-hard relative; the fractional version is polynomial and its greedy solution is optimal.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Options C and D are the usual slips: confusing the fractional problem with its NP-hard 0/1 cousin, and imagining that several items get cut. The split happens at most once, at the boundary item.

ALG-U3-Q27NATeasy

A knapsack has capacity 18. The available items, given as (weight, value), are (6, 42), (9, 54), (4, 20) and (5, 25). Items are divisible. What is the maximum total value that can be carried? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 111

Detailed solution

Concept. Sort by value per unit weight in decreasing order, take whole items while they fit, then split the first item that does not fit.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 42/6 = 7, 54/9 = 6, 20/4 = 5, 25/5 = 5.
  2. Order: (6, 42) with ratio 7, then (9, 54) with ratio 6, then the two ratio-5 items in either order.
  3. Take (6, 42): weight 6, value 42, capacity left 12.
  4. Take (9, 54): weight 15, value 96, capacity left 3.
  5. Take 3 of the 4 units of (4, 20): value 20 × (3/4) = 15.
  6. Total value 96 + 15 = 111, with the knapsack exactly full.

Answer: 111.

Common misconception. Answering 96 by taking only the whole items, or splitting (5, 25) first because it looks lighter. The ratio order — not the weight and not the total value — decides.

ALG-U3-Q28MCQmedium

A knapsack has capacity 12 and the divisible items (weight, value) are (5, 30), (4, 20), (6, 36) and (3, 15). The maximum total value the ratio-greedy algorithm carries is:

  1. 71
  2. 70
  3. 66
  4. 75
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Take items in decreasing ratio order and split only the boundary item.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 30/5 = 6, 20/4 = 5, 36/6 = 6, 15/3 = 5.
  2. The two ratio-6 items come first: (5, 30) and (6, 36).
  3. Take (5, 30): weight 5, value 30, capacity left 7.
  4. Take (6, 36): weight 11, value 66, capacity left 1.
  5. Take 1 unit of a ratio-5 item: value 5. Total 66 + 5 = 71.

Answer: 71 (option A).

Common misconception. Answering 66 by stopping at the whole items, or 75 by using a capacity of 15. Check the fractional tail separately: 1 unit at ratio 5.

ALG-U3-Q29MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the fractional knapsack greedy algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If every item has the same value per unit weight, then every order of the items gives an optimal solution.
  2. If the total weight of all the items is at least the capacity, the greedy knapsack ends up completely full.
  3. The algorithm takes a fraction of an item exactly when that item does not fit whole into the remaining capacity.
  4. The algorithm may skip a high-ratio item in order to take two lower-ratio items whose total weight is larger.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. With divisible items the objective is linear in the fraction taken, so value per unit weight is the only thing that matters.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: when all ratios are equal, any order fills the capacity with the same total value, so every order is optimal. True.
  2. Option B: as long as the total weight reaches the capacity, the algorithm keeps taking whole items and then splits one, so the knapsack is exactly full. True.
  3. Option C: whole items are taken while they fit; the first item that does not fit is split. True.
  4. Option D: skipping a higher-ratio item for lower-ratio ones strictly lowers the value per unit of capacity, so it can never be optimal. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Option D describes what 0/1 knapsack does — leaving capacity unused — and candidates carry that habit over. With divisible items the knapsack is always filled, so there is no reason to prefer a lower ratio.

ALG-U3-Q30MCQhard

A knapsack has capacity 25 and the divisible items (10, 60), (8, 48), (7, 35), (5, 30) and (12, 72). The maximum total value that can be carried is:

  1. 150
  2. 148
  3. 145
  4. 156
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Ratio order decides; ties in the ratio are harmless, but a lower-ratio item must never be preferred for the leftover capacity.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 60/10 = 6, 48/8 = 6, 35/7 = 5, 30/5 = 6, 72/12 = 6.
  2. Four items tie at ratio 6 and one sits at ratio 5.
  3. Take (10, 60): weight 10, value 60, capacity left 15.
  4. Take (8, 48): weight 18, value 108, capacity left 7.
  5. Take (5, 30): weight 23, value 138, capacity left 2.
  6. Take 2 units of (12, 72) at ratio 6: value 12. Total 138 + 12 = 150, knapsack exactly full.

Answer: 150 (option A).

Common misconception. 148 comes from taking the leftover 2 units out of (7, 35), simply because it is the next item in the list. Its ratio is 5, not 6, so it is the wrong item for the leftover capacity.

ALG-U3-Q31NATmedium

A knapsack has capacity 11 and the divisible items (4, 24), (5, 25), (6, 30) and (2, 10). What is the maximum total value that can be carried? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 59

Detailed solution

Concept. Sort by value per unit weight, fill whole items, split the boundary item.

Step by step.

  1. Ratios: 24/4 = 6, 25/5 = 5, 30/6 = 5, 10/2 = 5.
  2. Take (4, 24): weight 4, value 24, capacity left 7.
  3. Take (5, 25): weight 9, value 49, capacity left 2.
  4. Take 2 units of any ratio-5 item: value 10.
  5. Total 49 + 10 = 59, knapsack exactly full.

Answer: 59.

Common misconception. Answering 54 by stopping at the whole items, or 60 by rounding the capacity up to 12. The fractional tail of 2 units is worth 10, not 5.

Huffman coding 12
ALG-U3-Q08MCQeasy

Huffman's algorithm repeatedly removes the two smallest frequencies, merges them and inserts the sum. With a binary min-heap, what is the running time for n symbols?

  1. O(n)
  2. O(n log n)
  3. O(n²)
  4. O(2ⁿ)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. There are n-1 merges and each merge performs a constant number of heap operations, each costing O( log n).

Step by step.

  1. Build the heap from n frequencies: O(n) with bottom-up heapify.
  2. Perform n-1 merges; each does two extractions and one insertion, i.e. three heap operations of cost O( log n).
  3. Total O(n) + O(n log n) = O(n log n).
  4. A sorted-list implementation gives the same bound; the naive O(n²) scan for the two minima is what the heap replaces.

Answer: O(n log n) — option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming sorting is required first. Sorting is one valid route, but the heap alone already achieves O(n log n) and needs no pre-sorted input.

ALG-U3-Q09MCQmedium

Six symbols have frequencies 5, 9, 12, 13, 16 and 45. For a Huffman tree on these frequencies, what is the total cost — the sum of all merged weights, which equals the weighted external path length?

  1. 210
  2. 220
  3. 224
  4. 232
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each merge adds the merged weight to the total cost, and the sum of merge costs equals Σ_i f_i · ℓ_i, the weighted external path length of the Huffman tree.

Step by step.

  1. Merge 5 and 9 → 14. Cost so far 14. Heap: 12, 13, 14, 16, 45.
  2. Merge 12 and 13 → 25. Cost 39. Heap: 14, 16, 25, 45.
  3. Merge 14 and 16 → 30. Cost 69. Heap: 25, 30, 45.
  4. Merge 25 and 30 → 55. Cost 124. Heap: 45, 55.
  5. Merge 45 and 55 → 100. Cost 224.
  6. Total cost = 14 + 25 + 30 + 55 + 100 = 224.

Answer: 224 — option (C).

Common misconception. Reporting 100, the root weight, or the sum of the original frequencies. The cost is the sum of every merge, because a symbol merged early is paid for again at each level above it.

ALG-U3-Q10MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Huffman coding are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every Huffman code is prefix-free.
  2. For a fixed multiset of symbol frequencies, Huffman's algorithm produces a code of minimum expected codeword length.
  3. For a fixed multiset of symbol frequencies, the Huffman tree is unique.
  4. Two symbols of equal frequency may be exchanged in a Huffman tree without changing the total cost.
  5. Adding one more symbol of positive frequency to the alphabet can never increase the average codeword length.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Huffman codes are built on a full binary tree, so no codeword is a prefix of another, and the greedy merge of the two smallest weights is optimal for the weighted external path length.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: codewords are root-to-leaf paths, and a leaf has no leaf below it.
  2. (B) is the optimality theorem for Huffman coding.
  3. (C) is false: ties produce different but equally optimal trees — for frequencies 1, 1, 2 either pair of equal-frequency symbols may be merged first.
  4. (D) is true: swapping two equal-frequency leaves changes no leaf depth times frequency, so the cost is unchanged.
  5. (E) is false: a new symbol must be given a codeword, which lengthens some existing codewords and increases the average length.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading 'minimum expected length' as 'the code is unique'. Optimality is a property of the cost, not of the tree; Huffman trees are optimal but frequently not unique.

ALG-U3-Q11NATmedium

Five symbols have frequencies 1, 1, 2, 3 and 5. What is the total cost of Huffman's tree on these frequencies, that is, the sum of all merged weights? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 25

Detailed solution

Concept. Sum the weights produced by each merge; the total is the weighted external path length of the Huffman tree.

Step by step.

  1. Merge 1 and 1 → 2. Cost 2. Heap: 2, 2, 3, 5.
  2. Merge 2 and 2 → 4. Cost 6. Heap: 3, 4, 5.
  3. Merge 3 and 4 → 7. Cost 13. Heap: 5, 7.
  4. Merge 5 and 7 → 12. Cost 25.
  5. Depth check on the resulting tree: the 5 sits at depth 1, the 3 at depth 2, the symbol of frequency 2 at depth 3, and the two symbols of frequency 1 at depth 4, so 5·1 + 3·2 + 2·3 + 1·4 + 1·4 = 25, matching the merge sum.

Answer: 25.

Common misconception. Adding the original frequencies (12) instead of the merge costs. Every internal node's weight is charged once, and a symbol merged early is charged again at every level above it.

ALG-U3-Q12MCQhard

For n ≥ 2 symbols with positive frequencies, what is the largest possible length of a Huffman codeword?

  1. ⌈ log_2 n ⌉
  2. n - 1
  3. n
  4. It is unbounded as the frequencies become more skewed.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A Huffman tree is a full binary tree, and in a full binary tree every level except possibly the last contains at least one leaf once the tree is built by successive merges of two smallest weights.

Step by step.

  1. Each merge reduces the number of trees in the forest by one, from n down to 1, so exactly n-1 merges occur and the tree has n leaves and n-1 internal nodes.
  2. Take a deepest leaf at depth d. Each of the d internal nodes on its root-to-leaf path has a second child, and the subtree hanging from that child contains at least one leaf not on the path; these d leaves are distinct, and together with the deepest leaf itself they are d+1 distinct leaves.
  3. Since the tree has only n leaves, d + 1 ≤ n, so d ≤ n - 1.
  4. The bound is attained by Fibonacci-like frequencies such as 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, …, where each merge creates the next weight and the tree degenerates into a path.
  5. The balanced value ⌈ log_2 n ⌉ is only a lower bound on the height, not the maximum, so option (A) is wrong.

Answer: n-1 — option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming Huffman trees are balanced. They are optimal for expected length, not for height, and highly skewed frequencies give very deep trees.

ALG-U3-Q13MCQeasy

In a Huffman tree built for n ≥ 2 symbols, which statement about the internal nodes is TRUE?

  1. Every internal node has exactly two children.
  2. An internal node may have exactly one child when a frequency is zero.
  3. An internal node may have more than two children when several frequencies are equal.
  4. There are no internal nodes when all frequencies are equal.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Huffman's algorithm builds the tree by merging exactly two trees at a time, so every internal node is the parent of exactly two nodes.

Step by step.

  1. Each step removes two roots from the forest and creates one new root with those two as children.
  2. Therefore every internal node has exactly two children — the tree is a full binary tree.
  3. A node with one child would be a redundant merge and would waste a bit in every codeword.
  4. Equal frequencies change which nodes are paired, never the arity.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing Huffman trees with general tries, where a node may have one child. Huffman's merges are always binary.

ALG-U3-Q32MCQeasy

Huffman's algorithm repeatedly merges the two smallest frequencies. Implemented with a binary min-heap, its running time for n symbols is:

  1. O(n log n)
  2. O(n^2)
  3. O(n)
  4. O(2^n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A Huffman tree on n leaves has exactly n − 1 merges, and each merge performs two extractions and one insertion on the heap.

Step by step.

  1. There are n − 1 merges in total.
  2. Each heap operation costs O(log n), and each merge performs three of them.
  3. Total time O(n log n).
  4. If the frequencies arrive already sorted, two FIFO queues replace the heap and the bound improves to O(n).

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option B comes from scanning an unsorted list for the two minima at every step, which is O(n) per merge. The heap, or the two-queue trick on sorted input, removes that factor.

ALG-U3-Q33NATeasy

Four symbols have frequencies 2, 3, 4 and 5. What is the total cost of a Huffman tree on these frequencies — that is, the sum over symbols of frequency × codeword length? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Answer: 28

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of a Huffman tree equals the sum of the weights of all its merges, which is the same as the weighted external path length.

Step by step.

  1. Merge 2 and 3 into 5. Merge cost 5.
  2. The queue now holds 4, 5 and the new 5.
  3. Merge 4 and 5 into 9. Merge cost 9. Queue: 5, 9.
  4. Merge 5 and 9 into 14. Merge cost 14.
  5. Total cost 5 + 9 + 14 = 28.

Answer: 28.

Common misconception. Reporting only the final merge (14) or the sum of the original frequencies (14). The cost accumulates over every merge, not just the last one.

ALG-U3-Q34MCQmedium

Five symbols have frequencies 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5. The total cost of a Huffman tree on these frequencies is:

  1. 33
  2. 32
  3. 34
  4. 31
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every merge inserts its own weight back into the queue, so a newly formed value competes with the original frequencies.

Step by step.

  1. Merge 1 and 2 into 3. Cost 3. Queue: 3, 3, 4, 5.
  2. Merge 3 and 3 into 6. Cost 6. Queue: 4, 5, 6.
  3. Merge 4 and 5 into 9. Cost 9. Queue: 6, 9.
  4. Merge 6 and 9 into 15. Cost 15.
  5. Total 3 + 6 + 9 + 15 = 33.

Answer: 33 (option A).

Common misconception. 32 comes from pairing 4 and 5 too early, or from forgetting to push the merged value back into the queue. After the first merge the value 3 must compete with the original 3, 4 and 5.

ALG-U3-Q35MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Huffman coding are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. For a given frequency distribution, Huffman's algorithm produces a prefix code of minimum expected codeword length.
  2. The Huffman code for a given frequency distribution is unique.
  3. Every full binary tree with n leaves defines a prefix code for n symbols.
