Concept. The optimum is a subset of items whose weights sum to at most 15; on an instance this size the DP row for W = 15 is short enough to compute directly.

Step by step.

  1. (3, 4) + (5, 8) + (7, 11): weight 15, value 23.
  2. (2, 3) + (6, 9) + (7, 11): weight 15, value 23 as well, so the optimum is attained twice.
  3. The best alternatives are 22 — (2, 3) + (5, 8) + (7, 11) at weight 14, and (4, 5) + (5, 8) + (6, 9) at weight 15 — and 21 for (2, 3) + (3, 4) + (4, 5) + (6, 9).
  4. 24 is not attainable: the fractional optimum on this instance is 23.5, and no indivisible subset reaches even that.

Answer: 23 — option (B).

Common misconception. 21 and 22 are the exact-weight and near-exact-weight subsets that look attractive but spend value on low-ratio items. 24 is the fractional bound, which no indivisible subset attains.

All-pairs shortest paths 12
ALG-U4-Q20MCQeasy

Which statement about the Floyd–Warshall algorithm is TRUE?

  1. It runs in Θ(n³) time and is correct with negative edge weights as long as no negative-weight cycle is reachable.
  2. It runs in Θ(n²) time on a dense graph.
  3. It requires every edge weight to be non-negative.
  4. It is a greedy algorithm, so it fails whenever an edge has negative weight.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Floyd–Warshall is a dynamic program over the set of allowed intermediate vertices; it never commits to a locally cheapest edge, so negative edge weights are harmless. What it cannot survive is a negative-weight cycle, because then shortest paths are undefined.

Step by step.

  1. Three nested loops over k (intermediate vertex), i and j give Θ(n³) time on an n-vertex graph.
  2. The recurrence d^(k)[i][j] = min(d^(k−1)[i][j], d^(k−1)[i][k] + d^(k−1)[k][j]) only adds and compares path lengths; negative values are handled correctly.
  3. A negative cycle shows up as some d[i][i] < 0 after the last iteration, which is how the algorithm detects it rather than silently failing.
  4. Dijkstra is the greedy algorithm that requires non-negative weights; Floyd–Warshall is not Dijkstra.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (D) confuses Floyd–Warshall with Dijkstra. The greedy requirement (non-negative weights) belongs to Dijkstra; the DP requirement is the absence of a negative-weight cycle.

ALG-U4-Q21MCQeasy

In the recurrence d^(k)[i][j] = min( d^(k−1)[i][j], d^(k−1)[i][k] + d^(k−1)[k][j] ), what does the superscript k represent?

  1. The path may use intermediate vertices only from the set {1, 2, …, k}.
  2. The path has exactly k edges.
  3. It is the k-th shortest path discovered so far.
  4. Vertex k must be an endpoint of the path.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The DP's third dimension is the set of vertices allowed in the interior of the path. Restricting that set to a growing prefix {1…k} makes the state finite and gives the recurrence its two cases.

Step by step.

  1. d^(0)[i][j] is the direct edge weight (or ∞), because no intermediate vertex is allowed.
  2. Going from k−1 to k, a shortest i→j path either avoids vertex k — value d^(k−1)[i][j] — or passes through it, in which case it splits into a shortest i→k path and a shortest k→j path, both using only {1…k−1} inside.
  3. Taking the minimum of the two cases gives the recurrence.
  4. After k = n, every vertex is allowed as an intermediate, so d^(n)[i][j] is the true shortest-path distance.

Answer: Option (A) — the allowed set of intermediate vertices.

Common misconception. 'Exactly k edges' describes the Bellman–Ford style layering, not Floyd–Warshall. Endpoints are never restricted: only interior vertices are counted in k.

ALG-U4-Q22MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the Floyd–Warshall algorithm are TRUE?

  1. Replacing min by logical OR and + by logical AND in the recurrence computes the transitive closure of the graph.
  2. A negative-weight cycle exists if and only if some d[i][i] < 0 after the final iteration.
  3. The dynamic program is correct only if the graph is strongly connected.
  4. d^(k)[i][j] is the length of a shortest i→j path all of whose intermediate vertices lie in {1, …, k}.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Floyd–Warshall is a semiring algorithm: the same loop structure computes shortest paths over (min, +) and reachability over (OR, AND). Its state is the allowed intermediate set, and its self-distances expose negative cycles.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Over the Boolean semiring the recurrence becomes reach^(k)[i][j] = reach^(k−1)[i][j] OR (reach^(k−1)[i][k] AND reach^(k−1)[k][j]), which is exactly Warshall's transitive-closure algorithm.
  2. (B) TRUE. A negative cycle through i means a strictly negative closed walk i→i exists, so d[i][i] < 0 once all vertices are allowed; conversely a negative d[i][i] certifies such a walk.
  3. (C) FALSE. Disconnected pairs simply keep d[i][j] = ∞; the algorithm is correct on any graph, connected or not.
  4. (D) TRUE. That is the definition of the state; the recurrence builds it by deciding whether vertex k is used as an interior vertex.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (C) is the usual over-restriction: connectivity is needed for finite distances, not for correctness. Candidates also miss (A) because they treat the min/+ form as the only form; the loop is identical, only the semiring changes.

ALG-U4-Q23MCQmedium

A directed graph has vertices A, B, C, D and weighted edges A→B (3), A→C (8), B→C (2), B→D (5), C→D (1), D→A (2). The shortest-path distance from D to B is:

  1. 3
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. All-pairs shortest paths must consider routes that leave the direct neighbourhood. Here no edge D→B exists, so the answer comes from a path through A.

Step by step.

  1. There is no direct edge D→B, so the distance starts at ∞.
  2. D→A costs 2, and A→B costs 3, giving D→A→B = 5.
  3. Alternatives: D→A→C→B is not a path (C has no edge to B); D→A→C→D→A→B repeats vertices and costs more; D→A→B→D→A→B is a cycle, never optimal with positive weights.
  4. So the shortest distance is 5. The final Floyd–Warshall matrix has d[D][B] = 5 and also d[B][D] = 3 (B→C→D), which is why direction matters.

Answer: 5 — option (B).

Common misconception. 3 is d[B][D], the reverse direction; 6 is d[C][B] in the reverse graph or the length of D→A→C→D. In a directed graph the matrix is not symmetric, and reading the entry off the wrong row is the common error.

ALG-U4-Q24NAThard

A directed graph has vertices A, B, C, D, E and edges A→B (3), B→C (−2), A→C (4), C→D (5), D→E (1), B→D (9), C→E (12), A→E (20). The graph has no negative-weight cycle. What is the shortest-path distance from A to E? Give an exact integer.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 7

Detailed solution

Concept. With a negative edge present, Dijkstra's greedy rule is unsafe, but Floyd–Warshall's DP is not: it compares complete path lengths rather than extending the cheapest prefix. The shortest path is found by allowing more intermediate vertices step by step.

Step by step.

  1. Start with the direct edges: d[A][B] = 3, d[A][C] = 4, d[A][E] = 20; every other pair is ∞ except the listed edges.
  2. Allow B as an intermediate: d[A][C] = min(4, d[A][B] + w(B,C)) = min(4, 3 − 2) = 1. The negative edge is now doing the work.
  3. Allow C: d[A][D] = min(3 + 9, 1 + 5) = 6, and d[A][E] = min(20, 1 + 12) = 13.
  4. Allow D: d[A][E] = min(13, d[A][D] + 1) = min(13, 7) = 7.
  5. Allowing E changes nothing. The final table gives d[A][E] = 7 along the path A→B→C→D→E.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. 13 is the answer if the negative edge is used too late or not at all: A→C→E costs 16 with the direct A→C = 4, and A→B→D→E costs 13. The gain is compounded — A→C drops from 4 to 1, which pulls A→D from 12 down to 6, which pulls A→E from 13 down to 7 — so every intermediate stage must be recomputed, not just the last one.

ALG-U4-Q25MSQhard

Which of the following statements about all-pairs shortest paths are TRUE?

  1. On an unweighted graph, a breadth-first search from every vertex computes all-pairs distances faster than Floyd–Warshall.
  2. If a graph has negative edges but no negative-weight cycle, running Dijkstra's algorithm from every source computes correct all-pairs distances.
  3. Johnson's algorithm reweights the edges with a potential function obtained from one Bellman–Ford run from a new source, then runs Dijkstra from every vertex, in O(V² log V + VE) with a binary heap.
  4. If a negative-weight cycle is reachable, the final Floyd–Warshall matrix satisfies d[i][i] < 0 for every vertex i on that cycle.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Three different all-pairs strategies, each with its own precondition: BFS exploits unit weights, Dijkstra requires non-negative weights, Johnson repairs negative weights before using Dijkstra, and Floyd–Warshall needs no precondition but detects negative cycles instead of returning nonsense.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. BFS from each vertex costs O(V·(V+E)), which on a sparse graph is far below Θ(V³); Floyd–Warshall ignores the sparsity.
  2. (B) FALSE. Dijkstra finalises a vertex when it is popped, assuming no later path can be shorter. Take A→B (1), A→C (2), C→B (−2): Dijkstra from A pops B at distance 1 and finalises it, although the true distance is 0 through C. One negative edge is enough; no cycle is needed.
  3. (C) TRUE. Bellman–Ford from a new source s with 0-weight edges to all vertices gives potentials h(v) = δ(s,v) satisfying w(u,v) + h(u) − h(v) ≥ 0. Reweighting preserves shortest paths, so V Dijkstra runs give O(V² log V + VE) with a binary heap.
  4. (D) TRUE. A vertex i on a negative cycle can reach itself through that cycle with negative total weight, so d[i][i] < 0 after all vertices are allowed as intermediates; vertices not on the cycle keep d[i][i] = 0.

Answer: (A), (C) and (D) are TRUE; (B) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (B) is the persistent error: 'Dijkstra is fine as long as there is no negative cycle' is false — a single negative edge is enough to break it. (D) is often mis-stated as 'd[i][i] < 0 for all i', which would wrongly include vertices off the cycle.

ALG-U4-Q45MCQeasy

In Floyd–Warshall the three nested loops can be written in any of the six orders. Which ordering is correct?

  1. The vertex loop k must be outermost.
  2. The source loop i must be outermost.
  3. The destination loop j must be outermost.
  4. Any of the three may be outermost; the result is the same.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The meaning of the table after iteration k is fixed: shortest paths whose intermediate vertices all lie in {1, …, k}. That is a statement about a whole pass over the matrix, so the pass must be the outer loop.

Step by step.

  1. With k outermost, the whole matrix is correct for the set {1, …, k} after pass k.
  2. With i outermost, the row i is finished before the other rows have absorbed vertex k, so combinations through k are missed.
  3. The other four orders fail on some graph; the standard counterexample uses a two-edge path whose middle vertex is examined too late.

Answer: The k loop outermost — option (A).

Common misconception. Because the update looks symmetric in i and j, it is easy to believe the order does not matter. It does: only the k-outermost order computes a full pass over all intermediate-vertex sets.

ALG-U4-Q46MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about a Floyd–Warshall implementation are TRUE?

  1. Initialise d[i][i] = 0, d[i][j] = w(i, j) when the edge exists, and ∞ otherwise.
  2. The n×n array can be updated in place, because d[i][k] and d[k][j] do not change during iteration k.
  3. A separate n×n×n array is needed to keep d^{(k)} for every k.
  4. If ∞ is stored as a large sentinel, d[i][k] + d[k][j] can overflow, so the addition needs a guard.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The recurrence is d^{(k)}[i][j] = min(d^{(k−1)}[i][j], d^{(k−1)}[i][k] + d^{(k−1)}[k][j]); the implementation questions are about the base matrix, the array dimension, and arithmetic on infinity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: zero-length paths cost 0, single edges cost their weight, and the rest are unknown.
  2. (B) True: d[i][k] and d[k][j] are both computed with k as an endpoint, so neither is altered during pass k; the in-place update therefore matches the recurrence.
  3. (C) False: the k-th table depends only on the (k−1)-th, so two n×n arrays, or one with the in-place argument of (B), suffice.
  4. (D) True: a sentinel plus a negative weight can wrap, and even without wrap the sum of two sentinels must not be treated as a real distance.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. (C) is the usual over-allocation; the third dimension is what the recurrence lets you discard. And with negative edges the sentinel overflow in (D) is not hypothetical — a negative weight added to a large sentinel can make an unreachable pair look reachable.

ALG-U4-Q47NATeasy

A directed graph has vertices A, B, C, D and edges A→B (4), A→C (9), B→C (2), B→D (7), C→D (3). What is the shortest-path distance from A to D? Give an exact integer.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 9

Detailed solution

Concept. Shortest paths here can be found by enumerating the routes from A to D, since every edge has a single direction and the graph is tiny.

Step by step.

  1. A→B→D = 4 + 7 = 11.
  2. A→C→D = 9 + 3 = 12.
  3. A→B→C→D = 4 + 2 + 3 = 9.
  4. The minimum is 9.

Answer: 9.

Common misconception. 11 is the two-edge route and 12 the expensive first edge; the three-edge route wins because B→C is cheap. More edges do not mean a longer path — the number of edges is not part of the cost.

ALG-U4-Q48MCQhard

A directed graph has vertices 1, 2, 3, 4 and edges 1→2, 2→3, 3→1, 2→4. How many ordered pairs (u, v) satisfy: v is reachable from u, counting u = v as reachable by a path of length 0?

  1. 10
  2. 12
  3. 13
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Reachability is the transitive closure, and the closure of a relation always contains the diagonal: every vertex reaches itself in zero steps. Floyd–Warshall computes it by replacing min with OR and + with AND.

Step by step.

  1. Vertices 1, 2, 3 form a cycle, so each of them reaches all three of 1, 2, 3.
  2. Vertex 2 also has the edge to 4, and 4 has no outgoing edge, so 4 reaches only itself.
  3. Counts: 1 → {1, 2, 3, 4} = 4; 2 → {1, 2, 3, 4} = 4; 3 → {1, 2, 3, 4} = 4; 4 → {4} = 1. Total 13.

Answer: 13 — option (C).

Common misconception. 12 is the answer if the reflexive pairs are omitted, and 10 counts only the pairs that need at least one edge. The diagonal matters: a path of length 0 is a path, and the initialisation d[i][i] = 0 encodes exactly that.

ALG-U4-Q49MCQmedium

Which statement about Floyd–Warshall is TRUE?

  1. It needs a separate n×n×n array holding the matrix after every intermediate vertex.
  2. The vertex loop must be outermost, and after iteration k the table holds shortest paths whose intermediate vertices all lie in {1, …, k}.
  3. If the graph contains a negative-weight cycle the loop never terminates.
  4. Replacing min with + and + with × counts the number of distinct shortest paths between every pair.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The invariant of the outer loop is the whole content of the algorithm: after pass k, every pair's entry is optimal over paths that may use only the first k vertices internally.

Step by step.

  1. (A) False: each pass depends only on the previous one, so one n×n array updated in place suffices.
  2. (B) True: this is the invariant, and with k outermost every combination through k has been tried before pass k+1.
  3. (C) False: the loops are fixed in number — n passes of n² relaxations — so termination is unconditional; a negative cycle only makes the values meaningless (the diagonal turns negative).
  4. (D) False: that substitution computes path counts only if ties are handled separately; shortest-path counting needs a second table recording how many optimal routes end at each pair, reset whenever a shorter one is found.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. (C) confuses non-termination with incorrect output: unlike Bellman–Ford, Floyd–Warshall has no early exit and no bound to violate. (D) is a real technique, but it needs more than the two substitutions shown.

ALG-U4-Q50NAThard

A directed graph has vertices A, B, C, D, E and edges A→B (6), A→C (7), B→D (5), B→E (−4), C→B (8), C→E (9), D→A (2), D→C (−3), E→D (7). There is no negative-weight cycle. After Floyd–Warshall, what is d[C][A], the shortest distance from C to A? Give an exact integer.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 13

Detailed solution

Concept. With negative edges a shortest path may use more edges than the obvious one, so every route from C back to A must be considered, including routes that detour through the negative edge B→E.

Step by step.

  1. Direct-looking route C→B→D→A = 8 + 5 + 2 = 15.
  2. Through the negative edge: C→B→E→D→A = 8 + (−4) + 7 + 2 = 13.
  3. Other routes are longer: C→E→D→A = 9 + 7 + 2 = 18, C→B→E→D→C→… only revisits vertices.
  4. So d[C][A] = 13.

Answer: 13.

Common misconception. 15 is the route that ignores the negative edge, and it is the answer a Dijkstra-based instinct produces — Dijkstra is not correct on this graph. Adding edges can shorten a path only when some of them are negative, which is exactly why all-pairs needs a DP rather than repeated Dijkstra.

Unit 5 — Graph Algorithms: Traversals & Spanning Trees · 50 solved questions (2026-10-31)
BFS, DFS & edge classification 14
ALG-U5-Q01MCQeasy

An undirected graph has vertices A–G and edges AB, AD, BC, BE, CF, DG, EG. A depth-first search starts at A, and at every vertex the neighbours are examined in alphabetical order. In what order are the vertices first discovered?

  1. A, B, C, F, E, G, D
  2. A, B, C, F, G, E, D
  3. A, B, D, G, E, C, F
  4. A, B, C, E, G, D, F
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A depth-first search goes as deep as it can before it backtracks, so the discovery order is a preorder walk of the recursion tree: from a vertex it moves to its first unvisited neighbour and only returns when every neighbour of the current vertex is done.

Step by step.

  1. At A the neighbours are B and D; the alphabetically first is B, so A, B.
  2. At B the neighbours are A (visited), C, E; go to C: A, B, C.
  3. At C only F is new: A, B, C, F. F has no other neighbour, so the search backtracks to C and then to B.
  4. At B the next unvisited neighbour is E: A, B, C, F, E.
  5. At E go to G, and at G go to D: A, B, C, F, E, G, D. Everything is visited.

Answer: A, B, C, F, E, G, D — option (A).

Common misconception. Option (C) is the breadth-first order from A. Option (B) is the classic slip: it climbs from F straight to G, but F is adjacent only to C, so the search must backtrack to B and reach G through E.

ALG-U5-Q02MCQeasy

A connected undirected graph has 9 vertices and 13 edges. A depth-first search is run from one vertex. How many of the 13 edges are classified as back edges?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 8
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a DFS of an undirected graph the tree edges number exactly n − 1 for a connected graph, and every other edge is a back edge — an undirected DFS produces no forward and no cross edges.

Step by step.

  1. A spanning tree of a connected graph on 9 vertices has 9 − 1 = 8 edges, and these are the tree edges of the DFS.
  2. The remaining edges are the non-tree edges: 13 − 8 = 5.
  3. Each non-tree edge of an undirected graph joins a vertex to an ancestor in the DFS tree, so each one is a back edge.
  4. Therefore all 5 non-tree edges are back edges.

Answer: 5 back edges — option (B).

Common misconception. Subtracting from the vertex count instead of the tree-edge count gives 13 − 9 = 4 (option A). Option (C) is the number of tree edges, and option (D) forgets that one edge is used twice.

ALG-U5-Q03MCQmedium

A directed graph has edges A→B, B→C, C→A, A→D, D→C. A depth-first search starts at A and examines out-neighbours in alphabetical order. How is the edge D→C classified?

  1. A tree edge
  2. A back edge
  3. A forward edge
  4. A cross edge
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. For an edge u→v met during a DFS: if v is white it is a tree edge; if v is grey it is a back edge; if v is black then it is a forward edge when d[v] > d[u] and a cross edge when d[v] < d[u].

Step by step.

  1. From A the first out-neighbour is B: d[A] = 1, d[B] = 2.
  2. From B go to C: d[C] = 3. From C the edge C→A meets a grey vertex, so it is a back edge; C finishes at time 4 and B at 5.
  3. Back at A, the next out-neighbour is D: d[D] = 6.
  4. The edge D→C now meets C black with d[C] = 3 < d[D] = 6, so C was finished before D was even discovered.
  5. A black target discovered earlier than the source is neither an ancestor (that would be a back edge, and ancestors are grey) nor a descendant (that would need d[C] > d[D]), so the edge is a cross edge.

Answer: A cross edge — option (D).

Common misconception. The tempting error is to reason that because C has an edge back to A, everything reachable from A after C must be "below" C. The DFS had already finished C before D was discovered, so C is not an ancestor of D at all.

ALG-U5-Q04MCQhard

An undirected graph has edges AB, AC, BC, BD, BE, CF, CG, DE, EG, FG. A breadth-first search runs from A. How many of the ten edges are not tree edges and join two vertices that lie on the same BFS level?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A BFS tree assigns every vertex its distance from the source. A tree edge always joins level k to level k + 1, and in an undirected graph every non-tree edge joins vertices whose levels differ by at most one — so a non-tree edge is either within a level or between consecutive levels.

Step by step.

  1. Level 0 is A. Level 1 is the neighbourhood of A: B and C.
  2. Level 2 is everything else reachable from those: from B we get D, E and from C we get F, G, so level 2 is {D, E, F, G}.
  3. The BFS tree edges are AB, AC (level 0–1) and BD, BE, CF, CG (level 1–2): six tree edges, which is right for seven vertices.
  4. The four remaining edges are BC (both level 1), DE, EG and FG (all three inside level 2).
  5. All four join two vertices of the same level, so the count is 4.

Answer: 4 — option (C).

Common misconception. Counting all ten edges minus the six tree edges gives the number of non-tree edges, which is also 4 here, but the reason matters: had one non-tree edge joined level 1 to level 2, the answer would have been smaller. The level-difference-of-at-most-one property is what makes a BFS tree a shortest-path tree.

ALG-U5-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about breadth-first and depth-first search are TRUE?

  1. In a depth-first search of an undirected graph every non-tree edge is a back edge; no edge is ever classified as a forward or a cross edge.
  2. A breadth-first tree of an undirected graph can contain a tree edge joining two vertices whose levels differ by more than one.
  3. In a depth-first search of a directed graph, an edge u→v is a cross edge exactly when v is already finished and d[v] < d[u].
  4. Running breadth-first search on a graph whose edges all have unit weight gives, for each vertex, the number of distinct shortest paths from the source.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The classification rules differ between undirected and directed graphs, and BFS computes distances but not path counts unless the counter is added explicitly.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. In an undirected graph the edge uv is examined twice. When it is examined from the later-discovered endpoint the other endpoint is grey (an ancestor), because an undirected DFS never finishes a vertex while an unvisited neighbour of it is reachable. Hence every non-tree edge is a back edge.
  2. (B) FALSE. A BFS tree edge always joins a vertex to a vertex one level deeper, by construction.
  3. (C) TRUE. A black target with a smaller discovery time was finished before the source started, so it is in an earlier subtree: that is precisely a cross edge. A black target with a larger discovery time is a descendant, which is a forward edge.
  4. (D) FALSE. BFS gives distances; counting shortest paths needs a second array that is added to, and reset to 1, whenever a vertex is first reached.

Answer: (A) and (C) are TRUE; (B) and (D) are FALSE.

Common misconception. (B) is the usual confusion between tree edges and non-tree edges: only the non-tree edges of a BFS can join the same level or adjacent levels, and even those can never skip a level. (D) is the "BFS finds all shortest paths" overstatement — it finds the distance, and a shortest-path tree, but the number of shortest paths is extra bookkeeping.

ALG-U5-Q06NATeasy

A directed graph has edges A→B, B→C, C→A, D→E, E→D, F→A. A full depth-first scan visits the vertices in the order A, B, C, D, E, F, restarting at the next unvisited vertex whenever the current search ends. How many trees does the resulting DFS forest contain?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. A full DFS scan of a directed graph produces a forest with one tree per restart. Every vertex reached from the current root joins that root's tree, whatever direction the edges point, so the trees correspond to the weakly connected pieces as they are encountered, not to the strongly connected ones.

Step by step.

  1. Start at A: A→B→C→A returns to a grey vertex, so the first tree is {A, B, C}.
  2. The next unvisited vertex in the scan order is D: D→E→D gives the second tree {D, E}.
  3. The next unvisited vertex is F. Its only edge is F→A, and A is already finished, so F starts and ends its own tree {F}.
  4. Three restarts, three trees.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. The common error is to merge F into the first tree because F→A exists. An edge pointing into a finished tree is a cross edge; it does not attach F to that tree. Counting strongly connected components would also give 3 here, but that is a coincidence, not the rule.

ALG-U5-Q07NAThard

A directed graph has edges A→B, A→C, B→C, B→E, C→D, D→E. A depth-first search starts at A and examines out-neighbours in alphabetical order. How many edges are classified as forward edges?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. An edge u→v is a forward edge when v is a proper descendant of u in the DFS tree that is already finished, that is when v is black and d[u] < d[v].

Step by step.

  1. Trace the search with discovery and finish times. A is discovered at 1; its neighbours are B then C, so B is discovered at 2.
  2. From B the neighbours are C and E; go to C at 3, then C→D at 4, then D→E at 5.
  3. E has no out-edge and finishes at 6; D finishes at 7 and C at 8.
  4. Back at B the edge B→E meets E black with d[E] = 5 > d[B] = 2: a forward edge. B finishes at 9.
  5. Back at A the edge A→C meets C black with d[C] = 3 > d[A] = 1: a second forward edge. A finishes at 10.
  6. The tree edges are AB, BC, CD, DE; nothing is a back or cross edge. The forward edges are A→C and B→E.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Both forward edges are also "shortcuts" that a topological instinct wants to call tree edges. The test is the discovery time: the target must be black and discovered after the source. Reversing that inequality would misclassify them as cross edges.

ALG-U5-S2-Q01MCQeasy

A directed graph has the edges A→B, A→C, B→D, C→D, D→E, C→F. A breadth-first search starts at A. How many vertices lie at distance exactly 2 from A?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. BFS discovers vertices in nondecreasing order of their distance from the source, so the vertices it reaches after k rounds are exactly those at distance k. A vertex is credited to the round in which it is first discovered, not to every round from which an edge reaches it.

Step by step.

  1. Distance 0: A alone.
  2. Distance 1: the out-neighbours of A, namely B and C.
  3. Distance 2: neighbours of B and C that are still undiscovered — D (reached from both B and C, but counted once) and F (from C). That is two vertices.
  4. Distance 3: E, from D. The whole graph is now covered.

Answer: Option (B) — 2 vertices, D and F.

Common misconception. Counting D twice because two different edges reach it, which would give 3, or placing E at distance 2 because D→E exists. BFS labels a vertex at its first discovery only; later edges into it change nothing.

ALG-U5-S2-Q02MCQeasy

In a depth-first search of a directed graph, an edge (u,v) leads to a vertex v that is already finished and satisfies d[u] > d[v]. How is this edge classified?

  1. a tree edge
  2. a back edge
  3. a forward edge
  4. a cross edge
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. DFS classifies an edge by the colour of its target when the edge is explored, and for a finished (black) target by comparing discovery times: a finished vertex with a later discovery time is a descendant of the current vertex.

Step by step.

  1. Target white — the edge becomes a tree edge and the search descends into it.
  2. Target grey — the target is still on the recursion stack, so it is an ancestor: a back edge.
  3. Target black with d[u] < d[v] — the target was discovered after u and is already finished, so it is a proper descendant: a forward edge.
  4. Target black with d[u] > d[v] — the target was discovered earlier and has already finished, so it is not a descendant and not on the stack: a cross edge.

Answer: Option (D) — a cross edge.

Common misconception. Swapping the two comparisons, which turns a cross edge into a forward edge. The test is simply whether the target is a descendant of the current vertex, and a descendant always has the larger discovery time.

ALG-U5-S2-Q03MCQmedium

Let T be a breadth-first tree of a connected undirected graph, and let levels be the distances from the root in T. Which one of the following is TRUE for every edge (u,v) of the graph that is not in T?

  1. the levels of u and v differ by at most 1
  2. u and v lie on the same level
  3. the levels of u and v differ by exactly 1
  4. one of u and v is the root
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. BFS processes vertices in level order and, from a vertex at level k, every still-undiscovered neighbour is put at level k+1. So no edge of the graph can jump two levels.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose u is at level k and v at level j with j ≥ k + 2.
  2. When u was expanded, v was either already discovered — impossible, since level j is only reached after level k+1 — or undiscovered, in which case the edge would have put v at level k+1.
  3. Both cases contradict j ≥ k + 2, so |j − k| ≤ 1.
  4. The bound is tight: an edge between levels 1 and 2 occurs in the very first layer, and an edge inside one level occurs in any triangle whose root is the third vertex.

Answer: Option (A) — the levels differ by at most 1.

Common misconception. Reading the bound as "exactly 1". A graph containing the triangle A B C with root A has B and C both at level 1 joined by a non-tree edge, so the difference can also be 0.

ALG-U5-S2-Q04MCQhard

A directed graph has the edges A→B, A→C, B→D, C→D, D→A. A depth-first search starts at A and examines out-neighbours in alphabetical order. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. D→A is a back edge and C→D is a cross edge
  2. C→D is a forward edge and D→A is a back edge
  3. C→D is a back edge and D→A is a cross edge
  4. all five edges are tree edges
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A directed DFS classifies each edge at the moment it is explored, by the state of the target: white gives a tree edge, grey a back edge, and a finished target is a forward edge if it is a descendant (d[u] < d[v]) and a cross edge otherwise.

Step by step.

  1. Start A (grey). Its first out-neighbour is B: white, so A→B is a tree edge.
  2. At B the only out-neighbour is D: white, so B→D is a tree edge.
  3. At D the only out-neighbour is A, which is grey — still on the stack — so D→A is a back edge. D then finishes, then B.
  4. Back at A, the next out-neighbour is C: white, so A→C is a tree edge. At C the out-neighbour D is black and was discovered before C, so it is not a descendant: C→D is a cross edge.

Answer: Option (A) — D→A back, C→D cross.

Common misconception. Calling C→D a forward edge. A forward edge needs a target with a larger discovery time, i.e. a proper descendant; D was finished before C was even discovered.

ALG-U5-S2-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about breadth-first and depth-first search are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. On a graph stored as adjacency lists, both searches run in O(V + E) time.
  2. In an unweighted graph, BFS gives shortest-path distances from the source.
  3. A directed graph contains a cycle if and only if a DFS finds a back edge.
  4. In an undirected graph every edge of a DFS is either a tree edge or a forward edge.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Both searches touch each vertex once and scan each adjacency list once — on a directed graph the adjacency lists hold E entries, on an undirected graph 2E. Their difference is the order in which they expand, not their cost.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Each vertex is pushed once and each adjacency entry is examined once, so the total work is O(V + E) for either search. TRUE.
  2. (B) BFS expands strictly by level, so a vertex is labelled with the fewest edges that reach it — a shortest path in an unweighted graph. TRUE.
  3. (C) A back edge closes a cycle; conversely, if a cycle exists, the first vertex of it to be discovered has an edge back to itself along the cycle, and DFS meets that edge while the vertex is still grey. TRUE.
  4. (D) In an undirected graph a non-tree edge always joins a vertex to an ancestor, so it is a back edge; cross and forward edges cannot occur because the edge would have been explored from the other endpoint. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing that DFS is faster than BFS or that BFS costs more because of its queue. Both are linear; only the memory profile differs — O(V) for BFS against O(V) stack depth for DFS, but a BFS frontier can hold Θ(V) vertices.

ALG-U5-S2-Q06MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about DFS edge classification in a directed graph are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A back edge goes from a vertex to one of its ancestors, that is, to a grey vertex.
  2. A forward edge goes to a proper descendant that has already been finished.
  3. A cross edge may go to a vertex that belongs to an earlier DFS tree.
  4. An undirected graph can contain a cross edge.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Grey means "on the recursion stack", so a grey target is an ancestor; black means finished, so the edge is classified by discovery times. Everything that is not an ancestor or a descendant is in another subtree or another tree.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The vertices on the stack are exactly the ancestors of the current vertex, and grey marks precisely those. TRUE.
  2. (B) A forward edge targets a vertex discovered later (d[u] < d[v]) and already finished, which is a proper descendant. TRUE.
  3. (C) A vertex of an earlier tree is black and has a smaller discovery time than the current vertex, which is the cross-edge case. TRUE.
  4. (D) In an undirected graph the edge would be examined from both ends; whichever end is reached first makes it a tree edge, and the other end sees an ancestor. Cross edges need direction to exist at all. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Thinking a cross edge must stay inside one tree. A cross edge from a new root to a finished tree is common — it is exactly what stops that tree from absorbing the new vertex.

ALG-U5-S2-Q07NATeasy

An undirected graph on the vertices A–F has the edges AB, AC, BD, CE, DF, EF. A breadth-first search starts at A. What is the sum of the distances from A to all six vertices, counting the distance to A itself as 0?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 9

Detailed solution

Concept. In an unweighted graph the BFS level of a vertex is its distance from the source, so the question is a request for the level structure of the BFS tree.

Step by step.

  1. Level 0: A, distance 0.
  2. Level 1: neighbours of A, that is B and C, each at distance 1.
  3. Level 2: D from B, and E from C.
  4. Level 3: F, reached from D or from E — either way at distance 3.
  5. Sum = 0 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 = 9.

Answer: 9

Common misconception. Giving F distance 2 because it has two neighbours at distance 2. Both routes pass through one more edge, so its distance is 3; taking the minimum over all routes is the whole point of BFS.

Topological sort 12
ALG-U5-Q08MCQeasy

Which of the following directed graphs has no topological ordering?

  1. A→B, B→C, C→D
  2. A→B, A→C, B→D, C→D
  3. A→B, B→C, C→A, C→D
  4. A→B, C→B, C→D
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A directed graph has a topological ordering exactly when it is acyclic. A single directed cycle makes the ordering impossible, because every edge of the cycle demands that its tail come before its head, and going round the cycle demands the opposite.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) is a path, hence acyclic: A, B, C, D works.
  2. Option (B) is acyclic: A, B, C, D and A, C, B, D both work.
  3. Option (C) contains A→B→C→A, a directed cycle of length three. Any ordering would need A before B, B before C and C before A at once.
  4. Option (D) is acyclic: C, A, B, D works.

Answer: The graph of option (C) — it contains the cycle A→B→C→A.

Common misconception. A common slip is to test only for a cycle of length two (a pair of opposite edges). The three-cycle in option (C) has no two-cycle inside it, yet it destroys every ordering. Equally, a graph can look tangled and still be acyclic, as option (D) shows.

ALG-U5-Q09MCQeasy

A directed acyclic graph has edges A→B, A→C, B→D, C→D. How many distinct topological orderings does it have?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological ordering is a linear extension of the partial order given by reachability. The number of orderings is the number of ways to interleave the parts that are not forced to be in a fixed order.

Step by step.

  1. A has in-degree zero and must come first; D has out-degree zero and must come last.
  2. That leaves B and C in the middle. No edge relates them, so either order is allowed.
  3. The two orderings are A, B, C, D and A, C, B, D.

Answer: 2 — option (B).

Common misconception. Answering 4 treats the two independent middles as freely permutable and forgets that A and D are pinned at the ends. A DAG on n vertices with no edges has n! orderings, which is the other extreme to keep in mind.

ALG-U5-Q10MCQmedium

A directed acyclic graph has edges A→B, A→D, B→E, D→C, C→F, E→F. Kahn's algorithm is run with the ready set kept as a min-priority queue on vertex labels, so the alphabetically smallest ready vertex is always the one removed. Which ordering is produced?

  1. A, B, D, C, E, F
  2. A, B, C, D, E, F
  3. A, D, B, C, E, F
  4. A, B, D, C, F, E
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kahn's algorithm repeatedly removes a vertex of in-degree zero and decrements the in-degree of its out-neighbours. The data structure that holds the ready vertices decides which of several valid topological orders you get; a min-heap gives the lexicographically smallest one.

Step by step.

  1. Initial in-degrees: A: 0, B: 1 (from A), D: 1 (from A), C: 1 (from D), E: 1 (from B), F: 2 (from C and E).
  2. Ready set {A}: remove A, which drops B and D to zero. Ready set {B, D}.
  3. Remove B (smaller label), which drops E to zero. Ready set {D, E}.
  4. Remove D, which drops C to zero. Ready set {C, E}.
  5. Remove C; F falls from 2 to 1. Ready set {E}.
  6. Remove E, which drops F to zero; remove F.

Answer: A, B, D, C, E, F — option (A).

Common misconception. Option (C) is also a valid topological ordering, so it catches anyone who only checks the edge constraints and forgets the tie-break rule. Option (B) ignores D→C entirely, and option (D) puts F before E, which violates E→F.

ALG-U5-Q11MCQhard

A directed acyclic graph has vertices A, B, C, D, E and edges A→C, B→C, B→D, C→E, D→E. How many distinct topological orderings does it have?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Count linear extensions by fixing what the edges force and then counting the interleavings that remain. Here E must be last, so the whole question is how many orderings the first four vertices admit.

Step by step.

  1. E has in-degree 2 and out-degree 0, and both C and D must precede it; A and B reach E only through C and D. So E is last in every ordering.
  2. Now order A, B, C, D subject to A < C, B < C and B < D.
  3. Enumerate by the position of B:
  4. B first: then A, C, D follow with only A < C — three arrangements (A C D, A D C, D A C).
  5. B second: A is first, then C, D in either order — two arrangements (A B C D, A B D C).
  6. B third or later: impossible, because A, C and D cannot fill two slots ahead of it while satisfying A < C and B < D.
  7. Total 3 + 2 = 5 orderings.

Answer: 5 — option (C).

Common misconception. Counting by removing ready vertices by hand usually loses the case where D precedes A. Writing the count as "permutations of four vertices divided by something" fails here, because the constraints do not form a symmetric pattern.

ALG-U5-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about topological ordering are TRUE?

  1. A directed graph has a topological ordering if and only if it is acyclic.
  2. If a directed acyclic graph has exactly one topological ordering, then it contains a directed path that visits every vertex.
  3. Ordering the vertices of any directed graph by decreasing finishing time of a depth-first search always produces a topological ordering.
  4. In a directed acyclic graph, every vertex of in-degree zero can be placed first in some topological ordering.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological ordering exists exactly for DAGs, and its uniqueness is tied to the existence of a Hamiltonian path. The DFS finish-time recipe is a topological sort only when the graph is already a DAG.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. If a cycle existed, its edges would demand a strict circular order. If no cycle exists, Kahn's algorithm always drains the graph, so an ordering exists.
  2. (B) TRUE. With a unique ordering v₁, v₂, …, vₙ, consecutive vertices must be joined by an edge: if v_i → v_{i+1} were missing, the two could be swapped to give a second ordering. So the ordering is a directed path through all vertices.
  3. (C) FALSE. On a graph with a cycle the finish-time order exists but is not a topological order of anything, because no topological order exists. The recipe is correct only under the DAG hypothesis.
  4. (D) TRUE. A vertex of in-degree zero has nothing that must precede it, and Kahn's algorithm can start with it; the rest can then be completed because the graph is acyclic.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (C) is the classic omission of a hypothesis: "reverse postorder of a DFS is a topological sort" is a theorem about DAGs, and students routinely apply it to graphs with cycles and get a meaningless order. (B) is often doubted, but the swap argument settles it.

ALG-U5-Q13NATmedium

A directed graph has edges A→B, B→C, C→B, A→D, D→E. Kahn's algorithm is run on it. How many vertices are removed before the ready queue becomes empty?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Kahn's algorithm removes a vertex only when its in-degree has fallen to zero. If the queue empties with vertices left, those vertices lie on or behind a cycle; the count removed is the size of the acyclic part that the algorithm can reach.

Step by step.

  1. In-degrees: A: 0, B: 2 (from A and C), C: 1 (from B), D: 1 (from A), E: 1 (from D).
  2. Ready set {A}: remove A. That drops B to 1 and D to 0. Removed so far: 1.
  3. Ready set {D}: remove D, which drops E to 0. Removed: 2.
  4. Ready set {E}: remove E. Removed: 3.
  5. The queue is now empty, but B and C are still present with in-degree 1 each — they support each other on the cycle B→C→B.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting four, by removing B once A is gone, ignores that B still has an incoming edge from C. In-degree counts incoming edges, not incoming edges from removed vertices.

ALG-U5-S2-Q08MCQeasy

Which one of the following conditions is necessary and sufficient for a directed graph to have a topological ordering?

  1. it is connected
  2. it has no directed cycle
  3. every vertex has out-degree at least 1
  4. it has at most n − 1 edges
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological ordering is a linear extension of the reachability relation. A linear extension of a strict partial order always exists, and a cycle would demand v ≺ v, which no ordering can satisfy.

Step by step.

  1. If the graph is acyclic, repeatedly remove a vertex of in-degree 0: one always exists in a finite acyclic digraph, and removing it leaves an acyclic digraph. The removal order is a topological ordering. So acyclicity is sufficient.
  2. If the graph has a cycle v₁ → v₂ → … → v₁, a topological ordering would need v₁ ≺ v₂ ≺ … ≺ v₁, which is impossible. So acyclicity is necessary.
  3. Connectivity, out-degrees and the edge count are irrelevant: the empty graph on n vertices is disconnected and has no edges, yet every permutation of its vertices is a topological ordering.

Answer: Option (B) — it has no directed cycle.

Common misconception. Answering "it is connected", which confuses topological order with a traversal. Disconnected DAGs have topological orderings; the components are simply concatenated in any order.

ALG-U5-S2-Q09MCQmedium

A DAG has the edges A→C, B→C, C→D, B→D. Kahn's algorithm is run with a FIFO queue that is initialised with all zero-in-degree vertices in alphabetical order, and a vertex is enqueued when its in-degree falls to zero. Which ordering does it output?

  1. A, B, C, D
  2. B, A, C, D
  3. A, C, B, D
  4. A, B, D, C
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kahn's algorithm peels off vertices whose in-degree has fallen to zero. With a FIFO queue the output is the order in which vertices are enqueued, so the initial alphabetical seed fixes the whole run.

Step by step.

  1. In-degrees: A: 0, B: 0, C: 2, D: 2. Queue [A, B].
  2. Pop A: C drops to 1, so nothing is enqueued. Queue [B].
  3. Pop B: C drops to 0 and is enqueued; D drops to 1. Queue [C].
  4. Pop C: D drops to 0 and is enqueued. Pop D.
  5. Output A, B, C, D — four vertices for four vertices, so the graph is acyclic.

Answer: Option (A) — A, B, C, D.

Common misconception. Assuming the smallest available vertex is always chosen. That is a min-priority ready set, not a FIFO queue; here both happen to agree, but in general a queue defers a vertex enqueued later behind one enqueued earlier.

ALG-U5-S2-Q10MCQhard

How many distinct topological orderings does the DAG on the vertices 1–6 with the edges 1→2, 1→3, 2→4, 3→4, 4→5, 4→6 have?

  1. 2
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Count linear extensions by slicing the DAG at its forced positions. Vertex 1 must come first, 4 must come after both 2 and 3, and 4 must come before both 5 and 6; the only freedom is the relative order inside each of those two pairs.

Step by step.

  1. Vertex 1 has in-degree 0 and is the only such vertex, so it is first in every ordering.
  2. Vertices 2 and 3 both depend only on 1, so they may appear in either order: 2 ways.
  3. Vertex 4 needs 2 and 3, so it comes immediately after them.
  4. Vertices 5 and 6 depend only on 4, so they may appear in either order: 2 ways.
  5. Total = 2 × 2 = 4, namely 1 2 3 4 5 6, 1 2 3 4 6 5, 1 3 2 4 5 6, 1 3 2 4 6 5.

Answer: Option (B) — 4 orderings.

Common misconception. Multiplying the free pairs but forgetting that 5 and 6 cannot move before 4, which would give a much larger count, or answering 2 by noticing only the pair (2, 3).

ALG-U5-S2-Q11MCQeasy

A DAG has exactly one topological ordering. Which one of the following must be TRUE?

  1. the DAG contains a directed path that visits every vertex exactly once
  2. the DAG has exactly n − 1 edges
  3. the DAG is a tree
  4. every vertex has in-degree at most 1
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A unique topological ordering forces one vertex to be the only source at every step, so the peeled vertex always has an edge into the next one. That chain of forced choices is a Hamiltonian path.

Step by step.

  1. Let the unique ordering be v₁, v₂, …, vₙ.
  2. If some vᵢ had no edge to vᵢ₊₁, the two could be swapped and the result would still be a topological ordering — contradicting uniqueness.
  3. So vᵢ → vᵢ₊₁ for every i: the ordering itself is a directed path through all n vertices.
  4. The converse also holds, so uniqueness of the ordering is equivalent to the existence of such a Hamiltonian path.

Answer: Option (A) — the DAG has a directed path through every vertex.

Common misconception. Equating uniqueness with n − 1 edges. A DAG with a Hamiltonian path may carry extra edges (say 1→3 alongside 1→2→3), so the edge count is not fixed; and a tree on n vertices with edges pointing away from the root has many topological orderings.

ALG-U5-S2-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about topological ordering are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If Kahn's algorithm ever has two or more zero-in-degree vertices available at the same time, the DAG has at least two topological orderings.
  2. A DAG with a unique topological ordering has exactly one vertex of in-degree 0.
  3. A DAG on n vertices has at most n! topological orderings.
  4. A depth-first search of a DAG can produce an edge to an ancestor.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological ordering is a linear extension, so the count is governed by how many independent choices the DAG allows. Ties in the ready set are exactly the freedom the algorithm has.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Two ready vertices can be output in either order and both partial outputs extend to complete orderings, so at least two distinct orderings exist. TRUE.
  2. (B) Two zero-in-degree vertices would already give the choice of which to place first, contradicting uniqueness. TRUE.
  3. (C) Every ordering is a permutation of the n vertices, and there are n! permutations. TRUE.
  4. (D) An edge to an ancestor closes a cycle, and a DAG has no cycle, so no DFS of a DAG can produce one. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Thinking a DAG's DFS can still show a back edge "because DFS can reach finished vertices". Reaching a finished vertex gives a cross or forward edge; only a grey target, i.e. an ancestor, would mean a cycle.

ALG-U5-S2-Q13NATmedium

How many distinct topological orderings does the DAG on the vertices 1–5 with the edges 1→2, 1→3, 1→4, 2→5, 3→5, 4→5 have?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 6

Detailed solution

Concept. Here 1 must be first and 5 must be last, while 2, 3 and 4 are mutually incomparable. Every arrangement of the middle three is therefore valid.

Step by step.

  1. Vertex 1 has in-degree 0 and is the only source, so it is first.
  2. Vertex 5 needs 2, 3 and 4, so it is last.
  3. Vertices 2, 3 and 4 depend only on 1 and are not related to each other, so they may appear in any order in the three middle positions.
  4. The number of orderings is the number of permutations of three items, 3! = 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Answering 3 by counting the three middle vertices instead of their arrangements, or 24 by forgetting that 1 must precede all of them and 5 must follow all of them.

Prim's algorithm 12
ALG-U5-Q14MCQeasy

A weighted undirected graph on vertices A–F has edges AB 4, AD 1, BC 2, BE 3, CF 5, DE 6, EF 7, BD 8, CE 9. What is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree?

  1. 13
  2. 15
  3. 16
  4. 14
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A minimum spanning tree of a connected graph on n vertices has exactly n − 1 edges and is found greedily by the cut property — here, by taking the cheapest edge that does not close a cycle.

Step by step.

  1. Sort the edges: AD 1, BC 2, BE 3, AB 4, CF 5, DE 6, EF 7, BD 8, CE 9.
  2. Take AD 1; take BC 2; take BE 3 (now A, D, B, C, E are connected).
  3. Take AB 4 — it joins the A/D side to the B/C/E side and closes no cycle.
  4. CF 5 is the only edge reaching F, so take it.
  5. Six vertices need five edges; the tree is {AD, BC, BE, AB, CF} with total 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15.

Answer: 15 — option (B).

Common misconception. Taking the five cheapest edges without checking cycles gives 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15 here only by luck; the cheap-edge list must still be tested for connectivity. Choosing DE 6 instead of AB 4 gives 17, and dropping CF strands F.

ALG-U5-Q15MCQmedium

A weighted undirected graph on vertices A–F has edges AB 4, AD 1, BC 2, BE 3, CF 5, DE 6, EF 7, BD 8, CE 9. Prim's algorithm starts at A and at each step adds the cheapest edge leaving the current tree, ties broken alphabetically. In what order are the vertices added?

  1. A, D, B, C, E, F
  2. A, D, B, E, C, F
  3. A, B, C, F, E, D
  4. A, D, B, C, F, E
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Prim's algorithm grows one tree from a fixed root. The candidate set is every edge with exactly one endpoint inside the tree, and the cheapest of those is always safe to add by the cut property.

Step by step.

  1. Tree {A}. Candidate edges AD 1, AB 4; add AD 1, so D joins.
  2. Tree {A, D}. Candidates AB 4, DE 6, BD 8; add AB 4, so B joins.
  3. Tree {A, D, B}. Candidates BC 2, BE 3, DE 6, BD 8; add BC 2, so C joins.
  4. Tree {A, D, B, C}. Candidates BE 3, CF 5, DE 6, CE 9; add BE 3, so E joins.
  5. Tree {A, D, B, C, E}. Candidates CF 5, DE 6, EF 7, CE 9; add CF 5, so F joins.

Answer: A, D, B, C, E, F — option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) adds E before C, which would require BE 3 to be cheaper than BC 2. Option (D) reverses the last two: once E is in the tree, CF 5 is still cheaper than EF 7.

ALG-U5-Q16MCQeasy

Which property is the reason Prim's algorithm is correct?

  1. Adding, for every vertex, its cheapest incident edge always produces a minimum spanning tree.
  2. The heaviest edge of every cycle must appear in every minimum spanning tree.
  3. Running Prim's algorithm from two different start vertices always produces the same tree, even when edge weights repeat.
  4. For every cut of the graph, a minimum-weight edge crossing that cut belongs to some minimum spanning tree.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The cut property says that for any partition of the vertices into two non-empty parts, a lightest edge across the partition is safe to include. Prim's algorithm is exactly this property applied to the cut (tree, rest) at every step.

Step by step.

  1. At each step Prim's tree is one side of a cut and the unvisited vertices are the other side; the algorithm adds the lightest edge crossing it.
  2. By the cut property that edge lies in some minimum spanning tree, and extending the argument step by step keeps the partial tree a subtree of an MST.
  3. (A) is false: taking each vertex's cheapest incident edge can produce a cycle. On a triangle with weights 1, 2, 2 every vertex's cheapest edge lies in the triangle, and the three choices form a cycle.
  4. (B) is the opposite of the truth — the cycle property says the heaviest edge of a cycle is the one an MST can avoid.
  5. (C) is false in general: with repeated weights, different start vertices can lead to different minimum spanning trees.

Answer: The cut property — option (D).

Common misconception. (A) confuses the cut property with a vertex-by-vertex greedy rule; the greedy rule needs a cut, not a single vertex. (C) is true only when all weights are distinct, which is exactly the extra hypothesis the statement omits.

ALG-U5-Q17MCQmedium

An undirected graph on A, B, C, D has edges AB 1, BC 1, AC 1, CD 1, BD 3, AD 4. How many distinct minimum spanning trees does it have?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. A minimum spanning tree need not be unique when weights repeat. Any spanning tree whose edges can be exchanged one at a time with equal-weight alternatives without breaking the cut and cycle conditions is also a minimum spanning tree.

Step by step.

  1. The four weight-1 edges are AB, BC, AC, CD. A spanning tree of four vertices needs three edges, so the minimum possible total weight is 3.
  2. D has three incident edges: CD 1, BD 3, AD 4. A tree of weight 3 must reach D with the edge of weight 1, so CD is in every minimum tree.
  3. The remaining two edges must span A, B, C at total weight 2, so both must be weight-1 edges from the triangle {AB, BC, AC}.
  4. Any two of the three triangle edges form a spanning tree of the triangle, giving {AB, BC}, {AB, AC} and {BC, AC} — three trees, each of total weight 3.

Answer: 3 — option (C).

Common misconception. Answering 1 assumes that equal weights still give a unique tree. Answering 4 counts all three triangle edges as a choice, but a spanning tree of four vertices has exactly three edges, so AB, BC, AC together would close a cycle and is not a tree.

ALG-U5-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about minimum spanning trees are TRUE?

  1. If all edge weights of a connected graph are distinct, its minimum spanning tree is unique.
  2. When all edge weights are distinct, Prim's algorithm returns the same tree whichever vertex it starts from.
  3. A minimum spanning tree contains, for every cycle of the graph, the heaviest edge of that cycle.
  4. On a connected graph with V vertices and E edges, Prim's algorithm with a binary heap runs in O(E log V).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Distinct weights force uniqueness, which in turn makes every correct MST algorithm agree; the cycle property forbids the heaviest edge of a cycle from being forced into the tree; and a binary heap bounds Prim's cost by the number of heap operations.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Suppose two different minimum trees existed. Their symmetric difference contains a cycle on which the two trees alternate; swapping along it would exchange one edge for a strictly lighter one, contradicting minimality.
  2. (B) TRUE. Uniqueness makes the tree independent of the algorithm and of the start vertex.
  3. (C) FALSE. The cycle property says the heaviest edge of a cycle can always be excluded without losing minimality — it is the edge an MST avoids, not the one it must contain.
  4. (D) TRUE. Every edge enters and leaves the heap at most once, so there are O(E) heap operations at O(log V) each, plus the O(V) extraction phase.

Answer: (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (C) inverts the cycle property, which is the single most common MST error. Note also that (B) depends on (A): with repeated weights, two start vertices can legitimately return different minimum trees, so (B) must be read with its hypothesis attached.

ALG-U5-Q19NATmedium

An undirected graph on A–E has edges AB 2, BC −3, CD 4, DE 1, AE 5, BD 6, AC 7. What is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. Both Prim's and Kruskal's algorithms rely only on comparisons between edge weights, so negative weights are handled exactly as positive ones. (Negative cycles matter for shortest paths, not for spanning trees.)

Step by step.

  1. Sort the edges: BC −3, DE 1, AB 2, CD 4, AE 5, BD 6, AC 7.
  2. Take BC −3. Take DE 1.
  3. Take AB 2, which links A to {B, C}.
  4. Take CD 4, which links {A, B, C} to {D, E}.
  5. Five vertices need four edges, and all five are now connected; the remaining candidates AE 5, BD 6, AC 7 would close cycles.
  6. Total −3 + 1 + 2 + 4 = 4.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. The two standard errors are to refuse the negative edge, or to reach for Bellman–Ford reasoning and conclude that negative weights break the greedy choice. Also note that the MST is not the shortest-path tree: the distance from A to C in the tree is 2 − 3 = −1 along the tree, but the tree is chosen for total weight, not for any single path.

ALG-U5-S2-Q14MCQeasy

Prim's algorithm is implemented with a binary min-heap and adjacency lists. On a graph with V vertices and E edges its running time is:

  1. Θ(V²)
  2. O(E log V)
  3. O(V log E)
  4. O(E²)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Prim's algorithm performs V extractions of the minimum and, over the whole run, at most E decrease-key operations. A binary heap makes each of those O(log V).

Step by step.

  1. Each vertex enters the tree once, so there are V extract-min operations.
  2. Every edge is examined at most once from each endpoint, so there are O(E) decrease-key operations.
  3. Each heap operation costs O(log V), giving O((V + E) log V) = O(E log V) for a connected graph, where E ≥ V − 1.
  4. A Fibonacci heap improves this to O(E + V log V); the Θ(V²) bound belongs to the array implementation, which is better only when E is close to V².

Answer: Option (B) — O(E log V).

Common misconception. Quoting Θ(V²) as the complexity of Prim's algorithm in general. That is the array implementation; with a heap the cost is driven by the edges, not by V².

ALG-U5-S2-Q15MCQmedium

A weighted undirected graph on the vertices 1–6 has the edges 1–2 (6), 1–3 (1), 1–4 (5), 2–3 (5), 2–5 (3), 3–4 (5), 3–5 (6), 3–6 (4), 4–6 (2), 5–6 (6). Prim's algorithm starts at vertex 1. Which edge does it add third?

  1. 1–3, weight 1
  2. 3–6, weight 4
  3. 4–6, weight 2
  4. 2–3, weight 5
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Prim's algorithm repeatedly adds the lightest edge with exactly one endpoint inside the current tree — the lightest edge across the cut (tree, rest).

Step by step.

  1. Tree {1}. Crossing edges: 1–2 (6), 1–3 (1), 1–4 (5). Lightest is 1–3 (1): first edge.
  2. Tree {1, 3}. Crossing edges: 1–2 (6), 1–4 (5), 2–3 (5), 3–4 (5), 3–5 (6), 3–6 (4). Lightest is 3–6 (4): second edge.
  3. Tree {1, 3, 6}. Crossing edges: 1–2 (6), 1–4 (5), 2–3 (5), 3–4 (5), 3–5 (6), 4–6 (2), 5–6 (6). Lightest is 4–6 (2): third edge.
  4. The run finishes with 2–3 (5) and 2–5 (3), total weight 1 + 4 + 2 + 5 + 3 = 15.

Answer: Option (C) — 4–6 of weight 2.

Common misconception. Reaching for 2–3 (5) because it looks central. Only edges with exactly one endpoint in the current tree are candidates, and 4–6 becomes available precisely when 6 joins the tree.

ALG-U5-S2-Q16MCQhard

Every edge weight of a connected undirected graph is distinct. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. the minimum spanning tree is unique
  2. a graph whose minimum spanning tree is unique must have distinct weights
  3. Prim's algorithm requires non-negative weights
  4. a minimum spanning tree contains the heaviest edge of every cycle
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. With distinct weights every cut has a unique lightest edge, so the cut property names one specific safe edge at every step and the greedy choice is forced. Uniqueness of the tree is a consequence of distinctness, not equivalent to it.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose two different minimum spanning trees T₁ ≠ T₂ exist. Take an edge e ∈ T₁ ∖ T₂; adding it to T₂ closes a cycle, and some edge f of that cycle is not in T₁.
  2. By the cycle property w(e) ≠ w(f), and swapping f for e in T₂ would change the weight — contradicting the minimality of both. So the tree is unique.
  3. The converse fails: a 4-cycle whose weights are 1, 1, 2, 3 in order has a unique minimum spanning tree (the two edges of weight 1 cannot form a cycle together with anything cheaper), yet the weights are not all distinct.
  4. Prim's algorithm only ever compares weights, and the cut property holds for negative weights too, so non-negativity is not needed; and a minimum spanning tree avoids the heaviest edge of a cycle, it does not contain it.

Answer: Option (A) — the minimum spanning tree is unique.

Common misconception. Treating "distinct weights" and "unique minimum spanning tree" as equivalent. Distinct weights guarantee uniqueness; uniqueness can also happen with ties, as the 1, 1, 2, 3 cycle shows.

ALG-U5-S2-Q17MCQeasy

Prim's algorithm is started from a vertex s of a disconnected undirected graph and is stopped as soon as no edge leaves the current tree. Which one of the following does it return?

  1. a minimum spanning tree of the connected component that contains s
  2. a minimum spanning tree of the whole graph
  3. nothing, because the algorithm cannot start
  4. a spanning tree of the whole graph that need not be minimum
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Prim's algorithm only ever adds an edge that touches the tree it has built, so it can never leave the component of its start vertex. On a disconnected graph that component is all it can span.

Step by step.

  1. The tree starts as {s} and grows only through edges incident to it, so every vertex it reaches is in the same component as s.
  2. When the component is exhausted no crossing edge exists, and the algorithm stops.
  3. Within that component the cut property still applies at every step, so the result is a minimum spanning tree of the component.
  4. To cover the whole graph one restarts from an unvisited vertex in each remaining component, which produces a minimum spanning forest.

Answer: Option (A) — a minimum spanning tree of the component containing s.

Common misconception. Expecting an error or an infinite loop. Nothing goes wrong: the algorithm simply terminates with a tree that spans one component, which is why "spanning forest" is the right notion for disconnected graphs.

ALG-U5-S2-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Prim's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. With an adjacency matrix and an array of key values it runs in Θ(V²), which is preferable for dense graphs.
  2. It grows a single tree, whereas Kruskal's algorithm may grow several trees that are later merged.
  3. It can be implemented with a priority queue keyed by the lightest edge that leaves the current tree.
  4. It requires every edge weight to be non-negative.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Prim's algorithm is a cut-based growth of one tree; the data structure only changes the running time, never the set of edges it may consider.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Scanning an array of V keys takes O(V) per step and there are V steps, giving Θ(V²); for E = Θ(V²) this beats O(E log V). TRUE.
  2. (B) Prim keeps one connected tree and adds one edge at a time; Kruskal keeps a forest of components and merges them. TRUE.
  3. (C) That is exactly the heap implementation: the key of a vertex is the weight of its lightest edge to the tree. TRUE.
  4. (D) The cut property compares weights and holds for any real weights, negative ones included, so non-negativity is not required. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming the Θ(V²) bound is a defect. For a dense graph the array version is the faster choice; the heap version wins on sparse graphs, where E log V is much smaller than V².

ALG-U5-S2-Q19NATmedium

A weighted undirected graph on the vertices A–E has the edges AB 3, AC 1, BC 7, BD 5, CD 2, CE 4, DE 6. What is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 10

Detailed solution

Concept. For five vertices a spanning tree has exactly four edges. Sorting the edges and taking each one that joins two different components gives the minimum total, and Kruskal and Prim must agree on the weight.

Step by step.

  1. Sorted: AC 1, CD 2, AB 3, CE 4, BD 5, DE 6, BC 7.
  2. Take AC 1; take CD 2 — components {A, C, D}.
  3. Take AB 3 — components {A, B, C, D}.
  4. Take CE 4 — all five vertices are now connected, with four edges.
  5. Total = 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10. The remaining edges BD 5, DE 6, BC 7 would each close a cycle, so they are rejected.

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Adding the fifth-smallest edge as well and reporting 15, or taking BD 5 before AB 3. A tree on five vertices stops at four edges, and the four cheapest edges that do not close a cycle are the answer.

Kruskal's algorithm & disjoint sets 12
ALG-U5-Q20MCQeasy

A weighted undirected graph on vertices A–F has edges AB 4, AD 1, BC 2, BE 3, CF 5, DE 6, EF 7, BD 8, CE 9. Kruskal's algorithm processes the edges in increasing order of weight. Which edge is the first to be rejected because its endpoints are already connected?

  1. BD (weight 8)
  2. DE (weight 6)
  3. EF (weight 7)
  4. CE (weight 9)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm accepts an edge only when its endpoints lie in different components of the forest built so far; the union–find structure answers that question and performs the merge.

Step by step.

  1. Process AD 1: components {A, D}.
  2. BC 2: {A, D} and {B, C}.
  3. BE 3: {A, D} and {B, C, E}.
  4. AB 4: merges them into {A, B, C, D, E}.
  5. CF 5: F joins, so all six vertices are connected and the tree is complete.
  6. DE 6 is next: D and E are both already in the single component, so it is rejected.

Answer: DE with weight 6 — option (B).

Common misconception. The answer is not the heaviest edge, nor the first edge that would close a triangle. The forest is already spanning after five edges, so the very next edge in sorted order is rejected; every later edge is rejected too, which is why "the first rejected" and "the first edge after the tree is complete" coincide here.

ALG-U5-Q21MCQeasy

A disjoint-set forest stores parent pointers with parent[1] = 1, parent[2] = 1, parent[3] = 2 and parent[4] = 3. A find on element 4 is executed with full path compression. How many entries of the parent array change value?

  1. 2
  2. 0
  3. 1
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Path compression makes every node on the path from the queried element to the root point directly at the root. Nodes that already point at the root are left alone, so the count of changed entries is the number of non-root nodes on the path minus those already one hop from the root.

Step by step.

  1. Walking up from 4: 4 → 3 → 2 → 1, and parent[1] = 1, so the root is 1.
  2. The path is 4, 3, 2 and all three should point at 1 afterwards.
  3. parent[2] = 1 already, so it does not change.
  4. parent[3] changes from 2 to 1; parent[4] changes from 3 to 1.
  5. Two entries change.

Answer: 2 — option (A).

Common misconception. Counting three counts the node 2, whose pointer was already correct. Counting one counts only the queried node, which is what a lazy "compress just the argument" implementation does — that is not path compression.

ALG-U5-Q22MCQeasy

Which of the following statements about Kruskal's algorithm is TRUE?

  1. It must rebuild the forest with a fresh depth-first search after every union, and that dominates its running time.
  2. With union by rank and path compression, each union or find costs Θ(log E) in the worst case.
  3. It requires the graph to be connected, because otherwise it never terminates.
  4. Its running time is dominated by sorting the edges, giving O(E log E) for a graph with E edges.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm is sorting plus O(E) union–find operations. Sorting costs O(E log E) and the disjoint-set operations cost O(E α(V)), so sorting dominates.

Step by step.

  1. Sort the E edges: O(E log E).
  2. Scan the sorted list once, calling find twice and union at most once per edge: O(E α(V)) with union by rank and path compression.
  3. Total O(E log E), which is the same order as O(E log V) because E ≤ V² and log E ≤ 2 log V.
  4. (A) is the naive cycle test, which costs a search per edge; (B) contradicts the inverse-Ackermann bound; (C) is false — on a disconnected graph the algorithm simply returns a spanning forest.

Answer: Sorting dominates, so O(E log E) — option (D).

Common misconception. (B) is the trap for anyone who remembers "logarithmic" but not the inverse Ackermann function: with both heuristics the amortised cost per operation is O(α(n)), which is effectively constant. (C) confuses "no spanning tree exists" with "the algorithm misbehaves".

ALG-U5-Q23MSQhard

Which of the following statements about Kruskal's algorithm and the disjoint-set structure are TRUE?

  1. On a connected graph with V vertices, Kruskal's algorithm performs exactly V − 1 successful unions.
  2. If the edges are processed in non-decreasing weight order and ties are broken arbitrarily, the resulting tree is always a minimum spanning tree, though not necessarily the same one on every run.
  3. Union by rank without path compression gives amortised O(log n) per operation.
  4. Kruskal's algorithm detects a cycle by running a depth-first search on the forest built so far, and the total cost is still O(E log E).
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's correctness is independent of tie-breaking; its efficiency depends on the disjoint-set structure, whose guarantees are a union-by-rank height bound and an amortised inverse-Ackermann cost once path compression is added.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. A successful union merges two components, and the number of components falls from V to 1; every merge adds one tree edge, so there are exactly V − 1.
  2. (B) TRUE. The greedy choice is safe by the cut property whatever the tie-break; only the identity of the tree can vary.
  3. (C) TRUE. Union by rank keeps every tree of height at most ⌊log₂ n⌋, so a find walks at most logarithmically many pointers, and each union is O(1) after two finds.
  4. (D) FALSE. A depth-first cycle test costs O(V) per edge, so the total would be O(E·V), which is worse than O(E log E) for dense graphs.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C) are TRUE; (D) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (D) is tempting because it really is a correct algorithm — the false part is the complexity claim. (C) is often denied on the grounds that "only path compression helps", but union by rank alone already bounds the height, which is what the per-operation cost follows from.

ALG-U5-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the disjoint-set (union–find) structure are TRUE?

  1. Union by size guarantees that the height of any tree is at most ⌊log₂ n⌋.
  2. Path compression rewrites the parent pointer of every node on the path from the queried element to the root.
  3. With both union by rank and path compression, a sequence of m operations costs O(m α(n)).
  4. The rank of a node increases every time that node is passed to find.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Union by size or rank bounds tree height; path compression flattens the path walked by a find; together they give the inverse-Ackermann amortised bound. Neither heuristic changes the number of elements in a set, and only union changes a rank.

Step by step.

  1. (A) TRUE. Attaching the smaller tree under the larger root means a node's tree size at least doubles each time its depth increases, so the depth is at most log₂ n.
  2. (B) TRUE. That is the definition of full path compression: every node on the path is re-pointed at the root.
  3. (C) TRUE. This is the classical Tarjan bound; α(n) ≤ 4 for any conceivable n, so the cost is effectively linear.
  4. (D) FALSE. find never changes a rank. A rank increases only when a root is attached under another root of equal rank during a union.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C) are TRUE; (D) is FALSE.

Common misconception. (D) confuses rank with the parent pointer: path compression does change parent pointers during a find, but rank is a separate field that records the height bound and is updated only by unions. Answering (A) as false is also common, because people remember the bound as "log n" without the floor or the base.

ALG-U5-Q25NAThard

An undirected graph on vertices 1–7 has edges 1–2 (3), 1–3 (5), 2–3 (1), 2–4 (6), 3–4 (2), 3–5 (7), 4–5 (4), 4–6 (8), 5–6 (3), 5–7 (9), 6–7 (2). What is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 15

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm on a graph with several equally cheap edges: sort, then accept an edge only when union–find reports two different components. Seven vertices mean exactly six accepted edges.

Step by step.

  1. Sorted: 2–3 (1), 3–4 (2), 6–7 (2), 1–2 (3), 5–6 (3), 4–5 (4), 1–3 (5), 2–4 (6), 3–5 (7), 4–6 (8), 5–7 (9).
  2. Accept 2–3: {2, 3}. Accept 3–4: {2, 3, 4}. Accept 6–7: {6, 7}.
  3. Accept 1–2: {1, 2, 3, 4}. Accept 5–6: {5, 6, 7}.
  4. Accept 4–5: the two halves merge into one component of seven vertices.
  5. Six edges accepted; the rest are rejected as they close cycles.
  6. Total 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 + 3 + 4 = 15.

Answer: 15.

Common misconception. Accepting the six globally cheapest edges happens to work here, but the tempting sixth pick 4–5 (4) must be verified: had 1–3 (5) come first, the components {1, 2, 3, 4} and {5, 6, 7} would still need an edge between them, and 4–5 is the only cheap one. A common wrong answer, 17, comes from dropping 4–5 and taking 1–3 instead.

ALG-U5-S2-Q20MCQeasy

Kruskal's algorithm is run on a connected graph with 1000 vertices and 5000 edges. Which part dominates its running time?

  1. sorting the edges, O(E log E)
  2. the union–find operations, O(V²)
  3. building the adjacency lists
  4. the final check that the graph is connected
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm is a sort followed by a single linear scan in which each edge costs one or two union–find operations. The scan is cheap; the sort is not.

Step by step.

  1. Sorting E edges costs O(E log E).
  2. The scan performs 2E finds and at most V − 1 unions, which with union by rank and path compression costs O(E α(V)) — smaller than the sort.
  3. There are no adjacency lists to build, and connectivity needs no separate check: the algorithm has succeeded once V − 1 edges have been accepted.
  4. So the total is O(E log E), which is O(E log V) because log E = Θ(log V) for a simple graph.

Answer: Option (A) — the sorting, O(E log E).
