Common misconception. Naming the union–find operations as the bottleneck. Even the naive forest costs only O(E log V) there, and with compression the structure is almost free compared with the sort.

ALG-U5-S2-Q21MCQmedium

A disjoint-set forest uses union by rank (or by size) but no path compression. Starting from n singleton sets, what is the worst-case height of a tree?

  1. Θ(n)
  2. Θ(log n)
  3. Θ(α(n))
  4. Θ(√n)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Attaching the shorter tree under the taller one doubles the size of every node's tree whenever its depth grows by one, so depth is bounded by the logarithm of the set size.

Step by step.

  1. A node's depth increases only when its tree is hung under another tree, and union by rank does that only when the other tree is at least as large.
  2. So each time a node's depth grows by one, the size of the set containing it at least doubles.
  3. After k such events the set holds at least 2ᵏ elements, and a set has at most n elements, so k ≤ log₂ n.
  4. The bound is tight: repeatedly unioning equal-sized trees builds a tree of height ⌊log₂ n⌋. Path compression would improve the amortised cost to O(α(n)), but it is not used here.

Answer: Option (B) — Θ(log n).

Common misconception. Answering Θ(α(n)), which is the bound for the combination of union by rank and path compression. Union by rank alone gives O(log n) per operation; without either technique the height can be Θ(n).

ALG-U5-S2-Q22MCQhard

An undirected graph has distinct edge weights, and e is the heaviest edge on some cycle. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. e belongs to no minimum spanning tree
  2. e belongs to every minimum spanning tree
  3. e belongs to some minimum spanning trees but not to others
  4. removing e from the graph disconnects it
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The cycle property: for any cycle, the heaviest edge is not in any minimum spanning tree (with distinct weights it is excluded from every one of them). Equivalently, an edge that lies on a cycle is never a bridge.

Step by step.

  1. Let the cycle be C and suppose a minimum spanning tree T contains e.
  2. Deleting e splits T into two parts, and the rest of C must contain another edge f joining those two parts.
  3. Then T − e + f is a spanning tree of smaller total weight, because w(f) < w(e) — contradicting the minimality of T.
  4. So e is in no minimum spanning tree. Since e lies on a cycle, deleting it leaves the graph connected, so the last option is false too.

Answer: Option (A) — e belongs to no minimum spanning tree.

Common misconception. Choosing "some but not all", which is the correct answer only when weights may repeat. With distinct weights the heaviest edge of a cycle is excluded from every minimum spanning tree without exception.

ALG-U5-S2-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Kruskal's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It is correct by the cut property: a lightest edge across a cut is safe for some minimum spanning tree.
  2. It accepts an edge exactly when its two endpoints lie in different components of the edges accepted so far.
  3. On a disconnected graph it produces a minimum spanning forest.
  4. It works with negative weights only after every weight is increased by the same constant.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm scans edges in nondecreasing order and keeps an edge when it does not close a cycle. The disjoint-set structure is exactly the test "do these two endpoints lie in the same component?".

Step by step.

  1. (A) The components at any moment form a cut partition, and the next edge is the lightest one crossing between two of them, so the cut property applies. TRUE.
  2. (B) Different components means the edge does not close a cycle, and that is the acceptance test. TRUE.
  3. (C) The algorithm stops when no edge joins two components, leaving one tree per component — a minimum spanning forest. TRUE.
  4. (D) Adding a constant to every weight adds the same constant to every spanning tree of the same size, so the ordering of edges by weight is unchanged and no transformation is needed. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing that negative weights break a greedy algorithm. Shifting all weights by a constant does not even change the relative order of the edges, so Kruskal's behaviour is identical before and after the shift.

ALG-U5-S2-Q24NAThard

Eight singleton sets {1}, …, {8} are merged, in this order, by union by size with ties broken by attaching the root of the second argument under the root of the first: (1,2), (3,4), (5,6), (7,8), (1,3), (5,7), (1,5). What is the height of the resulting forest, that is, the number of edges on the longest path from a node to its root?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Union by size always hangs the smaller tree under the larger one; a tie is decided by the stated rule. The height grows only when two trees of equal height are merged.

Step by step.

  1. (1,2), (3,4), (5,6), (7,8) each merge two singletons: four trees of size 2 and height 1, with roots 1, 3, 5, 7.
  2. (1,3): sizes are equal, so root 3 goes under root 1. The tree {1,2,3,4} has size 4 and height 2, through 1 → 3 → 4.
  3. (5,7): equal sizes, so root 7 goes under root 5. The tree {5,6,7,8} has size 4 and height 2.
  4. (1,5): equal sizes again, so root 5 goes under root 1. The single tree of size 8 now has the path 1 → 5 → 7 → 8, which is three edges.
  5. Every other node is at depth at most 2, so the height is 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Answering 2 by assuming union by size keeps the height at log₂ 8 = 3 minus one, or forgetting that a tie still adds an edge to the depth. Trace the parent pointers: parent[8] = 7, parent[7] = 5, parent[5] = 1.

ALG-U5-S2-Q25NATeasy

A connected graph has 10 vertices and 18 edges. Kruskal's algorithm sorts the 18 edges and processes them in that order. How many of the 18 edges does it reject?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 9

Detailed solution

Concept. Kruskal's algorithm stops accepting once the accepted edges form a spanning tree, and a spanning tree on n vertices has exactly n − 1 edges. Every edge it examines before then is either accepted or rejected, so the two counts add up.

Step by step.

  1. A spanning tree on 10 vertices uses 10 − 1 = 9 edges.
  2. Kruskal's algorithm keeps exactly those 9 edges and rejects every other edge, because each rejected edge closes a cycle with edges already accepted.
  3. It examines all 18 edges only if necessary; in any case it ends with 9 accepted edges.
  4. Rejected = 18 − 9 = 9.

Answer: 9

Common misconception. Reporting 8, from n − 2, or 17, from rejecting everything except the first edge. Count the accepted edges first: a spanning tree always has exactly n − 1 of them, however many edges the graph has.

Unit 6 — Graph Algorithms: Shortest Paths · 50 solved questions (2026-11-01)
Dijkstra's algorithm 14
ALG-U6-Q01MCQeasy

A directed graph contains exactly one edge of weight −2 and has no negative-weight cycle. Dijkstra's algorithm is run from a source s that can reach that edge. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. it always returns correct shortest distances, because the graph has no negative-weight cycle
  2. it may report a distance that is larger than the true shortest distance
  3. it never terminates, because a label can keep decreasing
  4. it is correct whenever the graph is strongly connected
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Dijkstra's correctness rests on non-negativity. When the unsettled vertex with the smallest tentative label is settled, every alternative route to it must leave the settled set and therefore pays a non-negative edge, so no later relaxation can improve it. A negative edge breaks exactly that argument.

Step by step.

  1. Settlement is final in Dijkstra: once a vertex leaves the priority queue its label is never revisited.
  2. A route that is discovered later can still be cheaper if it uses the −2 edge, because the label already settled was computed without it.
  3. So the returned distance may exceed the true shortest distance. The algorithm still terminates, and it reports nothing about the negative edge.
  4. Absence of a negative cycle is the condition that makes Bellman–Ford correct, not Dijkstra.

Answer: Option (B) — the distance reported may be too large.

Common misconception. Candidates transfer the Bellman–Ford precondition ("no negative cycle is enough") to Dijkstra. Dijkstra needs non-negative weights; with a negative edge it can settle a vertex too early and never repair it.

ALG-U6-Q02MCQeasy

Dijkstra's algorithm is implemented with a binary min-heap and adjacency lists, and uses lazy deletion instead of decrease-key: when a shorter label is found the pair is pushed again, and stale entries are discarded when they are popped. On a graph with V vertices and E edges its running time is:

  1. Θ(V²)
  2. O(E log V)
  3. O(V log E)
  4. O(E²)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Each edge can cause at most one successful relaxation, so at most E pairs ever enter the heap. Every heap operation costs O(log E), and log E = O(log V²) = O(log V) for a simple graph.

Step by step.

  1. At most one push per edge → at most E pushes and E pops.
  2. Each push and each pop costs O(log E) with a binary heap.
  3. Stale entries are simply skipped on pop, which costs nothing extra.
  4. Total O(E log E), i.e. O(E log V). The Θ(V²) bound is the array implementation without a heap.

Answer: Option (B) — O(E log V).

Common misconception. The Θ(V²) answer is the matrix-and-array implementation. Mixing the two implementations up is the usual slip.

ALG-U6-Q03MCQeasy

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (4), s→b (1), b→a (2), a→c (5), b→c (8), c→d (2), a→d (10). Dijkstra's algorithm is run from s. What is the shortest distance from s to d?

  1. 7
  2. 8
  3. 10
  4. 13
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Dijkstra settles vertices in non-decreasing order of distance, so a vertex must be settled only after every cheaper route to it has been tried.

Step by step.

  1. Settle s = 0. Relax a = 4, b = 1.
  2. Settle b = 1. Relax a = min(4, 1 + 2) = 3, c = 1 + 8 = 9.
  3. Settle a = 3. Relax c = min(9, 3 + 5) = 8, d = 3 + 10 = 13.
  4. Settle c = 8. Relax d = min(13, 8 + 2) = 10.
  5. Settle d = 10.

Answer: Option (C) — 10, along s→b→a→c→d = 1 + 2 + 5 + 2.

Common misconception. 13 is the distance found the moment a is settled. Anyone who stops relaxing when d is first labelled reports 13; c is settled later and improves it.

ALG-U6-Q04MCQmedium

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (2), s→b (7), s→c (4), a→b (1), a→c (3), b→d (2), c→d (5), d→e (1). Dijkstra's algorithm is run from s. Which vertex is removed from the priority queue third? Count s as the first removal.

  1. a
  2. b
  3. c
  4. d
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. With non-negative weights the priority queue hands out vertices in non-decreasing order of their final distances, so the removal order is the distance order.

Step by step.

  1. Settle s = 0; labels a = 2, b = 7, c = 4.
  2. Settle a = 2; relax b = min(7, 3) = 3, c = min(4, 5) = 4.
  3. The queue now holds b = 3, c = 4, s→b already replaced, so b is settled next.
  4. Order: s(0), a(2), b(3), c(4), d(5), e(6).

Answer: Option (B) — b, with distance 3.

Common misconception. Settling c = 4 before b because c was labelled earlier (4 at the first step, when b showed 7). The queue orders by label, not by the moment a vertex was discovered.

ALG-U6-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Dijkstra's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. When every edge weight is non-negative, the distance label of a vertex is final at the moment it is removed from the priority queue.
  2. Implemented with a Fibonacci heap it runs in O(V log V + E) time.
  3. It can be used to detect a negative-weight cycle that is reachable from the source.
  4. On a directed acyclic graph, relaxing the edges in topological order is asymptotically faster in the worst case than running Dijkstra's algorithm.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Dijkstra is a label-setting algorithm: with non-negative weights the settled set always holds the true distances. Its cost is the cost of the priority queue, and on a DAG the topological order removes the queue entirely.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the settlement theorem, and it is exactly what non-negativity buys.
  2. (B) is the textbook bound: V extract-min operations at O(log V) each after the amortised O(1) decrease-key, plus E decrease-key operations.
  3. (C) is false: Dijkstra never re-examines a settled vertex, so it cannot see a cycle of negative total weight; it silently returns wrong distances.
  4. (D) is true: a DAG shortest-path sweep is Θ(V + E), which beats O(E log V) in the worst case.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Statement (C) is the trap. Negative-cycle detection needs the extra Bellman–Ford pass; Dijkstra has no mechanism to notice that a settled label could still be improved.

ALG-U6-Q06NATeasy

A weighted undirected graph on the vertices A–E has the edges AB 4, AC 1, CB 2, BD 5, CD 8, DE 3. Dijkstra's algorithm is run from A. What is the sum of the shortest distances from A to all five vertices, counting the distance to A itself as 0?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 23

Detailed solution

Concept. On an undirected graph every edge is usable in both directions, so each listed edge is two arcs of the same weight.

Step by step.

  1. Settle A = 0; labels B = 4, C = 1.
  2. Settle C = 1; relax B = min(4, 1 + 2) = 3, D = 1 + 8 = 9.
  3. Settle B = 3; relax D = min(9, 3 + 5) = 8.
  4. Settle D = 8; relax E = 8 + 3 = 11. Settle E = 11.
  5. Sum = 0 + 3 + 1 + 8 + 11 = 23.

Answer: 23

Common misconception. Taking AB = 4 as the distance to B gives 24. The path A→C→B costs 1 + 2 = 3 and is cheaper.

ALG-U6-Q07NAThard

A directed graph has the weighted edges A→B (1), A→C (2), B→C (1), B→D (3), C→D (2), A→D (5), D→E (1), C→E (6), B→E (10). How many distinct paths of minimum total weight lead from A to E?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Once the shortest distances are known, the number of shortest paths is counted in non-decreasing order of distance: a vertex inherits the count of every predecessor that lies on a tight edge, that is, one with d[u] + w(u,v) = d[v].

Step by step.

  1. Distances: A = 0, B = 1, C = min(2, 1 + 1) = 2, D = min(1 + 3, 2 + 2, 5) = 4, E = 4 + 1 = 5.
  2. Paths to C: A→C and A→B→C, both of weight 2 → count 2.
  3. Paths to D: A→B→D (4) and the two paths to C extended by C→D (4) → count 1 + 2 = 3. The direct edge A→D costs 5 and is not tight.
  4. Paths to E: only D→E is tight (5); C→E = 8 and B→E = 11 are not.
  5. So the count at E is the count at D = 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Counting only the paths that a single Dijkstra run happens to discover. The tie d(B) + w(B,C) = d(A) + w(A,C) = 2 is easy to miss, and it doubles the count below C.

ALG-U6B-Q01MCQeasy

Dijkstra's algorithm is run on a graph with V vertices and E edges. The priority queue is a Fibonacci heap, which supports decrease-key in amortised O(1). Which one of the following is the running time?

  1. O(E + V log V)
  2. O(V2)
  3. O(E log V)
  4. O(V E)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The cost of Dijkstra is decided by two operations on the priority queue: extracting the minimum, which happens once per vertex, and decreasing a key, which happens at most once per edge.

Step by step.

  1. Every vertex is extracted exactly once, at O(log V) per extraction, giving O(V log V).
  2. Every edge causes at most one successful relaxation, so at most E decrease-key operations at O(1) amortised each, giving O(E).
  3. Adding the two gives O(E + V log V).
  4. A binary heap would charge O(log V) per decrease-key and so give O(E log V).

Answer: Option (A) — O(E + V log V).

Common misconception. Quoting the binary-heap bound O(E log V) out of habit. The two bounds differ only in the cost of decrease-key, so the data structure has to be read off the question before the complexity is written down.

ALG-U6B-Q02MCQmedium

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (2), s→b (1), b→a (1), a→c (4), b→c (6), c→d (1), a→d (9). Dijkstra's algorithm is run from s. In which order are the vertices settled?

  1. s, b, a, c, d
  2. s, a, b, c, d
  3. s, b, a, d, c
  4. s, a, c, b, d
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Settlement order is driven by the tentative label, not by the order in which vertices happen to be written down. At each step the unsettled vertex with the smallest label leaves the queue, and only then are its outgoing edges relaxed.

Step by step.

  1. Settle s: d[a] = 2, d[b] = 1.
  2. Smallest label is b at 1. Relax: b→a gives 1 + 1 = 2, not smaller; b→c gives d[c] = 7.
  3. Next is a at 2. Relax: a→c gives 2 + 4 = 6 < 7, so d[c] = 6; a→d gives d[d] = 11.
  4. Next is c at 6. Relax: c→d gives 6 + 1 = 7 < 11, so d[d] = 7.
  5. Finally d at 7. Order: s, b, a, c, d.

Answer: Option (A) — s, b, a, c, d.

Common misconception. Settling a before b because the labels were initialised in that order, or because a comes first alphabetically. The queue is keyed on the current label, and b holds 1 against a's 2.

ALG-U6B-Q03MCQhard

Dijkstra's algorithm is modified so that the priority of a vertex u in the queue is d[u] + h(u) instead of d[u], where h(t) = 0 at the target t. The labels d[] themselves are updated exactly as before. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. The distance reported for t is always correct, because only the extraction order changes.
  2. If h overestimates the true remaining distance for some vertex, t can be extracted too early and a distance larger than the shortest is reported.
  3. The modification is incorrect unless h is identically zero.
  4. The modification is safe whenever every edge weight is equal.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Dijkstra's guarantee is a settlement guarantee: once a vertex leaves the queue its label is never revisited. Changing the priority therefore changes correctness, not merely the order of printing, because the moment of settlement is what freezes the answer.

Step by step.

  1. The label updates are untouched, so the numbers d[] still evolve by the same relaxation rule.
  2. What changes is which vertex is frozen next. If h is too large for some vertex u on a cheaper route, u is pushed behind vertices that are actually farther away.
  3. The target t can then reach the front while u is still unsettled, and its label is frozen at the value found so far.
  4. Hence the reported distance can exceed the true shortest distance. With an h that never overestimates, the answer stays correct.

Answer: Option (B) — an overestimating h can freeze t too early.

Common misconception. Treating the priority queue as a cosmetic detail. In Dijkstra and in every A*-style variant the extraction order is the proof of correctness, because settlement is final.

ALG-U6B-Q04MCQeasy

In the usual adjacency-list implementation of Dijkstra's algorithm, with a binary heap that supports decrease-key, how many times is a single directed edge relaxed in the worst case?

  1. once
  2. twice, once from each endpoint
  3. O(log V) times
  4. V − 1 times
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Dijkstra relaxes the outgoing edges of a vertex exactly once, at the moment that vertex is settled, and a vertex is settled exactly once.

Step by step.

  1. A vertex leaves the priority queue once, and its label is final from then on.
  2. Its outgoing adjacency list is scanned once, so each directed edge is tested once.
  3. Therefore each edge is relaxed once, and the total relaxation work is O(E).
  4. The V − 1 figure belongs to Bellman–Ford, which re-relaxes every edge in every pass.

Answer: Option (A) — once per edge.

Common misconception. Confusing Dijkstra with Bellman–Ford, or with a lazy-deletion implementation in which a stale entry may be pushed several times. Those stale pushes are queue entries, not fresh relaxations of the same edge.

ALG-U6B-Q16MSQmedium

Every edge weight is non-negative. Which of the following modifications still compute single-source shortest distances correctly? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Replacing the binary heap by an unsorted array that is scanned linearly for the minimum.
  2. Replacing the minimum by the maximum in the priority queue, in order to obtain longest paths.
  3. Running Dijkstra from s on the reversed graph, in order to obtain the distance from every vertex to s.
  4. Adding the same constant c > 0 to every edge and subtracting c times the number of edges at the end.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Correctness of Dijkstra comes from the greedy settlement rule on non-negative weights. Anything that preserves that rule, or reduces the problem to another instance of it, stays correct.

Step by step.

  1. An unsorted array still extracts the true minimum; it just costs O(V) per extraction, giving O(V2) overall. The answers are unchanged.
  2. Taking the maximum instead turns the algorithm into a longest-path heuristic, and longest paths are NP-hard on general graphs; the greedy settlement is invalid.
  3. Reversing every edge turns a path s → v into a path v → s of the same weight, so one run from s on the reversed graph yields the distances into s.
  4. Adding a constant changes paths with different edge counts by different amounts, so the ranking of paths changes and the correction is invalid.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating the priority queue as part of the algorithm's definition. Dijkstra needs some way to find the minimum, not a heap specifically; and offsetting all weights is a favourite trap because it looks like it should cancel.

ALG-U6B-Q21NATeasy

A directed graph with non-negative weights has the edges s→a (2), s→b (5), a→b (1), a→c (4), b→c (1). Dijkstra's algorithm is run from s. How many vertices other than s are settled before c is settled?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Settlement order is the order in which vertices leave the priority queue, which is the order of their final labels.

Step by step.

  1. Settle s: d[a] = 2, d[b] = 5.
  2. Settle a at 2: d[b] = 2 + 1 = 3 and d[c] = 2 + 4 = 6.
  3. Settle b at 3: d[c] = 3 + 1 = 4.
  4. Settle c at 4, so the vertices settled before it are a and b.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Counting s as one of the vertices settled before c. The question excludes s, and only a and b precede c.

ALG-U6B-Q22NATeasy

Dijkstra's algorithm is run from s on a connected graph with V = 9 vertices, and every vertex is reachable from s. Counting the extraction of s itself, how many times is the minimum extracted from the priority queue?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 9

Detailed solution

Concept. Each vertex is inserted once and extracted once, and a settled vertex is never reinserted.

Step by step.

  1. Initially s has label 0 and every other vertex has label ∞.
  2. The first extraction removes s, which settles it permanently.
  3. Each later extraction settles one more vertex, and connectivity guarantees that all nine become reachable and are eventually extracted.
  4. So the number of extractions equals the number of vertices, 9.

Answer: 9

Common misconception. Reporting 8 by forgetting that s itself is extracted, or reporting more than 9 by counting the stale duplicate entries of a lazy-deletion heap. Extractions of vertices are what the question asks for.

Bellman–Ford algorithm 12
ALG-U6-Q08MCQeasy

Bellman–Ford relaxes every edge once per pass. In the worst case, how many passes are needed before the distance labels are guaranteed correct on a graph with V vertices that has no negative-weight cycle reachable from the source?

  1. V − 1
  2. V
  3. V + 1
  4. 2V
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. After pass i, every label is at most the weight of the cheapest walk with at most i edges. A shortest path in a graph without a negative cycle is simple, so it has at most V − 1 edges.

Step by step.

  1. Pass 1 handles all shortest paths of one edge, pass 2 those of two edges, and so on.
  2. A simple path on V vertices uses at most V − 1 edges.
  3. Hence V − 1 passes suffice, and the bound is tight: a chain of V vertices needs all of them.
  4. The extra V-th pass is used only for cycle detection.

Answer: Option (A) — V − 1 passes.

Common misconception. Answering V because there are V vertices. Vertices and edges on a path differ by one.

ALG-U6-Q09MCQeasy

After the V − 1 passes of Bellman–Ford, how is a negative-weight cycle reachable from the source detected?

  1. run one additional pass over all edges; if any edge can still be relaxed, such a cycle exists
  2. check whether every vertex has a finite distance label
  3. check whether the sum of all edge weights is negative
  4. check whether the graph contains a cycle at all
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. In a graph whose reachable part has no negative cycle, every edge satisfies d[v] ≤ d[u] + w(u,v) after V − 1 passes. A negative cycle makes some edge permanently relaxable, because walking the cycle lowers the label without bound.

Step by step.

  1. Run the relaxation loop once more over the whole edge list.
  2. If some edge (u,v) still satisfies d[v] > d[u] + w(u,v), a negative cycle is reachable from the source.
  3. A negative cycle whose vertices are all unreachable from the source leaves every label untouched, so it is not reported — the detection is source-relative.
  4. The other three tests say nothing about cycles: a finite label set only means the graph is connected enough, and a negative total edge weight does not imply a negative cycle.

Answer: Option (A) — one extra pass, and any relaxable edge proves the cycle.

Common misconception. Believing that a negative sum of weights, or any cycle at all, is the signal. Only a still-relaxable edge after the final pass is.

ALG-U6-Q10MCQmedium

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (4), s→b (5), a→b (−3), a→c (6), b→c (2), b→d (4), c→d (1). Bellman–Ford is run from s. What is the shortest distance from s to d?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 8
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Bellman–Ford tolerates negative edges: it keeps relaxing until no pass changes a label, so a negative edge can lower a label that was already set in an earlier pass.

Step by step.

  1. After pass 1: a = 4, b = min(5, 4 − 3) = 1, c = 1 + 2 = 3, d = min(1 + 4, 3 + 1) = 4.
  2. Pass 2 changes nothing: a = min(4, …) = 4, b = min(1, 4 − 3) = 1, c = min(3, 4 + 6) = 3, d = min(4, 1 + 4, 3 + 1) = 4.
  3. The answer is 4, along s→a→b→d = 4 − 3 + 4 or s→a→b→c→d = 4 − 3 + 2 + 1.

Answer: Option (B) — 4.

Common misconception. 5 is s→b→d and ignores that the negative edge makes b cheaper through a. 8 is the sum of the raw weights of the longest route.

ALG-U6-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the Bellman–Ford algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It runs in O(V E) time.
  2. It handles negative edge weights correctly as long as no negative-weight cycle is reachable from the source.
  3. It reports a negative-weight cycle that is not reachable from the source.
  4. After V − 1 passes, every edge satisfies d[v] ≤ d[u] + w(u,v) when no reachable negative cycle exists.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Bellman–Ford is a dynamic program over the number of edges: pass i certifies all shortest paths of at most i edges. Its cost is one full edge scan per pass.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: V − 1 passes, each scanning E edges.
  2. (B) is true: negative edges are fine, since nothing is ever declared final.
  3. (C) is false: vertices unreachable from the source keep an infinite label, so no relaxation inside that part of the graph is ever performed and the cycle goes unnoticed.
  4. (D) is true: it is the optimality condition that the final pass certifies.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. (C) inverts the source-relative nature of the algorithm. Bellman–Ford answers single-source questions only; a negative cycle in another component is invisible to it.

ALG-U6-Q12MCQhard

A connected undirected graph on 6 vertices has 8 edges, exactly one of which has negative weight, and that edge lies on a cycle. Bellman–Ford is run from any source. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. it returns correct distances, because no cycle of negative total weight exists
  2. it reports a negative-weight cycle, because the negative edge can be traversed in both directions and so forms a cycle of negative total weight
  3. it returns correct distances only when the negative edge is a bridge
  4. it reports a negative cycle only when the graph is not a tree
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An undirected edge of weight w becomes two directed arcs, both of weight w. The 2-cycle u→v→u therefore has total weight 2w, which is negative whenever w is.

Step by step.

  1. Model each undirected edge as two arcs in opposite directions, the standard reduction.
  2. The negative edge {u,v} contributes arcs u→v and v→u, both negative.
  3. Walking u→v→u costs 2w < 0, a negative-weight cycle of length 2 that is reachable as soon as one of its ends is reachable.
  4. The extra pass therefore finds a relaxable edge and reports the cycle.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Thinking that a single negative edge is harmless because it is not part of a "real" cycle. In an undirected graph every edge is its own 2-cycle, so any negative edge produces a negative cycle — which is why undirected shortest paths assume non-negative weights.

ALG-U6-Q13NATmedium

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (2), s→b (3), b→a (−1), a→c (3), c→d (1), a→d (7), b→d (6), d→e (−2). There is no negative-weight cycle. Bellman–Ford is run from s. What is the shortest distance from s to e?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. A shortest path may be improved at a later pass when a negative edge lies behind it, so all passes must be completed before the labels are read.

Step by step.

  1. Pass 1: a = 2, b = 3, c = 5, d = min(2 + 7, 3 + 6, 5 + 1) = 6, e = 6 − 2 = 4.
  2. Pass 2: b→a gives a = min(2, 3 − 1) = 2, unchanged; every other label is unchanged.
  3. The path is s→b→a→c→d→e = 3 − 1 + 3 + 1 − 2 = 4.
  4. Check the alternatives: s→a→c→d→e = 2 + 3 + 1 − 2 = 4, the same value; s→a→d→e = 2 + 7 − 2 = 7.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. 7, from s→a→d→e. It is the route a single forward pass along the listed edge order might suggest if the c→d shortcut is overlooked.

ALG-U6B-Q05MCQeasy

What is the running time of Bellman–Ford on a graph with V vertices and E edges?

  1. O(V E)
  2. O(E log V)
  3. O(V2)
  4. O(V + E)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Bellman–Ford pays for its tolerance of negative weights with repetition: it relaxes every edge in every pass, and it needs V − 1 passes.

Step by step.

  1. One pass relaxes all E edges, which costs O(E).
  2. V − 1 passes are needed, because a shortest simple path has at most V − 1 edges.
  3. The product is O(V E); the extra detection pass does not change it.
  4. A DAG avoids the repetition altogether, because one pass in topological order suffices.

Answer: Option (A) — O(V E).

Common misconception. Writing O(E log V) because that is the familiar Dijkstra bound. Bellman–Ford has no priority queue at all; its cost is passes times edges.

ALG-U6B-Q06MCQmedium

Bellman–Ford is allowed to stop as soon as one complete pass over all edges performs no relaxation. Which one of the following does that tell you?

  1. The labels are final, and no negative-weight cycle is reachable from the source.
  2. A negative-weight cycle exists somewhere in the graph.
  3. The graph is a DAG.
  4. The source cannot reach every vertex.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A pass with no relaxation is a fixed point of the relaxation operator. Distances have stopped improving, and a reachable negative cycle is precisely the thing that would make them improve forever.

Step by step.

  1. If a pass relaxes nothing, every edge already satisfies d[v] ≤ d[u] + w(u,v).
  2. These are the shortest-path optimality conditions, so the labels are already correct.
  3. A negative cycle reachable from s would let some label keep decreasing, so every pass would relax at least one edge.
  4. No relaxation therefore rules out a reachable negative cycle, while a graph with unreachable vertices is untouched by the argument.

Answer: Option (A) — labels final, and no reachable negative cycle.

Common misconception. Reading early termination as evidence of a negative cycle. The signals are opposite: a negative cycle is announced by a pass that still relaxes after the V − 1 required passes.

ALG-U6B-Q07MCQhard

Bellman–Ford is run from s on a directed graph. After the V − 1 passes, one further pass still relaxes some edge. Which one of the following is the correct conclusion?

  1. There is a negative-weight cycle reachable from s.
  2. There is a negative-weight cycle somewhere in the graph, whether or not s can reach it.
  3. There is at least one negative-weight edge.
  4. The source itself lies on a negative-weight cycle.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The detection pass can only see what the labels can see. Labels only ever travel along edges reachable from s, so a cycle in an unreachable region leaves them untouched.

Step by step.

  1. After V − 1 passes, every shortest simple path is already realised, because a simple path has at most V − 1 edges.
  2. If an edge can still be relaxed, some better walk exists that is not simple, which means it repeats a vertex.
  3. Repeating a vertex profitably is exactly a cycle of negative total weight, and the walk is built from edges reachable from s.
  4. A negative cycle elsewhere cannot affect any label, so it produces no relaxation and is invisible to this test.

Answer: Option (A) — a negative-weight cycle reachable from s.

Common misconception. Choosing the wider statement that a negative cycle exists anywhere. Reachability is part of the conclusion; and the cycle need not pass through s itself, only be reachable from it.

ALG-U6B-Q08MCQeasy

Bellman–Ford has to be used instead of Dijkstra's algorithm when:

  1. edge weights may be negative
  2. the graph is dense
  3. the graph is undirected
  4. all-pairs distances are needed
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The only structural assumption Dijkstra makes about the weights is that they are non-negative. Bellman–Ford drops that assumption and pays for it in running time.

Step by step.

  1. Dijkstra settles a vertex permanently when its label is smallest, which is safe only if no later route can be cheaper.
  2. A negative edge can make a later route cheaper, so the settlement argument collapses.
  3. Bellman–Ford never settles anything permanently; it keeps relaxing until nothing improves, so negative edges are harmless as long as no negative cycle is reachable.
  4. Density, undirectedness and all-pairs work are handled by Dijkstra as well, so none of them forces the switch.

Answer: Option (A) — when weights may be negative.

Common misconception. Believing that an undirected graph or a dense graph needs Bellman–Ford. Neither matters: what matters is the sign of the weights, and a negative undirected edge is itself a negative 2-cycle.

ALG-U6B-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the passes of Bellman–Ford are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. After the k-th pass, every vertex whose shortest path uses at most k edges already carries its final label.
  2. The order in which the edges are relaxed inside a pass can change how many passes are needed, but not the labels after V − 1 passes.
  3. A distance label never increases during the algorithm.
  4. If the graph is a DAG, V − 1 passes are still necessary.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Bellman–Ford is a fixed-point iteration. Its progress is measured by how many edges a path uses, which is why the pass count is tied to path length rather than to the vertex count.

Step by step.

  1. Induction on k: a path of at most k edges is a path of at most k − 1 edges followed by one edge, and that last edge is relaxed during the k-th pass.
  2. Relaxation is a minimum, so a label can only fall; the sequence of labels is monotone decreasing.
  3. Relaxing in a favourable order, for instance topological order, can settle everything in one pass, but after V − 1 passes the result is the same either way.
  4. A DAG does not need V − 1 passes, because topological order makes a single pass sufficient.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing the pass count is a property of the graph rather than of the worst-case path length, or thinking a favourable relaxation order can change the final answer instead of only the number of passes.

ALG-U6B-Q23NATmedium

A directed graph has the weighted edges s→a (6), s→b (7), a→b (8), a→c (5), a→d (−4), b→c (−3), b→d (9), c→d (7), d→c (2), and there is no negative-weight cycle. Bellman–Ford is run from s. What is the shortest distance from s to d?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Bellman–Ford finds shortest walks; when there is no negative cycle, the shortest walk is a simple path and the labels converge to the shortest distances.

Step by step.

  1. From s: d[a] = 6, d[b] = 7.
  2. The negative edge pays off immediately: a→d gives 6 + (−4) = 2.
  3. The direct alternatives are worse: s→b→d is 7 + 9 = 16, and a→c→d needs d[c], which ends at 4 through b→c, giving 4 + 7 = 11.
  4. So the distance to d is 2, reached in a single hop from a.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Refusing the negative edge because it is the only one, or continuing to improve d[d] through d→c→d. The cycle c→d→c has weight 7 + 2 = 9 > 0, so it cannot be used to lower the distance.

Shortest paths in a DAG 12
ALG-U6-Q14MCQeasy

Single-source shortest paths in a directed acyclic graph whose edges may be negative can be computed in:

  1. Θ(V + E)
  2. Θ(E log V)
  3. Θ(V E)
  4. Θ(V³)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological order guarantees that when a vertex is processed every path into it has already been accounted for, so one relaxation per edge finishes the job — and no negative edge can invalidate a label because no label is ever declared final before all its predecessors are done.

Step by step.

  1. Topologically sort the DAG: Θ(V + E).
  2. Set d[s] = 0 and every other label to ∞.
  3. Scan the vertices in topological order and relax every outgoing edge once: Θ(V + E).
  4. Total Θ(V + E), the best possible since the input must be read.

Answer: Option (A) — Θ(V + E).

Common misconception. Assuming negative edges force Bellman–Ford. They force Θ(V E) only when cycles can exist; a DAG has none, so the topological order replaces the repeated passes.

ALG-U6-Q15MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE about longest simple paths?

  1. they can be found in polynomial time in every graph
  2. they can be found in O(V + E) time in a DAG, but the problem is NP-hard in a general graph
  3. they are always found by negating every weight and running Dijkstra's algorithm
  4. they equal the shortest path computed on the negated weights in every graph
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Negating the weights turns a longest-path problem into a shortest-path problem, and that transform is faithful exactly when the negated graph has no negative cycle — which a DAG guarantees. In a general graph a longest simple path cannot be certified edge by edge because a cheaper walk may repeat vertices.

Step by step.

  1. In a DAG every walk is a path, so the negated graph has no negative cycle and the topological-order sweep returns the longest path in O(V + E).
  2. In a general graph, longest path contains Hamiltonian path as a special case, which is NP-complete.
  3. Negating the weights and running Dijkstra is invalid: negating makes the weights negative, and Dijkstra requires non-negative weights.
  4. Negating and running Bellman–Ford returns the longest walk, which is unbounded when a positive cycle exists, not the longest simple path.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that "negate and run a shortest-path algorithm" is always valid. It is valid only when the negated graph has no negative cycle, and it computes longest walks, not longest simple paths.

ALG-U6-Q16MCQeasy

A DAG has the weighted edges A→B (2), A→C (5), B→C (1), B→D (6), C→D (2), D→E (3), C→E (9). What is the shortest distance from A to E?

  1. 5
  2. 8
  3. 12
  4. 14
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Relax the edges in topological order; each edge is examined once and the label it produces is already based on the best route to its tail.

Step by step.

  1. Topological order A, B, C, D, E.
  2. A = 0; B = 2, C = 5.
  3. C = min(5, 2 + 1) = 3.
  4. D = min(2 + 6, 3 + 2) = 5.
  5. E = min(5 + 3, 3 + 9) = 8.

Answer: Option (B) — 8, along A→B→C→D→E = 2 + 1 + 2 + 3.

Common misconception. 12 is A→C→E, the route taken by reading the edges in the order they are listed. The topological sweep is what exposes A→B→C as the cheaper way into C.

ALG-U6-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about shortest paths in a DAG are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In a topological sweep every edge is relaxed exactly once.
  2. Any valid topological order of the DAG produces the correct distances.
  3. The method fails as soon as one edge has a negative weight.
  4. Replacing min by max in the same sweep computes the longest path.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Correctness needs only that every edge leaves an already-processed vertex; any topological order has that property, and the number of edge relaxations is fixed at E whatever the order.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the sweep visits each vertex once and each outgoing edge once.
  2. (B) is true: topological orders differ, but each one places every tail before its head.
  3. (C) is false: acyclicity is what makes the sweep valid; negative weights are harmless because a label is never frozen before all its predecessors are processed.
  4. (D) is true: in a DAG every walk is a path, so the max relaxation over a topological order gives the longest path.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. (C) is the reflex that negative weights always mean Bellman–Ford. That reflex is about cycles, not about signs.

ALG-U6-Q18MSQhard

Which of the following statements about paths in a DAG are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The number of distinct paths from the source to every vertex can be counted in one topological sweep in O(V + E) time.
  2. A DAG on n ≥ 2 vertices has at most 2^(n−2) distinct paths from a fixed source to a fixed target.
  3. If a DAG has a unique topological order then it has exactly one path from its first vertex to its last.
  4. A longest path in a DAG can be found by negating every weight and running the topological-order shortest-path sweep.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A DAG supports counting and extremal path queries in one pass because every path into a vertex arrives from vertices that are already finished in topological order; the counts and the labels therefore compose.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: set cnt[s] = 1 and add cnt[u] into cnt[v] on every edge u→v processed in topological order.
  2. (B) is true: the extreme case is the tournament-like DAG in which every pair i < j carries the edge i→j; a path is then any subset of the n − 2 intermediate vertices, so there are 2^(n−2) of them.
  3. (C) is false: the DAG v₁→v₂, v₂→v₃, v₁→v₃ has the unique order v₁, v₂, v₃ but two paths from v₁ to v₃.
  4. (D) is true: negating gives a DAG with no negative cycle, so the topological sweep returns the longest path.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. (C) confuses a unique topological order with a unique path. A unique order only forces the edges vᵢ→vᵢ₊₁ to exist; extra forward edges create extra paths without creating extra orders.

ALG-U6-Q19NATmedium

A DAG has the weighted edges A→B (3), A→C (2), B→D (4), C→D (1), D→E (5), B→E (1), C→E (8). What is the total weight of a longest path from A to E?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 12

Detailed solution

Concept. Longest paths in a DAG obey the same topological recurrence with max in place of min: L[v] = max over edges u→v of (L[u] + w(u,v)).

Step by step.

  1. Topological order A, B, C, D, E.
  2. L[A] = 0, L[B] = 3, L[C] = 2.
  3. L[D] = max(3 + 4, 2 + 1) = 7.
  4. L[E] = max(7 + 5, 3 + 1, 2 + 8) = max(12, 4, 10) = 12.

Answer: 12, along A→B→D→E = 3 + 4 + 5.

Common misconception. 10, from A→C→E = 2 + 8. The heavier single edge does not compensate for the longer route through B and D.

ALG-U6B-Q09MCQeasy

Single-source shortest paths in a DAG are computed by relaxing the outgoing edges of each vertex once, in topological order. Why is one pass enough?

  1. When a vertex is processed, all its predecessors are already final, so its label cannot improve afterwards.
  2. Because a DAG has at most V − 1 edges.
  3. Because all weights in a DAG are non-negative.
  4. Because a DAG has a unique topological order.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A topological order lists every vertex after all of its predecessors. That single property converts the repeated relaxation of Bellman–Ford into a single sweep.

Step by step.

  1. In topological order, every edge points from an earlier vertex to a later one.
  2. So when a vertex is reached in the sweep, every path into it has already been accounted for, and its label is the true shortest distance.
  3. Relaxing its outgoing edges then passes that final value on, and no later step can lower it.
  4. One sweep over all edges therefore suffices, which is O(V + E).

Answer: Option (A) — predecessors are final before the vertex is processed.

Common misconception. Assuming a unique topological order is needed. Any valid topological order works, and DAG weights may perfectly well be negative; acyclicity, not the sign of the weights, is what makes one pass enough.

ALG-U6B-Q10MCQmedium

The DAG shortest-path sweep is run twice on the same DAG, using two different topological orders. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. Both runs produce the same distance for every vertex.
  2. The two runs may produce different distances for some vertex.
  3. Only the lexicographically smallest topological order gives correct distances.
  4. The second run must start from the labels left by the first run.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Correctness of the sweep rests on the order property shared by every topological order, namely that each vertex follows all of its predecessors, and not on which particular order is used.

Step by step.

  1. Take any topological order; every edge still points forward.
  2. Induction on position: the first vertex has no predecessor, so its label is final.
  3. If all predecessors of a vertex are final when it is reached, its label is final too, because every path into it ends with an edge from a predecessor.
  4. So both runs compute the same function, and the distances agree vertex by vertex.

Answer: Option (A) — the distances are identical.

Common misconception. Thinking the order is part of the answer. A DAG with two incomparable vertices has several valid orders, and the algorithm is correct on all of them; only the sequence of processing differs.

ALG-U6B-Q11MCQhard

In a DAG let d[v] be the shortest distance from s to v, and let e[v] be the smallest number of edges among all shortest paths from s to v. Which pair of recurrences is correct?

  1. d[v] = min over (u,v) of d[u] + w(u,v), and e[v] = 1 + min{ e[u] : u attains that minimum }
  2. d[v] = min over (u,v) of d[u] + w(u,v), and e[v] = 1 + min{ e[u] : u is any predecessor of v }
  3. d[v] = max over (u,v) of d[u] + w(u,v), and e[v] = 1 + min{ e[u] : u attains that maximum }
  4. d[v] = min over (u,v) of d[u] + w(u,v), and e[v] = d[v] divided by the mean edge weight on the path
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two quantities are being optimised, and they are not independent: the edge count is only meaningful among paths that already achieve the minimum weight. A lexicographic pair (distance, edges) has to be minimised component by component, in that order.

Step by step.

  1. A shortest path to v ends with some edge (u,v) where d[u] + w(u,v) is minimal, so the first recurrence is the usual one.
  2. Among those minimising predecessors, the path with the fewest edges is the one whose prefix already had the fewest edges.
  3. Hence e[v] = 1 + min{e[u] : u attains the minimum}, and the two values are carried together through the sweep.
  4. Taking the minimum over all predecessors, as in the second option, would count an edge into v from a vertex that is not on any shortest path.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Minimising the edge count and the weight separately. A predecessor with a small e[u] may be reached by a heavier route, so the count must be minimised only over the predecessors that attain the minimum distance.

ALG-U6B-Q12MCQmedium

The DAG shortest-path sweep is applied to a directed graph that contains a cycle. What happens?

  1. No topological order exists, so the precondition of the method fails and the sweep is not defined.
  2. The sweep still works provided the cycle has non-negative total weight.
  3. The sweep reports the negative-weight cycle.
  4. The sweep works for every vertex outside the cycle.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The sweep is not an algorithm in its own right; it is an algorithm given a topological order. If no such order exists, the input to the sweep does not exist either.

Step by step.

  1. A topological order requires that every edge point forward, which is possible only if the digraph is acyclic.
  2. With a cycle, no linear order can place each of its vertices after all its predecessors.
  3. So the sweep has nothing to iterate over, whatever the signs of the weights.
  4. For graphs with cycles the alternatives are Dijkstra (non-negative weights) or Bellman–Ford (any weights, no reachable negative cycle).

Answer: Option (A) — the precondition fails and the sweep is undefined.

Common misconception. Believing the sweep survives a cycle when the weights are non-negative. Acyclicity is required to produce the order at all; non-negativity is a different condition that belongs to Dijkstra.

ALG-U6B-Q18MSQhard

Which of the following statements about shortest paths in directed acyclic graphs are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. With negative weights, relaxing edges in topological order needs one pass, where Bellman–Ford would need V − 1.
  2. A DAG can contain a negative-weight cycle.
  3. The DAG sweep detects a negative-weight cycle automatically.
  4. Reversing every edge and running the sweep from t gives, for each vertex v, the shortest distance from v to t.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Acyclicity is a structural condition, and it is strictly stronger than the absence of a negative cycle. It both shortens the computation and removes the failure mode that Bellman–Ford has to watch for.

Step by step.

  1. One topological sweep visits each edge once, and every path is realised in that sweep, so one pass replaces V − 1.
  2. A cycle of any weight contradicts acyclicity, so a DAG contains no negative cycle at all.
  3. Because no cycle can exist, the sweep has nothing to detect and no detection step is built into it.
  4. Reversing all edges maps paths v → t onto paths t → v of equal weight, and the reversed graph is still a DAG, so the same sweep gives distances to t.

Answer: Options (A) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the sweep doubles as a cycle detector. It never checks for cycles; it simply cannot be given a topological order when one exists, which is a different failure.

ALG-U6B-Q24NATeasy

A DAG has the edges A→B (1), A→C (4), B→C (2), B→D (5), C→D (1). How many edges lie on a shortest path from A to D?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. A path's weight and a path's length are different quantities. The shortest path is the one of least weight, and its edge count has to be read off that particular path.

Step by step.

  1. Enumerate the paths from A to D: A→B→D weighs 1 + 5 = 6; A→C→D weighs 4 + 1 = 5; A→B→C→D weighs 1 + 2 + 1 = 4.
  2. The least weight is 4, attained by A→B→C→D.
  3. That path uses the edges A→B, B→C and C→D.
  4. So the shortest path has 3 edges, even though the two-edge path is a plausible guess.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Answering 2 because a two-edge path looks 'more direct'. The extra hop through B→C is cheaper than the long A→C edge, so the shortest path is the longer one in edges.

Transitive closure & Floyd–Warshall 12
ALG-U6-Q20MCQeasy

The transitive closure of a directed graph with V vertices and E edges is computed by running a breadth-first search from every vertex, with the graph stored as adjacency lists. The running time is:

  1. Θ(V + E)
  2. Θ(V E)
  3. Θ(V(V + E))
  4. Θ(V³)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. One search from a fixed source visits each vertex and each edge at most once, costing Θ(V + E). Repeating it for every source multiplies that by V.

Step by step.

  1. A single breadth-first search is Θ(V + E).
  2. There are V possible sources.
  3. Total Θ(V (V + E)), which is Θ(V²) on a sparse graph and Θ(V³) only when the graph is dense.
  4. Floyd–Warshall instead pays Θ(V³) regardless of E.

Answer: Option (C) — Θ(V(V + E)).

Common misconception. Reporting Θ(V³) out of habit. On a sparse graph the V searches are strictly better than Floyd–Warshall; the two agree only for dense graphs.

ALG-U6-Q21MCQmedium

Floyd–Warshall is extended with a matrix next[i][j] that stores the vertex that follows i on a shortest path from i to j. Given i and j, the path is recovered by:

  1. following next[i][j], then next[next[i][j]][j], and so on, until j is reached
  2. reading the iteration k at which d[i][j] was last updated and printing that k
  3. scanning row i of the distance matrix for its smallest entry and repeating
  4. reading the path off the diagonal, because the diagonal stores the predecessor of every vertex
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A shortest path is a sequence of hops, and one stored successor per ordered pair is enough to walk it: each step consumes one hop, so the walk costs the number of edges on the path.

Step by step.

  1. Initialise next[i][j] = j for every edge i→j that exists.
  2. When the update d[i][j] > d[i][k] + d[k][j] fires, set next[i][j] = next[i][k].
  3. To print the path, start at i and repeatedly move to next[cur][j] until j is reached.
  4. The stored matrix is Θ(V²), the same order as the distance matrix itself.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Storing only k per update. The intermediate vertex of the last improvement does not describe the whole path; the successor pointer does.

ALG-U6-Q22MCQmedium

In the Floyd–Warshall recurrence d[i][j] = min(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]) the operator min is replaced by max. The resulting entry equals:

  1. the weight of a longest simple path from i to j
  2. the weight of a maximum-weight walk from i to j, which is not the longest simple path and which has no finite value when a positive-weight cycle lies on such a walk
  3. the weight of a longest simple path in every graph, because max distributes over concatenation
  4. the same value as the shortest path whenever all weights are negative
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. The (min, +) pair works because shortest paths decompose at a vertex without repeating it. The pair (max, +) composes walks, and a walk may revisit vertices, so the result is not a simple path.

Step by step.

  1. A max recurrence combines the best walk into k with the best walk out of k, and nothing forbids those two walks from sharing vertices.
  2. The concatenation is therefore a walk, not necessarily a simple path, and it may be heavier than any simple path.
  3. If a positive-weight cycle is reachable on such a walk, no maximum exists at all.
  4. Detecting this needs a positive-cycle check, exactly as the min version needs a negative-cycle check.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming the two recurrences are symmetric. Longest simple path is NP-hard in general, so no Θ(V³) recurrence can compute it; the max version solves a different, easier problem.

ALG-U6-Q23MCQhard

Let d be the matrix produced by Floyd–Warshall from the usual initialisation, and let T be a transitive-closure matrix. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. T[i][j] = 1 exactly when d[i][j] < ∞, and this already gives the reflexive closure, because the diagonal is initialised to 0
  2. T[i][j] = 1 exactly when d[i][j] < ∞ gives the irreflexive closure, so the diagonal must then be cleared
  3. T[i][j] = 1 exactly when d[i][j] ≤ 0, because only non-positive distances correspond to a path
  4. T cannot be derived from d, because d stores weights and T stores booleans
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Floyd–Warshall initialises d[i][i] = 0 for every vertex, so a vertex always reaches itself with a path of length 0 and the diagonal stays finite. The test "finite distance" therefore returns the reflexive closure directly.

Step by step.

  1. Initialise d[i][i] = 0, d[i][j] = w(i,j) when the edge exists, and ∞ otherwise.
  2. After the run d[i][j] < ∞ exactly when a path from i to j exists, including the empty path i = j.
  3. So T[i][j] = 1 ⇔ d[i][j] < ∞ is the reflexive transitive closure; no diagonal fix-up is needed.
  4. For the strict closure, set T[i][i] = 0 and then restore it only for vertices that lie on a cycle, which is detected by d[i][i] < 0 or by a separate reachability test.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Clearing the diagonal "because a vertex cannot reach itself". In the reflexive closure it can, by the path of length 0, and Floyd–Warshall has already encoded that as d[i][i] = 0.

ALG-U6-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the transitive closure are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Running a breadth-first search from every vertex computes it in Θ(V(V + E)) time on adjacency lists.
  2. The transitive closure of an undirected graph is a symmetric matrix.
  3. Floyd–Warshall computes it in Θ(V³) time whatever the number of edges.
  4. In the transitive closure of a DAG on V vertices every row contains at most V − 1 ones.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Reachability is the (OR, AND) version of shortest paths: the same three-loop structure works, and the cost of the two methods depends on density.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: V searches of cost Θ(V + E) each.
  2. (B) is true: an undirected edge can be traversed both ways, so reachability is a symmetric relation.
  3. (C) is true: the triple loop performs V³ constant-time steps independent of E.
  4. (D) is false: in the DAG 1→2, 1→3, …, 1→V the first row is all ones, that is V ones, because the vertex reaches every other vertex and itself.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. (D) is the trap: the reflexive entry at (i,i) counts too, so a row can be entirely ones.

ALG-U6-Q25NAThard

A directed graph on the vertices 1–5 has the edges 1→2, 2→3, 3→1, 3→4, 4→5, 5→4, every edge having weight 1. Floyd–Warshall is run to completion. How many of the 25 entries of the final distance matrix are finite, that is, not ∞?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 19

Detailed solution

Concept. A finite entry means reachability, so the question is a transitive-closure count. Vertices that lie on a common cycle reach each other, and the diagonal is finite for every vertex because d[i][i] = 0.

Step by step.

  1. The cycles 1→2→3→1 and 4→5→4 put the vertices into two groups: {1,2,3} and {4,5}.
  2. Inside {1,2,3} every vertex reaches both others, and 3 also reaches 4 and 5.
  3. So 1, 2 and 3 each reach 4 vertices other than themselves: 12 ordered pairs with distinct ends.
  4. Inside {4,5}: 4 reaches 5 and 5 reaches 4, which adds 2 pairs. Nothing reaches 1, 2 or 3 from {4,5}.
  5. Distinct-end pairs = 12 + 2 = 14; add the 5 diagonal entries: 14 + 5 = 19.

Answer: 19

Common misconception. Forgetting the diagonal, which gives 14, or assuming the whole graph is one strongly connected component, which would give all 25.

ALG-U6B-Q13MCQeasy

Floyd–Warshall computes all-pairs shortest paths on a graph with V vertices. What is its running time?

  1. O(V3)
  2. O(V2)
  3. O(V E log V)
  4. O(V + E)
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The algorithm has three nested loops over the vertex set: an intermediate vertex and the two ends of the pair being improved.

Step by step.

  1. For each intermediate vertex k, every ordered pair (i,j) is updated.
  2. That is V choices of k times V2 pairs, so O(V3) work.
  3. Each update is a comparison and an addition, so the constant is small, but the order stands.
  4. The advantage over V runs of Dijkstra is that negative weights are allowed.

Answer: Option (A) — O(V3).

Common misconception. Answering O(V2) because the output matrix has V2 entries. The output size is not the running time; every entry is revisited once per intermediate vertex.

ALG-U6B-Q14MCQmedium

Floyd–Warshall has run to completion on a directed graph whose edge weights may be negative. How is a negative-weight cycle detected?

  1. Some diagonal entry d[i][i] is negative.
  2. Some off-diagonal entry d[i][j] is negative.
  3. The diagonal is no longer all zeros.
  4. Floyd–Warshall cannot detect it; Bellman–Ford must be run instead.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A negative cycle through i is exactly a walk from i back to i of negative total weight, and the diagonal entry is precisely the cheapest such walk.

Step by step.

  1. Initially d[i][i] = 0 and every cycle is built up by the recurrence.
  2. After the algorithm finishes, d[i][i] is the minimum weight of a walk from i to i.
  3. That minimum is negative exactly when a negative cycle passes through i.
  4. So a negative diagonal entry anywhere certifies a negative cycle, and it costs no extra work.

Answer: Option (A) — a negative diagonal entry.

Common misconception. Scanning the off-diagonal entries. Negative off-diagonal distances are perfectly normal when some edges are negative, so they prove nothing; only the diagonal isolates a cycle.

ALG-U6B-Q15MCQmedium

Floyd–Warshall is adapted to find maximum-capacity (bottleneck) paths between all pairs, with the update c[i][j] = max(c[i][j], min(c[i][k], c[k][j])). Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. The update is correct: the pair (max, min) plays the role that (min, +) plays for shortest paths.
  2. The update is wrong, because min does not distribute over max.
  3. The update is correct only when every capacity is the same.
  4. The update computes shortest paths, not bottleneck paths.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Floyd–Warshall is not about addition; it is a dynamic program over a semiring. Any pair of operations that is associative and distributes one over the other gives the same argument, with min replaced by the semiring addition and + by the semiring multiplication.

Step by step.

  1. For shortest paths the path value is the sum of edge weights and the choice is the minimum, giving the (min, +) pair.
  2. For bottleneck paths the path value is the smallest capacity on the path and the choice is the largest such value, giving (max, min).
  3. min distributes over max in a total order, so the induction over intermediate vertices carries through unchanged.
  4. Hence the same triple loop, with the two operators swapped, solves the bottleneck problem.

Answer: Option (A) — the semiring swap is valid.

Common misconception. Assuming only (min, +) works. The recurrence is a semiring computation, and the bottleneck version is the standard textbook example of a second valid pair.

ALG-U6B-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the Floyd–Warshall recurrence are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. After the k-th outer iteration, d[i][j] is the shortest distance using only intermediate vertices drawn from {1, …, k}.
  2. The algorithm requires every weight to be non-negative.
  3. The update may be done in place, because when there is no negative cycle d[i][k] and d[k][j] do not change during iteration k.
  4. Floyd–Warshall computes single-source distances only.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The algorithm is an induction over the set of permitted intermediate vertices, and its memory optimisation is justified by the fact that the two entries it reads are already final for the current iteration.

Step by step.

  1. The induction hypothesis after iteration k restricts intermediates to {1, …, k}, and iteration k + 1 adds k + 1 as the only new option.
  2. Weights may be negative; only a negative cycle is fatal, and that is reported by the diagonal.
  3. During iteration k, any walk from i to k that used k internally would repeat k, which cannot help without a negative cycle, so those entries are stable and in-place overwriting is safe.
  4. The output is a full V × V table of all-pairs distances.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing negative weights are forbidden. Floyd–Warshall is one of the few all-pairs methods that accepts them; and the in-place shortcut fails only when a negative cycle makes the diagonal entries keep improving.

ALG-U6B-Q20MSQhard

Which of the following statements about transitive closure and Warshall's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It is obtained from the Floyd–Warshall recurrence by replacing min with OR and + with AND.
  2. Warshall's algorithm computes it in O(V3) time.
  3. The algorithm fails if the graph contains a cycle.
  4. The reflexive transitive closure sets d[i][i] true for every i, even when no cycle passes through i.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Reachability is the boolean shadow of shortest paths: OR takes the place of choosing the cheapest, and AND takes the place of paying for a concatenation.

Step by step.

  1. The recurrence becomes t[i][j] = t[i][j] OR (t[i][k] AND t[k][j]), which is exactly the (OR, AND) reading of the same triple loop.
  2. Three nested loops over the vertex set give O(V3), and the boolean version is faster in practice than the arithmetic one.
  3. Cycles are handled without any special case: a cycle simply makes the corresponding diagonal entries true.
  4. The reflexive closure additionally declares every vertex reachable from itself by the empty path, so the diagonal is set to true regardless of cycles.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking a cycle breaks the boolean algorithm. A cycle is what turns diagonal entries on in the non-reflexive closure; it is a feature of reachability, not a failure.

ALG-U6B-Q25NATeasy

A directed graph on the vertices 1–4 has the edges 1→2, 2→3, 3→4. Its transitive closure matrix T is defined by T[i][j] = 1 when there is a path of length at least one from i to j. How many of the 16 entries of T equal 1?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 6

Detailed solution

Concept. The transitive closure records reachability by a non-empty path, so every ordered pair joined by a chain of one or more edges contributes one entry.

Step by step.

  1. Paths of one edge: 1→2, 2→3, 3→4, giving three entries.
  2. Paths of two edges: 1→3, 2→4, giving two more.
  3. Paths of three edges: 1→4, giving one more.
  4. The diagonal stays 0 because the graph is acyclic, and 4 is unreachable from anything, so the total is 3 + 2 + 1 = 6.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Setting the diagonal to 1 by reflexivity. The question defines the non-reflexive closure; the reflexive version would add four more entries and give 10.

Unit 7 — Complexity Theory & NP-Completeness · 50 solved questions (2026-11-02)
Classes P and NP 14
ALG-U7-Q01MCQeasy

Which of the following is the strongest statement about the classes P and NP that is known to be true?

  1. P ⊂ NP, that is P is a proper subset of NP.
  2. P ⊆ NP.
  3. NP ⊆ P.
  4. P and NP are incomparable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Every deterministic polynomial-time algorithm is a nondeterministic polynomial-time algorithm that never uses its choice, so P ⊆ NP. Nothing stronger has been proved.

Step by step.

  1. A deterministic machine running in polynomial time is also a nondeterministic machine running in polynomial time: simply ignore the branching.
  2. Hence every language of P lies in NP, which gives P ⊆ NP.
  3. Proper containment would mean some language lies in NP outside P, and exhibiting one would settle P ≠ NP; that is still open.
  4. Incomparability is open as well, since no language is known to lie in NP outside P.
  5. So the only inclusion that is a theorem is the weak one.

Answer: P ⊆ NP — option B.

Common misconception. Choosing P ⊂ NP because it is 'obviously' true. Proper containment is the famous open problem; only the weak inclusion has been proved.

ALG-U7-Q02MCQeasy

A language L belongs to NP exactly when:

  1. L is decidable and some algorithm decides it in polynomial time.
  2. every string of L has a certificate whose length is at most log n, where n is the length of the string.
  3. there is a deterministic verifier V running in polynomial time and a polynomial p such that x ∈ L ⇔ ∃c with |c| ≤ p(|x|) and V(x, c) accepts.
  4. L is decidable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP is the class of languages with short certificates and a fast verifier; equivalently, the languages decided by a nondeterministic polynomial-time machine.

Step by step.

  1. If a nondeterministic machine accepts x in polynomial time, record the sequence of choices it made on an accepting path; that sequence is a certificate of polynomial length.
  2. A deterministic verifier can replay those choices in polynomial time, which gives the implication from NP to the verifier definition.
  3. Conversely, a nondeterministic machine can guess the certificate c of length at most p(|x|) and then run V, so the verifier definition gives NP.
  4. Option D is too weak: every decidable language has a decider, but the decider may take exponential time.
  5. Option B is too strong: certificates of logarithmic length would make the class far smaller than NP.

Answer: The polynomial-time verifier characterisation — option C.

Common misconception. Reading NP as 'not polynomial' and choosing D, or assuming certificates must be short in a logarithmic sense. NP asks for polynomial-length certificates, verified in polynomial time.

ALG-U7-Q03MCQeasy

For which of the following classes is closure under complement still an open question?

  1. P
  2. PSPACE
  3. the regular languages
  4. NP
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. A class is closed under complement when the complement of every language in it also lies in it. For NP this is exactly the question NP = co-NP.

Step by step.

  1. P is closed under complement: run the polynomial-time decider and reverse its answer.
  2. PSPACE is closed under complement by Savitch's theorem, which gives a deterministic polynomial-space machine that can flip its answer.
  3. The regular languages are closed under complement because a DFA becomes a DFA for the complement when its accepting and non-accepting states are swapped.
  4. For NP the answer is unknown: co-NP is defined as the class of complements of NP languages, and whether NP = co-NP is open.
  5. So the open case is NP.

Answer: NP — option D.

Common misconception. Assuming that complementing an NP machine is as easy as complementing a DFA. A nondeterministic machine has many computation paths and 'no accepting path exists' cannot be certified by a single short witness.

ALG-U7-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A language in NP is decided by some nondeterministic Turing machine in polynomial time.
  2. NP is closed under union and under intersection.
  3. NP is closed under complement.
  4. If L ∈ NP then every string of L has a certificate whose length is bounded by a polynomial in the length of the string.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three equivalent faces of NP — nondeterministic polynomial time, polynomial-length certificates, polynomial-time verification — plus the closure properties that follow from them.

Step by step.

  1. A: this is one of the standard definitions of NP, so it is TRUE.
  2. B: for the union, run the two nondeterministic machines in parallel and accept if either accepts; for the intersection, run them one after the other and accept only if both accept. Both constructions stay polynomial, so TRUE.
  3. C: this would give NP = co-NP, which is open, so it is not a theorem; FALSE.
  4. D: a certificate is the sequence of nondeterministic choices on an accepting path of a polynomial-time machine, so its length is polynomial in the input length; TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. Treating complement as just another closure property, like union. Union and intersection are constructive and cheap; complement would collapse the NP versus co-NP question.

ALG-U7-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following decision problems are known to be in P? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 2-SAT
  2. 3-SAT
  3. deciding whether a given undirected graph is bipartite
  4. deciding whether a given integer is prime
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Several problems that look like their NP-complete relatives are in P, and GATE tests exactly those boundaries.

Step by step.

  1. A: 2-SAT is decided in linear time by building the implication graph (a ∨ b) ≡ (¬a ⇒ b) ∧ (¬b ⇒ a) and testing for a variable and its negation in one strongly connected component. TRUE.
  2. B: 3-SAT is NP-complete, and no polynomial algorithm is known. FALSE.
  3. C: a graph is bipartite exactly when a BFS 2-colouring meets no edge with equal colours, which is linear time. TRUE.
  4. D: primality testing is in P — the AKS algorithm settled this in 2002, and randomised tests such as Miller–Rabin are polynomial in practice. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) are in P; (B) is not known to be.

Common misconception. Assuming that a problem phrased with 'satisfiability' or 'primes' must be hard. Restricting clauses to two literals, or asking only whether a number is prime rather than factoring it, drops the problem into P.

ALG-U7-Q06NATmedium

A verifier runs in time 2n³ + 7c on an instance of length n and a certificate of length c, and every certificate for an instance of length n has length at most n². Give the smallest integer k for which the verifier's running time on an instance of length n is O(n^k).

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. The certificate is itself polynomial in the input length, so substituting its bound into the verifier's cost gives a polynomial in n whose degree is what the question asks for.

Step by step.

  1. A certificate may be as long as c = n², so the worst case is 2n³ + 7n².
  2. The two terms are of degree 3 and degree 2.
  3. The faster-growing term 2n³ dominates: 7n² / 2n³ = 7/(2n) → 0.
  4. So the running time is Θ(n³), and O(n³) holds while O(n²) does not.
  5. The smallest such integer is 3.

Answer: k = 3.

Common misconception. Multiplying the two polynomials instead of substituting, or answering 5 by adding the degrees. The certificate length is a bound on c, not a factor multiplied into the running time.

ALG-U7-Q07MCQhard

Suppose a language L is NP-complete and also lies in co-NP. Which of the following is the strongest conclusion that follows?

  1. P = NP
  2. NP = co-NP
  3. L ∈ P
  4. every language in NP is NP-complete
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. An NP-complete problem is a universal target for NP: every NP language reduces to it. Putting it inside co-NP therefore drags all of NP into co-NP.

Step by step.

  1. Let A ∈ NP. Because L is NP-complete, A ≤p L.
  2. co-NP is closed under polynomial-time many-one reductions, and L ∈ co-NP, so A ∈ co-NP. Hence NP ⊆ co-NP.
  3. For the reverse inclusion let A ∈ co-NP. Then Ā ∈ NP, so Ā ≤p L, and since L ∈ co-NP we get Ā ∈ co-NP, that is A ∈ NP. Hence co-NP ⊆ NP.
  4. The two inclusions give NP = co-NP.
  5. Nothing here forces P = NP, so option A is stronger than what is proved and is not a consequence.

Answer: NP = co-NP — option B.

Common misconception. Stopping at 'L is in NP and in co-NP, so it is easy', which would give L ∈ P. A problem in both classes is not thereby in P; the P versus NP ∩ co-NP question is open.

ALG-U7-Q26MCQeasy

The class co-NP is best described as:

  1. the set of languages whose complements lie in NP.
  2. the set of languages that do not belong to NP.
  3. the set of languages decided by a nondeterministic machine that never uses its choices.
  4. the set of languages L for which both L and its complement lie in P.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. co-NP is defined by complementing membership, not by negating the class: L ∈ co-NP ⇔ L̄ ∈ NP.

Step by step.

  1. Take a language L. It lies in co-NP when a nondeterministic polynomial-time machine accepts exactly the strings outside L.
  2. Equivalently, the complement L̄ is accepted by a nondeterministic polynomial-time machine, that is L̄ ∈ NP.
  3. So co-NP = { L : L̄ ∈ NP }, which is option A.
  4. Option B describes the set complement of NP as a collection of languages, which is not a complexity class defined this way; option C describes P; option D describes P as well, since P is closed under complement.

Answer: the set of languages whose complements lie in NP — option A.

Common misconception. Reading co-NP as 'languages not in NP'. Complementation is applied inside each language, not to the class as a whole.

ALG-U7-Q27MCQeasy
