Which of the following inclusions is a proved theorem?

  1. NP ⊆ co-NP
  2. P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP
  3. NP = co-NP
  4. co-NP ⊆ P
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Two facts combine: a deterministic polynomial-time machine is a nondeterministic one, and P is closed under complement.

Step by step.

  1. Every language of P is decided by a deterministic polynomial-time machine, which is also a nondeterministic polynomial-time machine, so P ⊆ NP.
  2. If L ∈ P then L̄ is decided by the same machine with its answer reversed, so L̄ ∈ P ⊆ NP, which gives L ∈ co-NP. Hence P ⊆ co-NP.
  3. Combining the two inclusions gives P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP.
  4. NP ⊆ co-NP, NP = co-NP and co-NP ⊆ P are all open.

Answer: P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP — option B.

Common misconception. Treating NP = co-NP as known because both classes contain P. Only the weak inclusion P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP has been proved.

ALG-U7-Q28MCQmedium

If some NP-complete language L belongs to co-NP, which of the following follows?

  1. P = NP
  2. NP = co-NP
  3. NP ⊂ co-NP, a proper containment
  4. L ∈ P
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. co-NP is closed under polynomial-time many-one reductions, so putting an NP-complete language inside co-NP drags all of NP along with it.

Step by step.

  1. Let A ∈ NP. Because L is NP-complete, A ≤p L.
  2. co-NP is closed under polynomial-time many-one reductions: if X ≤p Y and Y ∈ co-NP then X ∈ co-NP. Hence A ∈ co-NP, so NP ⊆ co-NP.
  3. For the other direction let B ∈ co-NP, so B̄ ∈ NP. Since L is NP-complete, B̄ ≤p L, and L ∈ co-NP gives B̄ ∈ co-NP, that is B ∈ NP. Hence co-NP ⊆ NP.
  4. The two inclusions give NP = co-NP; nothing here forces P = NP or puts L in P.

Answer: NP = co-NP — option B.

Common misconception. Concluding P = NP. Collapsing NP with co-NP is a different question from collapsing NP with P, and neither implication is known.

ALG-U7-Q29MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. NP ⊆ PSPACE
  2. co-NP ⊆ PSPACE
  3. If NP = co-NP then P = NP.
  4. P ⊆ NP ∩ co-NP
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Polynomial space is powerful enough to simulate nondeterministic polynomial time by exhaustive search, and PSPACE is closed under complement.

Step by step.

  1. A: a nondeterministic machine that runs in polynomial time can be simulated by walking all computation paths depth-first; each path needs polynomial space and the space is reused, so NP ⊆ PSPACE. TRUE.
  2. B: PSPACE is closed under complement (reverse the final answer of a deterministic polynomial-space machine), and NP ⊆ PSPACE, so co-NP ⊆ PSPACE. TRUE.
  3. C: this is not a theorem. Collapsing NP with co-NP says nothing about deterministic time, and P = NP would remain open. FALSE.
  4. D: P is contained in NP, and P is closed under complement, so P is contained in co-NP as well. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. Assuming every collapse of two nondeterministic classes implies P = NP. NP = co-NP and P = NP are independent questions as far as anyone knows.

ALG-U7-Q30NATmedium

How many of the following languages are known to lie in NP? (i) SAT (ii) 3-SAT (iii) the halting problem (iv) the decision version of VERTEX COVER (v) the complement of SAT

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Membership in NP needs a polynomial-length certificate that a deterministic verifier can check in polynomial time.

Step by step.

  1. (i) SAT: a satisfying assignment is a certificate of linear length and can be checked in linear time. In NP.
  2. (ii) 3-SAT: same certificate, restricted to a 3-CNF formula. In NP.
  3. (iii) The halting problem is not even decidable, so it cannot be in NP. Not counted.
  4. (iv) VERTEX COVER: a set of at most k vertices is a certificate, and checking that it covers every edge is linear. In NP.
  5. (v) The complement of SAT: whether it lies in NP is exactly the open question NP = co-NP, so it is not known to be in NP. Not counted.
  6. That leaves three languages: (i), (ii) and (iv).

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting the complement of SAT, or forgetting that the halting problem is undecidable rather than merely hard. 'Not known to be in NP' is not the same as 'in NP'.

ALG-U7-Q31MSQhard

Which of the following operations preserve membership in NP? (Select all that apply.)

  1. union
  2. intersection
  3. complement
  4. Kleene star
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP is closed under the operations built from running two nondeterministic machines, but complement is the one operation that is open.

Step by step.

  1. Union: guess which of the two machines to run, then run it; accept if it accepts. Polynomial. CLOSED.
  2. Intersection: run the two nondeterministic machines one after the other and accept only if both accept. Polynomial. CLOSED.
  3. Complement: negating an NP machine would require certifying that no accepting path exists, which is not a polynomial-length witness. OPEN, so not preserved.
  4. Kleene star: guess a split of the input into pieces and run the machine on each piece; the certificate records the split and the accepting paths. Polynomial. CLOSED.

Answer: Union, intersection and Kleene star are closed; complement is not known to be.

Common misconception. Assuming every boolean operation is cheap. Union and intersection are constructive because they combine accepting paths; complement would have to certify the absence of a path.

ALG-U7-Q32MCQmedium

Which of the following, if proved, would establish P ≠ NP?

  1. An NP-complete problem for which no polynomial-time algorithm is currently known.
  2. A language in NP that requires Ω(2ⁿ) time on infinitely many input lengths.
  3. NP = co-NP.
  4. An NP-hard problem that lies in P.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. P ≠ NP is a statement about lower bounds: some language in NP must be proved to need superpolynomial time.

Step by step.

  1. Option A records the state of the art, not a theorem; not knowing an algorithm is not a proof that none exists.
  2. Option B is a genuine lower bound for a language in NP, and 2ⁿ grows faster than every polynomial, so it separates NP from P.
  3. Option C, if anything, is a collapse of two nondeterministic classes and implies nothing about deterministic time.
  4. Option D would give P = NP: every language in NP reduces to the NP-hard language, so it would inherit a polynomial algorithm. It is the opposite of what is wanted.

Answer: a language in NP needing Ω(2ⁿ) time — option B.

Common misconception. Treating 'no polynomial algorithm is known' as a lower bound, or picking option D which actually forces P = NP.

Polynomial-time reductions 12
ALG-U7-Q08MCQeasy

If A ≤p B and B ∈ P, which of the following must hold?

  1. A ∈ P
  2. A is NP-complete
  3. B ≤p A
  4. A is undecidable
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A reduction moves solvability backwards: an easy target makes the source easy. That is the whole point of using reductions to prove membership in P.

Step by step.

  1. Let f be the polynomial-time reduction, so x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B.
  2. On input x, compute f(x) and then run the polynomial-time decider for B on it.
  3. Both stages are polynomial, and their composition is polynomial.
  4. So A is decided in polynomial time, that is A ∈ P.
  5. Nothing follows in the other direction: B ≤p A need not exist.

Answer: A ∈ P — option A.

Common misconception. Reversing the arrow and concluding something about B. The reduction carries hardness from B to A, never from A to B.

ALG-U7-Q09MCQeasy

To prove that a problem B is NP-hard, it is enough to exhibit:

  1. a polynomial-time algorithm for B
  2. a certificate for B that can be verified in polynomial time
  3. a polynomial-time reduction from a known NP-hard problem A to B
  4. a polynomial-time reduction from B to a known NP-hard problem A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-hardness of B means every language in NP reduces to B. A single known NP-hard source that reduces to B, composed with the reductions from NP, establishes exactly that.

Step by step.

  1. Hardness flows along the arrow: if A ≤p B and A is NP-hard, then for any X ∈ NP we have X ≤p A ≤p B, so X ≤p B.
  2. Therefore B is NP-hard, which is option C.
  3. Option D is the wrong direction: B ≤p A only shows that B is no harder than A, which is a statement about easiness.
  4. Option B shows B ∈ NP; on its own it proves nothing about hardness.
  5. Option A would prove B is easy, the opposite of the goal.

Answer: A reduction from a known NP-hard problem to B — option C.

Common misconception. Reducing B to a known hard problem, which is the single most common error in NP-completeness proofs. To show B is hard, the hard problem must reduce to B.

ALG-U7-Q10MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about polynomial-time many-one reductions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A ≤p B and B ≤p A then both A and B lie in P.
  2. ≤p is transitive.
  3. If A ≤p B and B ∈ P then A ∈ P.
  4. If A is NP-complete, A ≤p B and B ∈ NP, then B is NP-complete.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The three working properties of ≤p: it composes, it pulls easiness backwards, and it pushes hardness forwards.

Step by step.

  1. A: mutual reducibility means A and B have the same difficulty, which for two NP-complete problems is exactly what happens. It says nothing about P. FALSE.
  2. B: composing two polynomial-time functions gives a polynomial-time function, so A ≤p B and B ≤p C give A ≤p C. TRUE.
  3. C: compute the reduction and then decide B; both stages are polynomial. TRUE.
  4. D: A is NP-hard, so every language of NP reduces to A and hence, by transitivity, to B. With B ∈ NP as well, B is NP-complete. TRUE.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D) are TRUE; (A) is FALSE.

Common misconception. Reading mutual reducibility as evidence of easiness. 3-SAT and CLIQUE reduce to each other precisely because both are hard; the pair is closed under reduction, not pushed into P.

ALG-U7-Q11MCQeasy

A polynomial-time many-one reduction from A to B is a function f with which property?

  1. f is computable in polynomial time, and x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B for every string x.
  2. f is computable in polynomial time, and x ∈ A ⇒ f(x) ∈ B, with no condition when x ∉ A.
  3. f maps every string of A to a string of B and is computable in exponential time.
  4. f is a polynomial-time algorithm that decides B on input x.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A many-one reduction must be an exact translation of membership, computable fast enough that it can be composed with a polynomial-time solver.

Step by step.

  1. The biconditional is essential: answers must be preserved in both directions, so that deciding B decides A.
  2. A one-way implication as in option B would let f map everything into B and carry no information about non-membership.
  3. Exponential-time computation as in option C cannot be composed with a polynomial solver without destroying the polynomial bound.
  4. Option D describes a decider for B, not a translation from A to B.
  5. Hence the definition is polynomial-time computability plus the biconditional.

Answer: Polynomial time and x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B — option A.

Common misconception. Accepting the one-way implication. Hardness proofs depend on the converse direction, which is what makes a 'no' instance of B a 'no' instance of A.

ALG-U7-Q12NATmedium

A reduction from A to B runs in time O(n³) and maps an instance of A of length n to an instance of B of length O(n²). B is solved in time O(m⁴) on an instance of length m. Solving A by composing the reduction with the solver for B takes time O(n^k). Give k.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. Compose the two cost functions: the solver's cost is measured on the length of the instance the reduction produced, so substitute that length into the solver's bound.

Step by step.

  1. Reduction cost: O(n³).
  2. Length of the instance handed to the solver: m = O(n²).
  3. Solver cost: O(m⁴) = O((n²)⁴) = O(n⁸).
  4. Total: O(n³ + n⁸) = O(n⁸), because the degree-8 term dominates.
  5. So k = 8.

Answer: k = 8.

Common misconception. Multiplying the exponents (3 × 4 = 12) or adding the two bounds as n³ · n⁴. The substitution is of the produced length into the solver's polynomial, which raises the exponent to 2 × 4.

ALG-U7-Q13MCQhard

Let A be NP-complete and let B ∈ P. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. A ≤p B
  2. both A ≤p B and B ≤p A
  3. neither reduction exists
  4. B ≤p A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. An NP-hard target accepts a reduction from every NP language, and P ⊆ NP, so every problem in P reduces to A. The reverse reduction would collapse P and NP.

Step by step.

  1. Since B ∈ P and P ⊆ NP, B is a language in NP.
  2. A is NP-complete, hence NP-hard, so every language in NP reduces to A; in particular B ≤p A.
  3. If A ≤p B also held, then A would lie in P by the easiness-transfer property, and since A is NP-hard that would give P = NP.
  4. P = NP is not known, so A ≤p B is not a theorem and option B fails.
  5. Option C fails because B ≤p A does exist.

Answer: B ≤p A — option D.

Common misconception. Assuming reducibility is symmetric because both problems are 'polynomial-time related'. ≤p is a preorder, not an equivalence: the direction carries all the meaning.

ALG-U7-Q33MCQeasy

A polynomial-time many-one reduction from A to B is a function f such that:

  1. f is computable in polynomial time and x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B.
  2. f is computable in polynomial time and x ∈ A ⇒ f(x) ∈ B.
  3. f is computable in polynomial time and x ∈ B ⇔ f(x) ∈ A.
  4. f is a polynomial-time bijection from A onto B.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A reduction must translate membership exactly, in both directions, and be fast enough to compose with a polynomial-time solver.

Step by step.

  1. The forward direction x ∈ A ⇒ f(x) ∈ B alone is satisfied by the constant map into B and carries no information.
  2. The biconditional x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B makes deciding B decide A, which is the point of a reduction.
  3. Polynomial-time computability keeps the composition polynomial.
  4. Option C reverses the arrow (it reduces B to A); option D demands far more than a reduction needs, and no bijection is required.

Answer: polynomial-time computable with x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B — option A.

Common misconception. Accepting the one-way implication. Hardness proofs and easiness proofs both depend on the 'no' instances being preserved as well.

ALG-U7-Q34MCQeasy

If A ≤p B and B ∈ P, which of the following follows?

  1. A ∈ P
  2. B ≤p A
  3. A is NP-complete
  4. nothing can be concluded about A
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Easiness transfers backwards along a reduction: an easy target makes the source easy.

Step by step.

  1. On input x, first compute f(x) in polynomial time; the reduction guarantees x ∈ A ⇔ f(x) ∈ B.
  2. Then run the polynomial-time decider for B on f(x) and return its answer.
  3. Both stages are polynomial, and their composition is polynomial, so A is decided in polynomial time.
  4. Nothing follows about B ≤p A, and A need not be NP-complete; in fact A ∈ P rules that out unless P = NP.

Answer: A ∈ P — option A.

Common misconception. Reversing the arrow and concluding something about B, or assuming every language that reduces to an easy problem is itself NP-complete.

ALG-U7-Q35NATmedium

A reduction from A to B runs in O(n²) time and, on an instance of length n, produces an instance of length O(n³). The decider for B runs in O(m²) on an instance of length m. The composed algorithm decides A in O(n^k). Give the smallest integer k.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 6

Detailed solution

Concept. Compose the two costs: the solver's cost must be measured on the length of the instance the reduction produced.

Step by step.

  1. The reduction costs O(n²).
  2. The instance handed to the solver has length m = O(n³).
  3. Substituting into the solver's bound gives O(m²) = O((n³)²) = O(n⁶).
  4. The total is O(n² + n⁶) = O(n⁶), since the degree-6 term dominates.
  5. So the smallest such integer is 6.

Answer: k = 6.

Common misconception. Multiplying the exponents (2 × 3 = 6 is a coincidence here) or adding the two bounds as n² · n². The substitution is of the produced length into the solver's polynomial, which raises the exponent to 3 × 2.

ALG-U7-Q36MSQhard

Which of the following statements about polynomial-time many-one reductions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A ≤p B and B ∈ NP then A ∈ NP.
  2. If A ≤p B and B ≤p C then A ≤p C.
  3. If A ≤p B then B ≤p A.
  4. If A ≤p B and B ∈ P then A ∈ P.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. Membership in NP and in P both travel backwards along a reduction, and reductions compose; reducibility itself is a preorder, not a symmetric relation.

Step by step.

  1. A: given a verifier for B, verify f(x) with the same certificate, so A has a polynomial-time verifier. TRUE.
  2. B: compute g(f(x)); a composition of two polynomial-time functions is polynomial, and the biconditional chains. TRUE.
  3. C: ≤p is reflexive and transitive but not symmetric. An easy language reduces to a hard one, and the reverse map need not exist. FALSE.
  4. D: compose the reduction with the polynomial decider for B. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are TRUE; (C) is FALSE.

Common misconception. Treating ≤p as if it were an equivalence. Two mutually reducible problems are equally hard, but one-directional reducibility only orders them.

ALG-U7-Q37MCQmedium

To prove that a new decision problem X is NP-complete it is enough to show:

  1. X ∈ NP and X ≤p 3-SAT.
  2. X ∈ NP and 3-SAT ≤p X.
  3. X is NP-hard and X ≤p SAT.
  4. X ∈ NP and every problem in NP reduces to X in exponential time.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-completeness = membership in NP + hardness, and hardness is proved by reducing a known NP-complete problem to the new one.

Step by step.

  1. X ∈ NP supplies the upper bound.
  2. 3-SAT ≤p X, together with 3-SAT being NP-hard, gives that every language of NP reduces to X by transitivity. That is the lower bound.
  3. Option A reduces X to an easy-to-state but already hard problem, which only shows X is no harder than 3-SAT.
  4. Option C omits membership in NP, and exponential-time reductions are too weak to preserve polynomial solvability.
  5. Option D is not a polynomial-time reduction at all, so it proves nothing about P versus NP.

Answer: X ∈ NP and 3-SAT ≤p X — option B.

Common misconception. Reducing the new problem to a known NP-complete one. That direction shows X ∈ NP at best; the known hard problem must reduce to X.

ALG-U7-Q38MCQhard

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. If A ≤p B and B ∈ P then A ∈ P.
  2. If A ≤p B and B is NP-hard then A is NP-hard.
  3. If A ≤p B and B ∈ NP then A ∈ NP.
  4. If A is NP-complete, A ≤p B and B ∈ NP, then B is NP-complete.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Everything follows from the direction of the arrow: easiness and membership flow backwards, hardness flows forwards.

Step by step.

  1. A is true: compute f(x) and run the polynomial-time decider for B on it; the composition is polynomial.
  2. C is true: run B's polynomial-time verifier on f(x) with the same certificate; the composition is still a polynomial-time verifier for A.
  3. D is true: B ∈ NP gives the upper bound, and every language of NP reduces to A, which reduces to B, so B is NP-hard as well.
  4. B is false. A ≤p B makes A no harder than B, so a hard target says nothing about the source: A could be 2-SAT while B is SAT.
  5. Hence exactly one statement fails, and it is the one that pushes hardness backwards along the arrow.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Reading A ≤p B as "A is at least as hard as B". The arrow points from the easier problem to the harder one, so hardness is inherited by the target, never by the source.

NP-complete problems: 3-SAT, clique, vertex cover 13
ALG-U7-Q14MCQeasy

Which problem was the first to be proved NP-complete?

  1. SAT, in the Cook–Levin theorem
  2. 3-SAT
  3. CLIQUE
  4. HAMILTONIAN CYCLE
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. Cook and Levin showed in 1971 that Boolean satisfiability is NP-complete, giving the first member of the class and therefore a root for all later reductions.

Step by step.

  1. The proof encodes the entire computation of a nondeterministic polynomial-time machine as a Boolean formula.
  2. The formula is satisfiable exactly when the machine has an accepting computation, and its size is polynomial in the input length.
  3. That makes SAT NP-hard, and SAT is in NP because an assignment is a polynomial certificate.
  4. Every later NP-completeness result reduces from SAT or from one of its descendants.
  5. 3-SAT, CLIQUE and HAMILTONIAN CYCLE came afterwards, each by reduction.

Answer: SAT — option A.

Common misconception. Answering 3-SAT because it is the more convenient starting point for later reductions. 3-SAT is derived from SAT by a reduction that splits long clauses.

ALG-U7-Q15MCQeasy

Which of the following problems is NP-complete?

  1. 2-SAT
  2. deciding whether a graph has an Eulerian circuit
  3. HAMILTONIAN CYCLE
  4. single-source shortest paths with non-negative weights
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. The Hamiltonian and Eulerian conditions are the standard contrast: visiting every vertex once is hard, traversing every edge once is easy.

Step by step.

  1. A: 2-SAT is decided in linear time through strongly connected components of the implication graph, so it is in P.
  2. B: a connected graph has an Eulerian circuit exactly when every vertex has even degree, which is checked in linear time.
  3. C: HAMILTONIAN CYCLE is NP-complete, by reduction from VERTEX COVER; no polynomial algorithm is known.
  4. D: Dijkstra's algorithm solves it in O(E + V log V) time with non-negative weights.
  5. So only option C is NP-complete.

Answer: HAMILTONIAN CYCLE — option C.

Common misconception. Confusing Eulerian with Hamiltonian. 'Every edge exactly once' is a degree condition; 'every vertex exactly once' is the hard one.

ALG-U7-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If any NP-complete language belongs to P then P = NP.
  2. Every language in NP is polynomial-time reducible to every NP-complete language.
  3. There are infinitely many NP-complete languages.
  4. The complement of an NP-complete language is NP-complete.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-completeness is defined by being both in NP and NP-hard, and its consequences follow from the transitivity of ≤p.

Step by step.

  1. A: an NP-complete language is NP-hard, so every language of NP reduces to it; if it were in P, every language of NP would be in P. TRUE.
  2. B: that is precisely the NP-hard half of the definition of NP-complete. TRUE.
  3. C: from one NP-complete language infinitely many are obtained, for example by padding or by intersecting with trivially different regular sets, all still NP-complete. TRUE.
  4. D: the complement of an NP-complete language is co-NP-complete; if it were NP-complete then NP = co-NP, which is open. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are TRUE; (D) is not.

Common misconception. Assuming NP-completeness is inherited by the complement. It is inherited by co-NP-completeness, and identifying the two classes is exactly the open NP versus co-NP question.

ALG-U7-Q17MCQmedium

A graph G has n = 10 vertices. The reduction CLIQUE ≤p VERTEX-COVER replaces G by its complement and sets the required cover size to n − k. G has a clique of size 4 exactly when its complement has a vertex cover of size:

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 10
  4. 14
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. A clique in G is an independent set in the complement, and the complement of a maximum independent set is a minimum vertex cover.

Step by step.

  1. A set S is a clique in G exactly when no two of its vertices are non-adjacent in G, that is, exactly when S is an independent set in the complement graph.
  2. In any graph, S is an independent set exactly when V − S is a vertex cover: every edge has at least one end outside S.
  3. So a clique of size k in G corresponds to an independent set of size k in the complement, and hence to a vertex cover of size n − k there.
  4. With n = 10 and k = 4, the cover size is 10 − 4 = 6.

Answer: 6 — option B.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by keeping the same size, or 14 by adding instead of subtracting. The correspondence is a complement of the vertex set, so the sizes sum to n.

ALG-U7-Q18NAThard

Apply the standard reduction from 3-SAT to CLIQUE to φ = (x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ ¬x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ x₃) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ ¬x₂ ∨ ¬x₃). One vertex is created for each literal occurrence, and an edge joins two vertices that lie in different clauses when their literals are not complementary. How many edges does the constructed graph have?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 42

Detailed solution

Concept. The reduction builds a vertex per literal occurrence and joins every cross-clause pair except the contradictory ones, so the edge count is the number of cross-clause pairs minus the number of complementary pairs.

Step by step.

  1. Four clauses of three literals give 4 × 3 = 12 vertices.
  2. Pairs inside one clause are never joined: 4 × C(3,2) = 12 of them, leaving C(12,2) − 12 = 66 − 12 = 54 cross-clause pairs.
  3. Count the complementary pairs, clause pair by clause pair: clauses 1 and 2 give (x₁, ¬x₁) and (¬x₃, x₃); clauses 1 and 3 give (x₂, ¬x₂) and (¬x₃, x₃); clauses 1 and 4 give (x₁, ¬x₁) and (x₂, ¬x₂); clauses 2 and 3 give (¬x₁, x₁) and (x₂, ¬x₂); clauses 2 and 4 give (x₂, ¬x₂) and (x₃, ¬x₃); clauses 3 and 4 give (x₁, ¬x₁) and (x₃, ¬x₃).
  4. That is 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 12 complementary pairs, each excluded.
  5. Edges = 54 − 12 = 42.

Answer: 42 edges.

Common misconception. Forgetting to subtract the within-clause pairs (giving 54), or subtracting only one complementary pair per clause pair (giving 48). Every literal has exactly one complement among the twelve, and the count above pairs them all.

ALG-U7-Q19MCQhard

3-SAT and HAMILTONIAN CYCLE are both NP-complete. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. 3-SAT ≤p HAMILTONIAN CYCLE, but not conversely
  2. neither problem reduces to the other
  3. HAMILTONIAN CYCLE ≤p 3-SAT, but not conversely
  4. each problem reduces to the other in polynomial time
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. All NP-complete problems are mutually reducible: each is NP-hard, so each reduces to every other member of NP.

Step by step.

  1. 3-SAT is NP-complete, hence NP-hard, and HAMILTONIAN CYCLE lies in NP, so HAMILTONIAN CYCLE ≤p 3-SAT.
  2. By the same argument with the roles exchanged, 3-SAT ≤p HAMILTONIAN CYCLE.
  3. Both directions hold simultaneously; there is no asymmetry among NP-complete problems.
  4. Reducibility is a preorder, so the two problems sit in one equivalence class under mutual reduction.

Answer: Each reduces to the other — option D.

Common misconception. Believing one NP-complete problem is 'more fundamental' than another because SAT came first historically. Historical order affects proofs, not reducibility.

ALG-U7-Q39MCQeasy

Which of the following decision problems is known to lie in P?

  1. CLIQUE
  2. 2-SAT
  3. 3-SAT
  4. VERTEX COVER
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. Restricting clauses to at most two literals removes the hardness: 2-SAT is decided in linear time through the implication graph.

Step by step.

  1. Build the implication graph with an edge (a → b) for every clause (¬a ∨ b) and an edge (b → a) for the same clause read the other way.
  2. The formula is unsatisfiable exactly when some variable and its negation lie in the same strongly connected component.
  3. Strongly connected components are found in linear time, so 2-SAT is in P.
  4. CLIQUE, 3-SAT and VERTEX COVER are all NP-complete, so no polynomial algorithm is known for them.

Answer: 2-SAT — option B.

Common misconception. Assuming that a problem that shares the name SAT must be hard. The two-literal restriction is what makes 2-SAT tractable.

ALG-U7-Q40MCQeasy

The decision version of VERTEX COVER asks, given a graph G and an integer k, whether:

  1. G has a vertex cover of size at most k.
  2. G has a vertex cover of size exactly k.
  3. every edge of G belongs to some vertex cover of size k.
  4. G has a minimum vertex cover that is unique.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The decision version of a minimisation problem asks a yes/no question with a threshold, not for the optimum value.

Step by step.

  1. A vertex cover is a set S of vertices such that every edge has at least one endpoint in S.
  2. The decision problem fixes a bound k and asks whether some cover of size at most k exists.
  3. 'At most' is essential: a cover of size less than k can always be padded, so 'exactly k' is a different and less natural question.
  4. Uniqueness of the minimum cover is a separate structural question and is not part of the standard decision problem.

Answer: whether G has a vertex cover of size at most k — option A.

Common misconception. Confusing the decision version with the optimisation version, which asks for the minimum size. Only decision problems have a membership in NP.

ALG-U7-Q41NATeasy

In the standard reduction from 3-SAT to CLIQUE, a 3-CNF formula with 5 clauses and 3 literals per clause is turned into a graph. Give the value of k, the target clique size, used in the reduction.

Show answer & solution

Answer: 5

Detailed solution

Concept. The reduction creates one vertex per literal occurrence and joins two vertices unless they are in the same clause or are complementary literals.

Step by step.

  1. One vertex is created for each literal occurrence, so the graph has 5 × 3 = 15 vertices.
  2. Vertices inside the same clause are never adjacent, so a clique contains at most one vertex per clause.
  3. A clique of size equal to the number of clauses picks exactly one literal from each clause, and the construction forbids complementary pairs.
  4. So the target size is the number of clauses, k = 5.

Answer: k = 5.

Common misconception. Answering 15 (the number of vertices) or 3 (the literals per clause). The target is the number of clauses.

ALG-U7-Q42NATmedium

A 3-CNF formula has 4 clauses, each with exactly 3 literals. The standard reduction builds a graph on 12 vertices and a target clique of size 4. If VERTEX COVER is then asked on the complement of that graph, what is the smallest k for which the formula is satisfiable exactly when the graph has a vertex cover of size at most k?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 8

Detailed solution

Concept. In the complement graph a clique becomes an independent set, and the complement of an independent set is a vertex cover.

Step by step.

  1. The 3-SAT to CLIQUE construction gives a graph H on 12 vertices in which the formula is satisfiable exactly when H has a clique of size 4.
  2. Take the complement graph H̄. A clique of size 4 in H is an independent set of size 4 in H̄.
  3. In any graph, the complement of an independent set is a vertex cover, so an independent set of size 4 leaves a vertex cover of size 12 − 4 = 8.
  4. Sizes add up to the number of vertices, so the smallest such k is 8.

Answer: k = 8.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by keeping the same size, or 16 by adding instead of subtracting. The correspondence is a complement of the vertex set, so the two sizes sum to n.

ALG-U7-Q43MSQmedium

Which of the following problems are NP-complete? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 3-SAT
  2. VERTEX COVER (decision version)
  3. deciding whether a graph is bipartite
  4. HAMILTONIAN CYCLE
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. The NP-complete members of this list are the ones that stay hard on general graphs; the tractable one has a linear-time characterisation.

Step by step.

  1. 3-SAT is NP-complete: SAT reduces to it by splitting long clauses, and it is in NP through a truth-assignment certificate.
  2. VERTEX COVER is NP-complete, by a reduction from CLIQUE through the complement graph.
  3. Bipartiteness is in P: a BFS 2-colouring either succeeds or exposes an odd cycle, in linear time. So it is not NP-complete unless P = NP.
  4. HAMILTONIAN CYCLE is NP-complete, by a reduction from VERTEX COVER or from 3-SAT.

Answer: 3-SAT, VERTEX COVER and HAMILTONIAN CYCLE; bipartiteness is in P.

Common misconception. Including bipartiteness because it mentions graphs, or excluding HAMILTONIAN CYCLE because the Eulerian analogue is easy. Eulerian is a degree condition; Hamiltonian is not.

ALG-U7-Q44MCQeasy

The standard reduction from CLIQUE to INDEPENDENT SET works by:

  1. taking the complement graph and keeping the same k.
  2. keeping the graph unchanged and setting k′ = n − k.
  3. taking the complement graph and setting k′ = n − k.
  4. deleting one vertex from the graph and keeping k.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. A clique in G is exactly an independent set in the complement graph, with the same vertex set and the same size.

Step by step.

  1. Two vertices are adjacent in the complement exactly when they are non-adjacent in G.
  2. A set S is a clique in G when every pair of its vertices is adjacent in G, that is, no pair is adjacent in the complement.
  3. That is exactly the condition for S to be independent in the complement graph.
  4. So the same S and the same k witness both problems, and the reduction keeps k unchanged.

Answer: complement graph with the same k — option A.

Common misconception. Changing k as well. Mixing the two operations gives a reduction to VERTEX COVER, not to INDEPENDENT SET.

ALG-U7-Q45MCQhard

In the reduction from 3-SAT to CLIQUE, which of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The formula is satisfiable iff the graph has a clique of size equal to the number of clauses, and such a clique picks one literal per clause without picking a literal together with its negation.
  2. The graph has a clique of size equal to the number of clauses iff the formula is unsatisfiable.
  3. A clique of maximum size may contain two vertices that come from the same clause.
  4. The reduction runs in polynomial time only when the formula is satisfiable.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. The reduction encodes each literal occurrence as a vertex and forbids exactly the two ways a set of literals can fail to be a partial assignment.

Step by step.

  1. Vertices of the same clause are left non-adjacent, so a clique holds at most one literal per clause; reaching the number of clauses therefore means exactly one per clause.
  2. Complementary literals are left non-adjacent, so a clique never contains both x and ¬x.
  3. Such a clique is a consistent partial assignment that satisfies one literal in every clause, which extends to a full satisfying assignment.
  4. Conversely a satisfying assignment supplies one true literal per clause, and those vertices are pairwise adjacent.
  5. The construction is a fixed polynomial-time procedure and is carried out whatever the formula is, so option D is false, and options B and C contradict the two non-adjacency rules.

Answer: option A.

Common misconception. Believing the reduction needs the formula to be satisfiable, or that the graph can encode two literals of one clause. Both non-adjacency rules are what make the correspondence exact in both directions.

NP-hard identification 11
ALG-U7-Q20MCQeasy

How does NP-hard differ from NP-complete?

  1. An NP-hard problem must lie in NP, whereas an NP-complete problem need not.
  2. An NP-hard problem need not lie in NP and may even be undecidable, whereas NP-complete means NP-hard and in NP.
  3. The two terms are synonyms.
  4. NP-hard problems are exactly those with no polynomial-time algorithm, whereas NP-complete problems have one.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (B)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-complete = NP-hard ∩ NP. Hardness alone says every NP language reduces to the problem; it says nothing about whether the problem's own solutions can be checked quickly.

Step by step.

  1. NP-hardness is a lower bound: every language in NP reduces to the problem in polynomial time.
  2. That definition never mentions membership in NP, so a problem outside NP can be NP-hard.
  3. The halting problem is the standard example: it is NP-hard because SAT reduces to it, yet it is undecidable and therefore not in NP.
  4. Adding the requirement of membership in NP gives NP-completeness.
  5. Option D confuses the definition with a consequence: hardness means no polynomial algorithm unless P = NP, but the converse does not define the class.

Answer: NP-hard need not be in NP — option B.

Common misconception. Using the two names interchangeably. Every NP-complete problem is NP-hard, but the halting problem is NP-hard and not NP-complete.

ALG-U7-Q21MSQmedium

Which of the following problems are NP-hard? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 2-SAT
  2. the halting problem
  3. deciding whether a given DFA accepts every string
  4. the travelling-salesman optimisation problem, that is, finding a minimum-cost tour
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (B) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-hardness is about being at least as hard as everything in NP; it is not restricted to problems inside NP, nor granted to problems that turn out to be in P.

Step by step.

  1. A: 2-SAT is in P, so it is NP-hard only if P = NP; it is not known to be NP-hard. Not selected.
  2. B: SAT reduces to the halting problem, so every language of NP reduces to it; it is NP-hard, and undecidable. Selected.
  3. C: DFA universality is decided by a product construction in polynomial time, so it is in P and not known to be NP-hard. Not selected.
  4. D: the optimisation form of TSP is at least as hard as its NP-complete decision form, so it is NP-hard; it is not in NP because it is not a decision problem. Selected.

Answer: Options (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Restricting NP-hard to problems in NP, which would exclude both the halting problem and the TSP optimisation problem. Hardness is a lower bound and applies outside NP.

ALG-U7-Q22MCQmedium

The optimisation version of the travelling-salesman problem, which asks for a minimum-cost tour, is:

  1. NP-hard, and not known to belong to NP
  2. NP-complete
  3. in P
  4. undecidable
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP contains decision problems only. An optimisation problem can be NP-hard without being a member of NP, because there is no yes/no answer to certify.

Step by step.

  1. The decision form — is there a tour of cost at most k — is NP-complete.
  2. Any algorithm for the optimisation form yields an algorithm for the decision form by comparing the optimum with k, so the optimisation form is at least as hard, hence NP-hard.
  3. Membership in NP requires a language, that is a set of strings with a yes/no answer; a minimum-cost tour is a function value, not a decision.
  4. So the optimisation form is NP-hard but is not a member of NP, and therefore not NP-complete.
  5. It is decidable, since enumerating all tours terminates, so it is not undecidable either.

Answer: NP-hard, and not known to be in NP — option A.

Common misconception. Calling every NP-hard problem NP-complete. The optimisation form fails the membership half of the definition.

ALG-U7-Q23NATeasy

Among the four decision problems 3-SAT, 2-SAT, CLIQUE and single-source shortest paths, how many are NP-complete?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 2

Detailed solution

Concept. Separating the NP-complete members of a short list from their polynomial-time look-alikes.

Step by step.

  1. 3-SAT is NP-complete: it is in NP, and SAT reduces to it.
  2. 2-SAT is in P, decided in linear time by strongly connected components of the implication graph.
  3. CLIQUE is NP-complete, by reduction from 3-SAT.
  4. Single-source shortest paths with non-negative weights is in P by Dijkstra's algorithm.
  5. The NP-complete members are 3-SAT and CLIQUE, so the count is 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Counting 2-SAT because it shares the word 'SAT'. The two-literal restriction removes the hardness entirely.

ALG-U7-Q24MCQhard

Suppose L is NP-hard and L ≤p L′. Which of the following follows?

  1. L′ must belong to NP.
  2. L′ must be NP-complete.
  3. L′ must be NP-hard.
  4. nothing follows about L′.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. Hardness travels forward along a reduction: if everything in NP reaches L, and L reaches L′, then everything in NP reaches L′.

Step by step.

  1. Let A ∈ NP. Since L is NP-hard, A ≤p L.
  2. We are given L ≤p L′, so by transitivity A ≤p L′.
  3. This holds for every A ∈ NP, which is exactly the statement that L′ is NP-hard.
  4. Nothing is claimed about membership: L′ may lie outside NP, so option A and option B both fail.
  5. Option D fails because the conclusion in option C does follow.

Answer: L′ must be NP-hard — option C.

Common misconception. Concluding NP-completeness, which needs the extra fact L′ ∈ NP. Hardness alone is preserved by the reduction; membership is not.

ALG-U7-Q25NATmedium

Consider six problems: (i) the halting problem, (ii) the decision version of the travelling-salesman problem, (iii) 2-SAT, (iv) SAT, (v) deciding whether a given DFA accepts every string, (vi) VERTEX COVER. How many of the six are NP-hard?

Show answer & solution

Answer: 4

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-hard covers the NP-complete problems plus everything above them, including undecidable problems; only problems known to be in P are excluded.

Step by step.

  1. (i) The halting problem is undecidable, and SAT reduces to it, so it is NP-hard.
  2. (ii) The decision form of TSP is NP-complete, hence NP-hard.
  3. (iii) 2-SAT is in P, so it is NP-hard only if P = NP; it is not counted.
  4. (iv) SAT is NP-complete, hence NP-hard.
  5. (v) DFA universality is decided in polynomial time by a product construction, so it is not counted.
  6. (vi) VERTEX COVER is NP-complete, hence NP-hard.
  7. The NP-hard problems are (i), (ii), (iv) and (vi), four in all.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Excluding the halting problem because it is undecidable, or including 2-SAT because it looks like SAT. Hardness is a lower bound and reaches beyond NP, while polynomial-time problems are below it.

ALG-U7-Q46MCQeasy

A language L is NP-hard exactly when:

  1. every language in NP reduces to L in polynomial time.
  2. L reduces to every language in NP in polynomial time.
  3. L ∈ NP and every language in NP reduces to L.
  4. L is not in P.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-hardness is a lower bound: L is at least as hard as everything in NP.

Step by step.

  1. The definition quantifies over the languages of NP: for every A ∈ NP, A ≤p L.
  2. Membership of L in NP is not required; adding it gives NP-completeness, which is option C.
  3. Option B reverses the arrow and describes a language that is no harder than NP.
  4. Option D is neither necessary nor sufficient: undecidable languages are NP-hard yet not in P, and the question P versus NP is open.

Answer: every language in NP reduces to L — option A.

Common misconception. Building NP membership into the definition, which turns NP-hard into NP-complete and wrongly excludes the halting problem and optimisation problems.

ALG-U7-Q47MCQmedium

Which of the following is NP-hard but not known to belong to NP?

  1. SAT
  2. VERTEX COVER (decision version)
  3. finding the chromatic number of a graph
  4. 2-SAT
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (C)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP contains decision problems only. A minimisation problem can be NP-hard and still lie outside NP, because it has no yes/no answer to certify.

Step by step.

  1. SAT and VERTEX COVER are decision problems in NP, and both are NP-complete, so they are not the answer.
  2. 2-SAT is in P and therefore not NP-hard unless P = NP.
  3. The chromatic number is a number to be computed, so 'find the chromatic number' is not a language with a yes/no answer and is not a member of NP.
  4. It is NP-hard, because the decision problem 'is the chromatic number at most 3?' is NP-complete and an algorithm for the number settles it.
  5. Hence it is NP-hard without being known to lie in NP.

Answer: finding the chromatic number — option C.

Common misconception. Assuming every NP-hard problem must be in NP. Optimisation problems are the standard counterexamples; the halting problem is the extreme one.

ALG-U7-Q48MSQhard

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every NP-complete language is NP-hard.
  2. Every NP-hard language belongs to NP.
  3. If P = NP then every NP-hard language lies in P.
  4. If an NP-hard language lies in P then P = NP.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Options (A) and (D)

Detailed solution

Concept. NP-hard is a lower bound that reaches beyond NP, so it is inherited by NP-complete languages and it collapses P and NP when it meets P.

Step by step.

  1. A: NP-completeness is defined as NP-hard together with membership in NP, so the hardness half holds by definition. TRUE.
  2. B: the halting problem is NP-hard, since SAT reduces to it, yet it is undecidable and so not in NP. FALSE.
  3. C: even with P = NP, an NP-hard language may be undecidable, so it need not lie in P. FALSE.
  4. D: if L ∈ P is NP-hard then every A ∈ NP satisfies A ≤p L, and easiness transfers backwards, so A ∈ P and NP ⊆ P. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A) and (D) are TRUE; (B) and (C) are FALSE.

Common misconception. Option C looks harmless but forgets that NP-hardness is not confined to decidable problems. A collapse of P and NP does not make the halting problem decidable.

ALG-U7-Q49NATeasy

How many of the following languages are NP-hard? (i) SAT (ii) the halting problem (iii) 2-SAT (iv) the decision version of VERTEX COVER (v) the set of primes written in binary

Show answer & solution

Answer: 3

Detailed solution

Concept. Hardness needs every language of NP to reduce to the problem; problems in P qualify only if P = NP, and undecidable problems can qualify.

Step by step.

  1. (i) SAT is NP-complete, hence NP-hard.
  2. (ii) The halting problem is NP-hard: SAT reduces to it, since a decider for halting would decide satisfiability by simulating a search.
  3. (iii) 2-SAT is in P, so it is NP-hard only if P = NP; it is not known to be.
  4. (iv) VERTEX COVER is NP-complete, hence NP-hard.
  5. (v) Primality is in P (the AKS algorithm), so it is not known to be NP-hard.
  6. The NP-hard members are (i), (ii) and (iv), three in all.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Excluding the halting problem because it is undecidable, or including 2-SAT and primality because they are about hard-looking search. Membership in P rules hardness out unless P = NP.

ALG-U7-Q50MCQmedium

If P ≠ NP, which of the following must hold?

  1. No NP-complete language lies in P.
  2. NP = co-NP
  3. Every NP-hard language is undecidable.
  4. 2-SAT is NP-complete.
Show answer & solution

Answer: Option (A)

Detailed solution

Concept. An NP-complete language in P would drag all of NP into P, so P ≠ NP forbids it.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose an NP-complete L lay in P. Every A ∈ NP reduces to L, and easiness transfers backwards, so A ∈ P and P = NP.
  2. Since we assume P ≠ NP, no NP-complete language lies in P.
  3. NP = co-NP is an independent open question and is not implied either way.
  4. NP-hard languages include decidable ones such as SAT, so they are not all undecidable.
  5. 2-SAT is in P, so it cannot be NP-complete when P ≠ NP.

Answer: no NP-complete language lies in P — option A.

Common misconception. Believing P ≠ NP also settles NP versus co-NP, or that hardness forces undecidability. The two questions are separate.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 2 (Divide and Conquer & Sorting), Set 3 — the second pass over this unit, opened after its 50-question allowance was filled on 2026-10-27 and 2026-10-28. All four subtopics are covered: merge sort & quick sort (7) — the two bounds on the number of comparisons a merge can make, the tie-break that makes a merge stable, Lomuto partitioning on sorted input, the split point Hoare's partition returns, the count of merge operations, and the one-sided recurrence of a bad pivot; counting & radix sort (6) — integer keys used as array indices, MSD against LSD radix sort, the expected linear time of bucket sort, the cost and space of the two linear sorts, the prefix-sum array, and radix sort against a single count array over the whole key range; selection & the k-th element (6) — quickselect's quadratic worst case, the median of two sorted arrays, a bounded heap, the comparison count of a minimum pivot, the geometric recurrence behind the expected bound, and quickselect against median-of-medians; and inversion counting (6) — counting out-of-order pairs, the block count made by one merge step, structural facts about the count, its parity, the merge-sort counter, and a reverse-sorted array with one pair repaired. Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • A merge is bounded by the interleaving, not by the total length. The fewest comparisons come from runs that do not interleave at all, the most from runs that interleave until one key is left, and both bounds are read off the two pointer walks.
  • Stability is a tie-break rule. A sort is stable because of one branch of one comparison, so the merge takes the left run on a tie, the digit sorts must be stable, and the partition does not care.
  • The pivot rule decides the shape. Balanced splits give Θ(n log n) and logarithmic depth; a pivot that is always an extreme key gives Θ(n²) and linear depth, on sorted input as readily as on any other.
  • Linear sorts spend the key range. Counting sort is Θ(n + k) in time and space, radix sort replaces one large k by d small ones, and bucket sort buys linear time with an assumption about the distribution.
  • Selection recurses into one part. That is why it is linear in expectation and still quadratic in the worst case, why the recurrence is geometric rather than branching, and why a guaranteed linear bound costs a larger constant.
  • Inversions are counted in blocks. One merge step adds the remaining length of the left run for each key taken from the right, an adjacent exchange moves the count by exactly 1, and a transposition moves it by an odd amount.

Source categories

Each question carries a data-source tag. STANDARD_TEXTBOOK marks questions built on the standard treatment of divide-and-conquer sorting, linear-time sorting and selection (CLRS, Cormen et al.; Kleinberg and Tardos); ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS marks questions written for this series around a situation those texts do not pose directly. A GATE_PYQ_VARIATION tag would mark a question modelled on a previous-year paper, and it is used only where the paper, year and official key are recorded in the Question Bank tab; no item on this paper carries that tag, because no algorithms previous-year question has been verified against an official paper and answer key. Nothing here is presented as an official GATE question.
